Source corrections: The official U.S. Reports identifies Justice Scalia, not Justice Thomas, as the author of the unanimous opinion in *Wright*, and records a vacatur and remand rather than a simple reversal. It records *NYNEX* as vacated and remanded, identifies Justice Scalia as joining Justice Ginsburg's opinion in *Buckley* while Justice O'Connor and Justice Breyer filed a mixed writing, and identifies *Minnesota v. Johns* as decided together with *Minnesota v. Carter*. These public-source corrections create no adjudicative blocker. `OT_1998CHUNK1.md` ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep525/usrep525070/usrep525070.pdf))

## Decisions and Dispositions

### Marquez v. Screen Actors Guild, Inc.

Argued October 5, 1998 | Decided November 3, 1998  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit; judgment below:  
summary judgment affirmed on the facial fair-representation and grace-period jurisdiction issues, with distinct notice and enforcement claims remanded, 124 F.3d 1034 (9th Cir. 1997). ([tile.loc.gov](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep525/usrep525033/usrep525033.pdf?utm_source=chatgpt.com), [tile.loc.gov](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep525/usrep525033/usrep525033.pdf?utm_source=chatgpt.com))  
Area of Law: Labor law; duty of fair representation; NLRA primary jurisdiction

Questions Presented:

1. Whether a union breaches its duty of fair representation by negotiating and enforcing a union-security clause that tracks NLRA § 8(a)(3) without separately stating the limits imposed by *NLRB v. General Motors* and *Communications Workers v. Beck*, and whether a federal court may decide the statutory issue embedded in that duty claim.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O'Connor | Stone, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence | Kennedy | Thomas | Supports affirmance | Complete concurrence |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: Stone announces no ground beyond the Court's opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Statutory union-security language

Holding and operative rule: A union does not breach its duty of fair representation merely by using § 8(a)(3)'s statutory union-security terms without restating every limitation supplied by later judicial decisions.

Reasoning: The duty of fair representation is breached by conduct that is arbitrary, discriminatory, or in bad faith. The clause used Congress's own language, which federal law gives a settled and limited meaning. Use of that legal shorthand is not wholly irrational, and the language alone does not establish an intent to deceive represented employees. *Beck* concerned the union's operative collection and use of compulsory fees, not a facial requirement that a collective-bargaining agreement reproduce the complete body of governing labor law. The Court therefore decides only the drafting challenge. It does not decide whether SAG gave misleading notice, demanded formal membership, collected nonchargeable fees, or enforced the clause unlawfully. Those distinct claims remain governed by the Ninth Circuit's remand. ([tile.loc.gov](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep525/usrep525033/usrep525033.pdf?utm_source=chatgpt.com), [tile.loc.gov](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep525/usrep525033/usrep525033.pdf?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

##### Question 2: Grace-period theory and NLRB jurisdiction

Holding and operative rule: A claim alleging only that a union-security arrangement violates the NLRA remains initially within the NLRB's jurisdiction unless the operative facts independently support arbitrary, discriminatory, or bad-faith representation. A federal court may resolve an NLRA issue when it arises collaterally within a properly supported duty-of-fair-representation claim.

Reasoning: The label attached to a complaint does not determine the adjudicatory forum. A genuine fair-representation action is cognizable in federal court, but a plaintiff may not obtain district-court adjudication of a stand-alone statutory administration question merely by invoking the duty of fair representation. Marquez's grace-period theory, as presented, alleged that the clause was inconsistent with § 8(a)(3) without identifying additional representational misconduct. That statutory issue therefore belongs to the Board in the first instance. This allocation does not prevent a federal court from deciding a collateral NLRA question when independent allegations of arbitrariness, discrimination, or bad faith make the statutory issue necessary to a judicially cognizable claim. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep525/usrep525033/usrep525033.pdf))

#### Concurrences

Kennedy, joined by Thomas: The facial holding does not protect a union that uses statutory language as part of actual deception, unlawful collection, or misleading enforcement. The clause's wording may remain relevant evidence in the separate claims already remanded for factual development.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Vaca v. Sipes* and *Air Line Pilots Assn. v. O'Neill*: followed; arbitrariness, discrimination, or bad faith remains necessary for a duty-of-fair-representation violation.

*Communications Workers v. Beck*: clarified; a statutory violation alone does not invariably establish a breach of the duty of fair representation.

*San Diego Building Trades Council v. Garmon*: applied; an NLRA-only grace-period claim remains initially for the NLRB.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit is affirmed, and its remand of the distinct notice and enforcement claims remains in force.

Effect: SAG incurs no liability merely from the facial language of the union-security clause. Marquez may continue litigating the separately remanded notice and enforcement allegations, while an NLRA-only grace-period challenge must proceed through the Board's ordinary processes.

### Pfaff v. Wells Electronics, Inc.

Argued October 6, 1998 | Decided November 10, 1998  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Federal Circuit; judgment below:  
the disputed patent claims held invalid under the pre-AIA on-sale bar, 124 F.3d 1429 (Fed. Cir. 1997). ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep525/usrep525055/usrep525055.pdf))  
Area of Law: Patent law; on-sale bar

Questions Presented:

1. When an invention is on sale under pre-AIA 35 U.S.C. § 102(b), particularly whether a completed commercial offer and enabling design can trigger the bar before a working prototype exists.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stevens | Stone, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: Stone announces no ground beyond the Court's opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Commercial offer and readiness for patenting

Holding and operative rule: An invention is on sale under pre-AIA § 102(b) when, before the critical date, it is the subject of a commercial offer for sale and is ready for patenting. Readiness may be established by reduction to practice or by drawings or descriptions sufficiently specific to enable a skilled artisan to practice the invention.

Reasoning: Section 102(b) addresses commercial placement of an invention, not completion of a particular engineering milestone. A working prototype is sufficient evidence that the invention is complete, but the statute does not make one indispensable. Before the critical date, Texas Instruments made a definite purchase commitment for more than 30,000 sockets, and Pfaff had supplied detailed drawings from which the devices could be manufactured without further inventive work. Those facts establish both a commercial offer and readiness for patenting. Requiring a prototype would permit an inventor to exploit a completed design commercially while postponing the statutory period, extending practical exclusivity beyond the term Congress authorized. Preliminary negotiations or a conception still requiring inventive development remain outside the rule. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep525/usrep525055/usrep525055.pdf))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

Pre-AIA 35 U.S.C. § 102(b): clarified; the on-sale bar requires a commercial offer and readiness for patenting, not a completed physical prototype.

*The Telephone Cases*: applied; enabling drawings may demonstrate that an invention is sufficiently complete for patenting.

*Pennock v. Dialogue* and *Elizabeth v. Pavement Co.*: followed; commercial exploitation is distinguished from experimental activity undertaken to perfect an invention.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Federal Circuit is affirmed.

Effect: The invalidity judgment remains in force because the commercial offer and enabling drawings preceded the critical date. Inventors may continue genuine experimentation without triggering the bar, but they may not postpone the statutory period after commercially offering an invention that is ready for patenting.

---

### Wright v. Universal Maritime Service Corp.

Argued October 7, 1998 | Decided November 16, 1998  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Fourth Circuit; judgment below:  
dismissal of Wright's ADA action for failure to pursue collective-bargaining arbitration affirmed, 121 F.3d 702 (4th Cir. 1997). ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep525/usrep525070/usrep525070.pdf))  
Area of Law: Arbitration; labor law; Americans with Disabilities Act

Questions Presented:

1. Whether a general collective-bargaining arbitration clause clearly and unmistakably requires an employee to arbitrate an Americans with Disabilities Act claim.

#### Judgment

Vacated and remanded, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Scalia | Stone, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports vacatur and remand | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to vacate and remand.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: Stone announces no ground beyond the Court's opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Waiver of the federal statutory forum

Holding and operative rule: A collective-bargaining agreement requires arbitration of an employee's federal antidiscrimination claim only when the agreement clearly and unmistakably identifies that statutory claim or an equivalent class of independent federal rights. The general grievance language in Wright's agreement does not satisfy that requirement.

Reasoning: The ordinary presumption favoring arbitration of collective-bargaining disputes is tied to the arbitrator's competence to interpret and apply the agreement. Wright's cause of action arises under the ADA and ultimately concerns the meaning of federal law, not merely the application of a contractual term. Such an independent statutory claim is not presumed to fall within general language covering matters under dispute or disputes concerning the agreement. Neither the collective-bargaining agreement nor the seniority plan identifies the ADA, federal discrimination statutes, or independent statutory claims. The Court therefore finds no waiver and does not decide whether a clear union-negotiated waiver would be enforceable in every setting. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep525/usrep525070/usrep525070.pdf))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Mastrobuono v. Shearson Lehman Hutton*: applied; arbitration extends only to matters the governing agreement actually submits.

*Alexander v. Gardner-Denver Co.*: followed; an independent federal employment right and forum are not surrendered through general collective-bargaining language.

*Metropolitan Edison Co. v. NLRB*: extended; the clear-and-unmistakable waiver requirement applies to a union-negotiated surrender of a federal judicial forum.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Fourth Circuit is vacated, and the case is remanded with instructions to return the matter to the district court for adjudication of Wright's ADA claim unless another independently lawful ground disposes of it.

Effect: Wright need not arbitrate the ADA claim under the general provisions before the Court. The decision neither establishes liability under the ADA nor determines the enforceability of a collective-bargaining agreement that expressly and unmistakably submits federal statutory claims to arbitration.

---

### Minnesota v. Carter

Argued October 6, 1998 | Decided December 1, 1998  
On writs of certiorari to the Supreme Court of Minnesota; judgments below:  
evidence suppressed and respondents' convictions set aside, 569 N.W.2d 169 and 180 (Minn. 1997). ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep525/usrep525083/usrep525083.pdf))  
Area of Law: Fourth Amendment; residential privacy; police observation

Questions Presented:

1. Whether invited visitors present in an apartment for a short commercial transaction possess a legitimate expectation of privacy against deliberate police observation through the apartment window.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 5-4.  
Supporting: O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Breyer. Opposing: Stone, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Plurality | O'Connor | Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence | Scalia | Thomas | Supports reversal and remand | Complete concurrence |
| Concurrence | Kennedy | No other Justice | Supports reversal and remand | Complete concurrence |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Breyer | No other Justice | Concurs in the judgment | Public-vantage observation ground |
| Dissent | Stone | Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg | Would affirm | Personal privacy and deliberate narrow-gap observation |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.  
Public role: dissent author.  
Announced ground: Host authorization, actual private use of a substantially screened residential interior, and the resident's exclusion of the public give the invited occupants a personal Fourth Amendment privacy interest despite the visit's commercial purpose and brevity. Police conduct a search when they deliberately exploit a narrow aperture in otherwise substantially closed blinds to investigate the screened interior, even while standing in a location lawfully accessible to the public.

#### Decision of the Court

Judgment announced by O'Connor. No opinion of the Court.

##### Question 1: Personal privacy of short-term invited occupants

Holding and operative rule: Short-term invited occupants admitted by the resident to use a substantially screened residential interior for private activity possess a personal Fourth Amendment privacy interest in that interior, even when the visit is commercial and brief. (joined by Stone, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer).

Reasoning: Five Justices agree that respondents may invoke the Fourth Amendment personally. Thompson admitted Carter and Johns into the apartment, remained with them, and allowed them to use the screened interior for a private collaborative activity lasting approximately two and one-half hours. Simulated *United States v. Fields* already rejects leasehold title as indispensable and recognizes privacy through a permitted relationship to a screened residential space. The illegality and commercial character of the concealed conduct do not themselves eliminate privacy, just as criminal activity does not otherwise withdraw Fourth Amendment protection. The controlling majority proposition is confined to host-authorized occupants actually using the screened interior privately. It does not protect persons lacking permission, delivery personnel who do not share the interior, or visitors in portions of a residence held open to general observation.

##### Question 2: Observation through the gap in the blinds

No controlling rationale under *Marks*; the judgment reverses the suppression ruling, but no five-Justice coalition agrees whether deliberate investigative use of the narrow gap from a lawful public location was a search.

Reasoning: Four Justices supporting reversal conclude that respondents lacked a personal privacy interest and therefore do not decide whether the observation was a search. Breyer supplies the necessary fifth vote for reversal on a different ground, concluding that the officer stood outside the curtilage in a place used by the public and observed what an ordinary passerby could see through the blinds. Stone, Stevens, Souter, and Ginsburg conclude that sustained investigative exploitation of a narrow gap in an otherwise substantially closed residential barrier was a search. Neither rationale is a logical subset of the other. The judgment therefore resolves suppression in this case without creating a controlling rule that either categorically validates or categorically prohibits deliberate observation through a narrow aperture from a lawful public location. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep525/usrep525083/usrep525083.pdf))

#### Concurrences

Scalia, joined by Thomas: The Fourth Amendment's text and property-based protections do not confer a personal right in another person's house merely because an individual is present with permission. Respondents lacked the connection necessary to invoke the householder's protection.

Kennedy: Almost all genuine social guests may share a host's privacy, but respondents established only a fleeting commercial connection to the apartment and no meaningful social relationship with its resident.

Necessary fifth vote: Breyer agrees that respondents possess a personal Fourth Amendment interest, but concludes that the officer's observation from a publicly used location outside the curtilage was constitutionally permissible. His public-vantage rationale supports reversal but does not command a majority.

#### Dissents

Stone, joined by Stevens, Souter, and Ginsburg: The resident's permission, respondents' actual private use, and the substantially closed blinds establish personal privacy. Deliberate police use of the narrow gap for sustained investigation of the screened home interior was a search and required lawful authorization.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*United States v. Fields*, decided February 9, 1998: extended; host authorization, actual private use, and substantial residential screening protect short-term occupants despite a commercial purpose, while the ordinary lawful-public-vantage boundary remains unchanged because no majority adopted a replacement rule.

*Minnesota v. Olson*: extended; overnight residence is sufficient but not necessary when the resident has admitted the claimant to share the privacy of a screened home interior.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgments of the Supreme Court of Minnesota are reversed, and the cases are remanded for proceedings consistent with the conclusion that the challenged observation supplies no basis for suppressing the evidence against respondents.

Effect: The suppression judgment may not stand in these prosecutions. Respondents possess a personal privacy interest in the apartment, but the Court establishes no controlling rationale governing deliberate observation through a narrow gap from a lawful public location.

---

### Knowles v. Iowa

Argued November 3, 1998 | Decided December 8, 1998  
On writ of certiorari to the Supreme Court of Iowa; judgment below:  
conviction affirmed under a state rule authorizing a full search incident to issuance of a traffic citation, 569 N.W.2d 601 (Iowa 1997). ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep525/usrep525113/usrep525113.pdf))  
Area of Law: Fourth Amendment; traffic stops; search incident to arrest

Questions Presented:

1. Whether an officer who issues a traffic citation rather than making a custodial arrest may conduct a full search of the driver and vehicle as an automatic search incident to arrest.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stone | Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.  
Public role: Court author.  
Announced ground: A citation does not create the custodial conditions or evidence-preservation needs that justify a full search incident to arrest. Particularized protective measures and other recognized search exceptions remain available on their own facts.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Full search following a citation

Holding and operative rule: Issuance of a traffic citation, without a custodial arrest, does not authorize a full search of the driver or vehicle as a search incident to arrest. A search requires consent, probable cause, particularized protective grounds, or another recognized exception.

Reasoning: The search-incident exception is tied to the safety and evidence-preservation concerns created by taking a person into custody. Knowles was not arrested, faced no custodial transport, and received a citation for speeding, an offense that supplied no destructible evidence in the passenger compartment. The officer identified no specific fact suggesting that Knowles was armed or dangerous. General roadside safety concerns permit measures proportionate to an identified risk, including orders to exit a vehicle and a *Terry* frisk when supported by reasonable suspicion. They do not justify an automatic evidentiary search of every cited motorist and vehicle. Iowa's rule therefore converted a purpose-bound exception into a general search authority untethered to arrest, danger, or evidence. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep525/usrep525113/usrep525113.pdf))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Chimel v. California*: followed; the search-incident exception remains tied to custodial safety and evidence preservation.

*United States v. Robinson* and *New York v. Belton*: declined to extend to a citation-only traffic stop.

*Terry v. Ohio*: reaffirmed; particularized protective measures remain available when reasonable suspicion of danger exists.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Supreme Court of Iowa is reversed, and the case is remanded with instructions to suppress the fruits of the citation-only search and conduct any further proceedings on the remaining admissible record.

Effect: Knowles's conviction cannot rest on the marijuana and pipe obtained through the automatic citation search. Officers remain free to conduct searches supported by consent, probable cause, reasonable suspicion of danger, or another independently applicable exception.

---

### Haddle v. Garrison

Argued November 10, 1998 | Decided December 14, 1998  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Eleventh Circuit; judgment below:  
dismissal of the § 1985(2) claim affirmed because at-will employment was not treated as constitutionally protected property, 132 F.3d 46 (11th Cir. 1998). ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep525/usrep525121/usrep525121.pdf))  
Area of Law: Civil rights; witness retaliation; 42 U.S.C. § 1985(2)

Questions Presented:

1. Whether termination of at-will employment is an injury to person or property actionable under § 1985(2) when allegedly inflicted to deter or retaliate against federal-court testimony.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stone | Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.  
Public role: Court author.  
Announced ground: Loss of at-will employment caused by a conspiracy to deter or retaliate against federal testimony is an injury to person or property under § 1985(2). The statute does not require a constitutional entitlement to continued employment.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Retaliatory interference with at-will employment

Holding and operative rule: Loss of at-will employment caused by a conspiracy to deter or retaliate against federal testimony constitutes injury to person or property within § 1985(2), even though the employee lacked a constitutional entitlement to continued employment.

Reasoning: The Eleventh Circuit imported constitutional property-entitlement doctrine into a statute that uses broader injury language to protect the federal judicial process. At-will status defines the employer's ordinary authority to end the employment relationship; it does not authorize third parties to procure termination for a purpose Congress expressly prohibited. Common-law principles have long recognized wrongful third-party interference with an at-will employment relationship as a concrete legal injury. Haddle alleged that the conspiracy caused his discharge, loss of wages, and loss of employment because he cooperated with a federal grand jury and was expected to testify at trial. Those allegations are sufficient at the pleading stage. He must still prove conspiracy, prohibited intent, causation, and damages. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep525/usrep525121/usrep525121.pdf))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

42 U.S.C. § 1985(2): clarified; injury to person or property includes concrete economic loss caused by retaliatory third-party interference with at-will employment.

Common-law protection against wrongful interference with employment: applied; the absence of guaranteed tenure does not eliminate the injured relationship.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Eleventh Circuit is reversed, and the case is remanded with instructions that the district court adjudicate the remaining conspiracy, intent, causation, and damages elements.

Effect: Haddle's complaint may proceed beyond dismissal. The decision creates no tenure right and does not convert every wrongful discharge into a federal civil-rights claim; liability remains confined to conduct satisfying § 1985(2).

---

### NYNEX Corp. v. Discon, Inc.

Argued October 5, 1998 | Decided December 14, 1998  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Second Circuit; judgment below:  
Discon's per se group-boycott theory and derivative conspiracy-to-monopolize theory permitted to proceed, 93 F.3d 1055 (2d Cir. 1996). ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep525/usrep525128/usrep525128.pdf))  
Area of Law: Antitrust; Sherman Act; group boycott

Questions Presented:

1. Whether a buyer's decision to purchase removal services from one supplier rather than another, allegedly as part of a fraud, is a per se unlawful group boycott under Sherman Act § 1.

#### Judgment

Vacated and remanded, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Breyer | Stone, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg | Supports vacatur and remand | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to vacate and remand.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: Stone announces no ground beyond the Court's opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Per se treatment of a vertical supplier substitution

Holding and operative rule: A single buyer's substitution of one supplier for another, without horizontal concerted exclusion, is not a per se group boycott under Sherman Act § 1. Fraud, favoritism, or an improper motive does not supply the missing horizontal structure.

Reasoning: Per se condemnation is reserved for categories whose structure and predictable effect on competition are sufficiently established to dispense with case-specific market analysis. The complaint alleges a vertical purchasing decision, not an agreement among competing buyers or suppliers to exclude a rival. *Klor's* involved a combination containing horizontal coordination among appliance suppliers; it did not condemn every refusal by one buyer to continue dealing with one seller. Discon alleges conduct that may be fraudulent, tortious, or harmful to consumers, but injury to one supplier does not by itself establish injury to the competitive process. Any adequately pleaded antitrust claim must therefore proceed under the elements and proof ordinarily governing competitive harm rather than a per se boycott rule. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep525/usrep525128/usrep525128.pdf))

##### Question 2: Derivative Section 2 ruling

Holding and operative rule: The Second Circuit's derivative § 2 conspiracy-to-monopolize ruling is vacated because it rested on the same purchasing practices and unestablished competitive harm. The Court does not finally decide whether an adequately pleaded § 2 theory may proceed on remand.

Reasoning: The court below treated the same supplier substitution as supporting both the per se § 1 theory and the conspiracy-to-monopolize theory. Once the per se premise is removed, the § 2 analysis must independently address harm to the competitive process and the other statutory elements. The Court therefore vacates rather than finally rejects that component. It also declines to decide the broader argument that Discon's complaint alleges no competitive injury anywhere, because that argument lies outside the limited question granted. Fraud, business tort, rule-of-reason, and monopolization theories remain governed by their own elements and preservation requirements.

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Klor's, Inc. v. Broadway-Hale Stores, Inc.*: distinguished; its per se rule depends on coordinated exclusion containing a horizontal component.

*Business Electronics Corp. v. Sharp Electronics Corp.*: followed; a vertical restraint is not placed in a boycott per se category without the established structural feature supporting categorical condemnation.

*FTC v. Indiana Federation of Dentists*: distinguished; coordinated withholding by horizontal participants differs from a single buyer's supplier choice.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Second Circuit is vacated, and the case is remanded with instructions to reject per se group-boycott treatment and reconsider the derivative § 2 ruling under the proper elements.

Effect: Discon may not proceed on the theory that the supplier substitution is automatically unlawful. Adequately preserved rule-of-reason, monopolization, fraud, and other claims remain available for adjudication without any finding that they succeed.

---

### El Al Israel Airlines, Ltd. v. Tsui Yuan Tseng

Argued November 10, 1998 | Decided January 12, 1999  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Second Circuit; judgment below:  
New York personal-injury claim allowed to proceed after the Warsaw Convention claim failed, 122 F.3d 99 (2d Cir. 1997). ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep525/usrep525155/usrep525155.pdf))  
Area of Law: Treaties; international aviation; Warsaw Convention

Questions Presented:

1. Whether a passenger may recover under local tort law for personal injury connected to international air carriage when the Warsaw Convention supplies no recovery because its conditions are not met.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 8-1.  
Supporting: Stone, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: Stevens.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Ginsburg | Stone, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Stevens | No other Justice | Would affirm | Complete dissent |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: Stone announces no ground beyond the Court's opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Exclusivity within Article 17's field

Holding and operative rule: When a passenger-injury occurrence falls within Article 17's temporal and substantive field, Article 24 permits an action, however founded, only subject to the Warsaw Convention's conditions and limits. Local law may not create recovery solely because the claimant fails one of those conditions.

Reasoning: Articles 17 and 24 operate together as a uniform liability system for passenger injury during international carriage. Article 17 identifies the field and the conditions under which carrier liability exists; Article 24 prevents a claimant from avoiding those conditions by relabeling the same injury under local tort law. Tseng's claim concerns an occurrence in the course of embarking on international carriage, but she alleges psychic and psychosomatic injury without the bodily injury then required by Article 17. That failure marks the treaty's liability boundary rather than an invitation to replace the Convention with state law. *Zicherman* permits local law to measure damages after Convention liability has been established; it does not authorize local law to create liability where Article 17 is unsatisfied. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep525/usrep525155/usrep525155.pdf))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

Stevens: Article 24 should not be read to extinguish a local-law claim when the Convention itself supplies no cause of action for the injury alleged. He would preserve state-law recovery outside the Convention's affirmative liability provisions.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Air France v. Saks*: followed; whether an occurrence lies within Article 17's field remains determined under the Convention.

*Eastern Airlines, Inc. v. Floyd*: applied; purely psychic injury without qualifying bodily injury does not satisfy Article 17 as then construed.

*Zicherman v. Korean Air Lines Co.*: distinguished; local law may measure an established Convention remedy but may not create liability when the Convention's conditions fail.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Second Circuit is reversed as to the personal-injury claim, and the case is remanded with instructions to reinstate dismissal of that claim.

Effect: Tseng may not recover under New York tort law for the passenger injury within Article 17's field. The separate baggage judgment, occurrences outside embarking, carriage, or disembarking, and questions arising under Article 25 remain untouched.

---

### Buckley v. American Constitutional Law Foundation, Inc.

Argued October 14, 1998 | Decided January 12, 1999  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Tenth Circuit; judgment below:  
the challenged registered-voter, identification-badge, and individual-reporting provisions invalidated, 120 F.3d 1092 (10th Cir. 1997). ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/525/182/case.pdf))  
Area of Law: First Amendment; elections; ballot initiatives

Questions Presented:

1. Whether Colorado may require initiative-petition circulators to be registered voters, wear identification badges naming themselves and their employer, and disclose identifying and compensation information.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 7-2.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg. Opposing: O'Connor, Breyer.

| Judgment component | Disposition and vote | Supporting | Opposing | Relief or remand |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Registered-voter qualification | Affirmed, 7-2 | Stone, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg | O'Connor, Breyer | The injunction against excluding otherwise qualified circulators solely because they are unregistered remains in force. |
| Personal-name badge | Affirmed, 9-0 | Stone, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | No Justice | The personal-name requirement may not be enforced during solicitation. |
| Individual public reporting | Affirmed, 7-2 | Stone, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg | O'Connor, Breyer | The challenged publication of each circulator's address and individual compensation remains enjoined. |

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Ginsburg | Stone, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Thomas | No other Justice | Concurs in the judgment | All three challenged provisions under strict scrutiny |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | O'Connor | Breyer | Concurs in part and dissents in part | Concurs in the personal-name badge judgment; dissents from the registered-voter and individual-reporting judgments |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm each judgment component.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: Stone announces no ground beyond the Court's opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Registered-voter qualification

Holding and operative rule: Colorado may not exclude otherwise qualified resident-adult petition circulators solely because they are unregistered voters when retained identification, affidavit, service, verification, and fraud-enforcement mechanisms address the State's accountability interests more directly.

Reasoning: Petition circulation combines ballot-access activity with direct political advocacy. The registration requirement substantially reduces the number of people available to carry an initiative's message and therefore produces the same type of speaker reduction condemned in *Meyer v. Grant*. Registration does not itself measure honesty, knowledge of election law, residence, or amenability to process. Colorado retains circulator affidavits, address information, signature verification, subpoena authority, and fraud penalties. Those tools address identification and enforcement without excluding adults who choose not to register. The holding does not decide the validity of a genuine residence requirement, the minimum-age provision, the circulation period, the affidavit requirement, or signature-verification rules. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/525/182/case.pdf))

##### Question 2: Personal-name badge during solicitation

Holding and operative rule: Colorado may not compel a petition circulator to display the circulator's personal name during the expressive encounter when post-circulation identification serves enforcement with materially less interference in political advocacy.

Reasoning: A personal-name badge places compelled attribution directly inside the face-to-face effort to persuade another person concerning political change. Simulated *McIntyre v. Ohio Elections Commission* protects anonymous political advocacy from broad identification mandates unless the State establishes an overriding interest and narrow fit. Colorado can identify and subpoena circulators through retained affidavits and records after circulation. It therefore has not shown why the natural-person advocate must disclose a personal name at the moment of persuasion. The holding does not invalidate a paid-or-volunteer notation, sponsor identification, or confidential post-circulation records used for enforcement. O'Connor and Breyer agree with this component of the judgment. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/525/182/case.pdf))

##### Question 3: Public reports naming individual circulators

Holding and operative rule: Sponsor identity and aggregate expenditure disclosure remain permissible, but the challenged publication of each paid circulator's address and individual compensation fails exacting scrutiny because the record does not establish a substantial incremental relation between those details and an additional governmental need.

Reasoning: Colorado has substantial interests in election integrity, identifying financial sponsorship, and informing voters about the forces supporting an initiative. Those interests are materially served by sponsor disclosure, aggregate spending reports, circulator affidavits, subpoenas, and fraud sanctions. The challenged reports go further by publicly identifying each circulator's address and individual earnings. The record does not show that publishing those details materially improves fraud detection or voter understanding beyond the information already available. The decision does not create categorical immunity from election disclosure and does not foreclose a narrower individual-reporting rule supported by evidence establishing the required relation. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/525/182/case.pdf))

#### Concurrences

Thomas: Each challenged provision directly or substantially burdens political speech and should be reviewed under strict scrutiny. He agrees that Colorado has not established the necessity and narrow tailoring required for any of the three provisions.

O'Connor, joined by Breyer: The personal-name badge directly burdens the live political exchange and fails demanding review. They agree that the paid-or-volunteer component is outside the question decided.

#### Dissents

O'Connor, joined by Breyer: The registered-voter rule primarily regulates the mechanics of qualifying an initiative and is a reasonable election-administration measure. The reporting requirements substantially advance fraud prevention and voter information and should remain valid under the applicable election and disclosure principles.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Meyer v. Grant*: applied; a registration rule that substantially reduces available petition speakers materially burdens core political circulation.

*McIntyre v. Ohio Elections Commission*: extended; exacting review applies to compelled personal-name attribution during live petition advocacy.

*Buckley v. Valeo*: applied; electoral disclosure must bear a substantial relation to a substantial governmental interest, and sponsor or aggregate disclosure does not automatically justify publication of every individual's address and earnings.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Tenth Circuit is affirmed as to the registered-voter qualification, personal-name badge, and challenged individual-reporting provisions.

Effect: Colorado may not enforce those three restrictions in the forms adjudicated here. It may continue to enforce the minimum-age rule, circulation period, affidavits, fraud and signature-verification provisions, paid-or-volunteer notation, sponsor attribution, aggregate expenditure disclosure, and any narrower reporting requirement that satisfies the governing scrutiny.

---

### City of West Covina v. Perkins

Argued November 3, 1998 | Decided January 13, 1999  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit; judgment below:  
summary judgment for the City reversed because the seizure notice did not describe California's return-of-property procedures, 113 F.3d 1004 (9th Cir. 1997). ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep525/usrep525234/usrep525234.pdf))  
Area of Law: Procedural due process; property seizure; notice

Questions Presented:

1. Whether due process requires police who lawfully seize property under a warrant to give the owner individualized notice of state-law procedures for seeking return of the property.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Kennedy | Stone, Stevens, O'Connor, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Thomas | Scalia | Concurs in the judgment | Rejects the individualized-instruction claim while reserving the Due Process Clause as the source of a seizure-notice obligation |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: Stone announces no ground beyond the Court's opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Content of post-seizure notice

Holding and operative rule: When the government gives timely notice identifying the seized property and the responsible custodian, due process does not additionally require personalized instructions describing a published, generally available state-law procedure for obtaining return of the property.

Reasoning: Individualized notice of the seizure is necessary because the owner otherwise may have no reasonable way to know who took the property or what governmental act triggered the need to seek relief. The same rationale does not require police to provide individualized legal instruction about remedies established in accessible statutes and case law. Perkins received notice of the search, the responsible agency, the issuing court and judge, contact information, and an inventory of the property taken. The record did not establish that the omitted warrant number was necessary to invoke California's procedure. *Memphis Light* involved an unpublished and practically inaccessible internal procedure, not a remedy contained in generally available law. The Court decides only the categorical instruction requirement and leaves open any preserved claim that a nominal remedy was concealed, obstructed, misleading, or practically inaccessible. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep525/usrep525234/usrep525234.pdf))

#### Concurrences

Thomas, joined by Scalia: The Due Process Clause does not require detailed notice of state-law remedies. They would reserve whether procedural due process independently requires notice that property was seized during execution of a criminal search warrant, a matter they regard as more naturally governed by the Fourth Amendment.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*United States v. James Daniel Good Real Property*: distinguished; its pre-seizure hearing rule for civil forfeiture of real property does not create a post-seizure legal-instruction requirement for personal property taken under a criminal warrant.

*Mullane v. Central Hanover Bank & Trust Co.*: followed; notice must identify the governmental deprivation sufficiently to permit the affected person to respond.

*Memphis Light, Gas & Water Division v. Craft*: distinguished; procedural instructions may be required when the available route is unpublished or inaccessible, but not merely because the governing law is not personally explained.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit is reversed, and the case is remanded with instructions to reject the claim that due process invariably requires personalized legal instructions describing California's published return procedures.

Effect: The City is not constitutionally required to provide each property owner with a tailored guide to state motions and writs. Any preserved challenge to the actual adequacy, accessibility, obstruction, or operation of California's procedures, unreasonable retention, or the lawfulness of the original seizure remains open on remand.

---

The census cases arose from separate judgments in the District of Columbia and Eastern District of Virginia.
