These corrections create no adjudicative blocker. `OT_1998CHUNK2.md` ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep525/usrep525255/usrep525255.pdf))

## Decisions and Dispositions

### Department of the Army v. Blue Fox, Inc.

Argued December 1, 1998 | Decided January 20, 1999  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit; judgment below: equitable-lien claim against Army funds allowed to proceed, 121 F.3d 1357 (9th Cir. 1997). ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep525/usrep525255/usrep525255.pdf))  
Area of Law: Administrative law; federal sovereign immunity; APA waiver

Questions Presented:

1. Whether APA § 702 waives federal sovereign immunity for a subcontractor’s action seeking an equitable lien on money owed by the Army to a prime contractor.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stone | Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.  
Public role: Court author.  
Announced ground: APA § 702 does not waive sovereign immunity for an equitable lien whose object is substitute payment from general federal property when the claimant identifies no specific fund that the Government is legally obligated to restore.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: APA waiver for an equitable lien against federal funds

Holding and operative rule: Section 702 does not waive federal sovereign immunity for an equitable lien when no identifiable retained fund remains and the claimant instead seeks substitute compensation from general Government property for a private contractor’s default.

Reasoning: Section 702 waives immunity for relief other than money damages, but the characterization of a remedy as equitable does not determine whether it falls within that language. A remedy is specific when it seeks the return of particular property or enforcement of a defined payment obligation already owed to the claimant. Blue Fox identifies neither. The Army owed it no direct contractual payment, and the record establishes no segregated or traceable fund belonging to Blue Fox. The proposed lien would attach Government property to compensate the subcontractor for Verdan’s failure to pay. That is substitute monetary relief. Simulated *Lane v. Peña*, 518 U.S. 187, requires an unequivocal waiver for the particular Treasury remedy sought, and § 702 contains no language authorizing creditors to attach federal property. Congress addressed subcontractor protection through the Miller Act’s bond remedy, but that enactment does not create a direct claim against the Government when a required bond was omitted. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep525/usrep525255/usrep525255.pdf))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Lane v. Peña*: applied; sovereign immunity must be unequivocally waived for the particular monetary or Treasury remedy sought.

*Bowen v. Massachusetts*: distinguished; enforcement of a specific statutory payment obligation does not authorize substitute compensation from general federal assets.

*Buchanan v. Alexander* and *United States v. Ansonia Brass & Copper Co.*: followed; creditors may not attach Government funds or property without an express waiver.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit is reversed, and the case is remanded with instructions to dismiss the equitable-lien claim against the Army.

Effect: Blue Fox may not obtain payment by imposing a lien on federal property. The judgment does not foreclose recovery against a responsible Miller Act surety, a properly founded Tucker Act claim, relief involving an identifiable fund the Government is legally required to restore, or another remedy supported by an unequivocal waiver.

---

### Lopez v. Monterey County

Argued November 2, 1998 | Decided January 20, 1999  
On appeal from the United States District Court for the Northern District of California, convened as a three-judge court; judgment below: elections permitted under arrangements reflecting unprecleared state-law changes while preclearance remained absent, three-judge District Court judgment on remand. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep525/usrep525266/usrep525266.pdf))  
Area of Law: Voting Rights Act § 5; preclearance; interim election remedies

Questions Presented:

1. Whether Monterey County may implement state-mandated voting changes without preclearance and whether a federal court may authorize elections under an unprecleared governmental arrangement while preclearance remains absent.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 8-1.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Stone, Stevens, Scalia, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Kennedy | No other Justice | Concurs in the judgment | County administration of the changes and nonuse of the unprecleared governmental plan |
| Dissent | Thomas | No other Justice | Would affirm | Complete dissent |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: Stone announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Administration of state-mandated voting changes

Holding and operative rule: A covered political subdivision must obtain § 5 preclearance before its officials administer a postbaseline voting change within the subdivision, even when a noncovered State selected the policy and left the local government no discretion. (joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer).

Reasoning: Simulated *Lopez v. Monterey County*, 519 U.S. 9, decided November 6, 1996, resolves the coverage question. Section 5 focuses on whether a covered jurisdiction seeks to administer a voting change, not on whether that jurisdiction originated the policy. Monterey County officials give the California enactments operative effect by conducting the affected elections and administering the changed judicial structure. A state-command exception would permit a noncovered State to accomplish through mandatory local administration what the covered subdivision could not lawfully do on its own. The rule does not place California as a whole under § 5 and does not decide whether the changes have a discriminatory purpose or retrogressive effect. Those substantive preclearance questions remain for the statutory forums designated by Congress.

##### Question 2: Use of an unprecleared plan through a judicial order

Holding and operative rule: A district court may not preserve or authorize an unprecleared governmental voting arrangement merely by placing it within a judicial order. Pending preclearance, the lawful alternatives are the precleared baseline or a genuinely independent, necessary, and temporary plan formulated by the court.

Reasoning: Section 5 requires prospective nonuse of an unprecleared governmental change. That command would lose force if a court could allow the same arrangement to operate while the jurisdiction pursued preclearance and describe the resulting election as judicially authorized. A true interim judicial plan is different because it arises from the court’s obligation to ensure that a lawful election can occur when the existing legal arrangements are unavailable. Such a plan must be independently formulated, necessary to meet an immediate electoral need, and no broader than that need. A court may not adopt the County’s unprecleared arrangement as its own or treat ongoing preclearance efforts as sufficient authorization for continued governmental use.

#### Concurrences

Kennedy: The County’s implementation gives operative effect to the challenged changes, and the district court may not preserve those changes through an interim order while preclearance remains absent. He would not rest the judgment on a categorical proposition that local discretion is always irrelevant to every possible § 5 administration question.

#### Dissents

Thomas: Section 5 should not apply when a covered county merely follows a binding enactment of a noncovered State and lacks authority to alter the policy. He would affirm the district court’s authority to permit the elections to proceed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Lopez v. Monterey County*, decided November 6, 1996: followed; state authorship and absence of local discretion do not remove a covered subdivision’s administration from § 5.

*Young v. Fordice*: applied; a state-mandated voting change remains subject to the preclearance sequence when administered by a covered jurisdiction.

*Connor v. Johnson*: distinguished; a genuinely independent judicial interim plan differs from a governmental plan merely continued or approved by a court.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The district-court judgment is reversed, and the case is remanded with instructions to prevent prospective use of the unprecleared governmental changes unless and until preclearance is obtained, while preserving authority to formulate a genuinely independent and necessary interim judicial plan.

Effect: Monterey County may use the lawful precleared baseline, obtain preclearance through a prescribed route, or operate under a confined plan actually formulated by the court because electoral necessity requires one. The decision does not predetermine the content of any interim plan or the ultimate entitlement to preclearance.

---

### Humana Inc. v. Forsyth

Argued November 30, 1998 | Decided January 20, 1999  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit; judgment below: federal RICO action permitted to proceed, 114 F.3d 1467 (9th Cir. 1997). ([Legal Information Institute](https://www.law.cornell.edu/supct/html/97-303.ZS.html))  
Area of Law: Insurance regulation; McCarran-Ferguson Act; civil RICO

Questions Presented:

1. Whether the McCarran-Ferguson Act bars a federal RICO action alleging insurer fraud when Nevada insurance law also regulates the conduct and supplies remedies.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Ginsburg | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: Stone announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: McCarran-Ferguson reverse preemption

Holding and operative rule: A federal RICO claim does not invalidate, impair, or supersede state insurance law when it targets conduct the State also forbids, leaves the state regulatory scheme fully operative, and adds compatible remedies rather than frustrating an exclusive state mechanism.

Reasoning: McCarran-Ferguson does not reserve the entire field of insurance regulation to the States. A generally applicable federal law is barred when its operation would render state insurance law ineffective, replace it, frustrate a declared state policy, or interfere with the State’s administrative regime. Nevada prohibits the alleged deception, recognizes private remedies, permits compensatory and punitive relief, and has not made its administrative process exclusive. RICO therefore reinforces rather than obstructs Nevada’s substantive policy against insurance fraud. The availability of treble damages does not itself establish impairment. Greater or additional federal liability may coexist with state remedies when regulated actors can comply with both systems and the federal remedy does not defeat a state-approved practice or displace an exclusive remedial compromise. The policyholders must still prove every RICO element, injury, causation, and entitlement to relief. ([Legal Information Institute](https://www.law.cornell.edu/supct/html/97-303.ZS.html))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Department of the Treasury v. Fabe*: clarified; McCarran-Ferguson protects state insurance regulation from federal interference, not from every compatible federal remedy.

McCarran-Ferguson Act § 2(b): clarified; impairment requires injurious interference with the operation or policy of state insurance law, not merely additional liability.

RICO: applied; federal remedies may complement parallel state prohibitions when the state system remains fully operative.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit is affirmed, and the case shall proceed under the ordinary requirements governing the pleaded RICO claims.

Effect: The policyholders may pursue RICO relief for the alleged insurance fraud. The Court makes no finding that fraud occurred and leaves Humana free to contest the statutory predicates, injury, causation, damages, and all other preserved defenses.

---

### Department of Commerce v. United States House of Representatives; Clinton v. Glavin

Argued November 30, 1998 | Decided January 25, 1999  
On direct appeals from the United States District Court for the District of Columbia and the United States District Court for the Eastern District of Virginia, each convened as a three-judge court; judgments below: permanent injunctions entered against the proposed use of sampling for congressional apportionment, 11 F. Supp. 2d 76 (D.D.C. 1998) and 19 F. Supp. 2d 543 (E.D. Va. 1998). ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep525/usrep525316/usrep525316.pdf))  
Area of Law: Article III standing; Census Act; Enumeration Clause

Questions Presented:

1. Whether the Census Act permits statistical sampling to determine the population used for congressional apportionment and, if it does, whether that method satisfies the Enumeration Clause.

#### Judgment

Reversed in part, vacated in part, and remanded; votes vary by docket.

| Judgment component | Disposition and vote | Supporting | Opposing | Relief or remand |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| No. 98-564, Clinton v. Glavin | Reversed and remanded, 5-4 | Stone, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | The Eastern District of Virginia injunction is vacated, and judgment shall be entered for the federal appellants on the statutory and constitutional claims decided here. |
| No. 98-404, Department of Commerce v. United States House of Representatives | Vacated and remanded, 6-3 | O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg | Stone, Stevens, Breyer | The District of Columbia judgment is vacated, and the House’s action shall be dismissed for lack of Article III standing. |

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stone | Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand in No. 98-564 | Glavin standing, plan-specific Census Act holding, Enumeration Clause holding |
| Plurality | Stone | Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg | Supports reversal and remand in No. 98-564 | Broader construction that § 195’s apportionment exception removes a mandate but imposes no prohibition on sampling authorized by § 141(a) |
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg | Supports vacatur and remand in No. 98-404 | House standing and dismissal |
| Concurrence | Breyer | No other Justice | Supports reversal and remand in No. 98-564 | Challenged sampling procedures as lawful supplements rather than substitutes for the enumeration |
| Dissent | Stone | Stevens, Breyer | Would reverse in No. 98-404 | Institutional standing of the House |
| Dissent | O’Connor | Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | Would affirm in No. 98-564 | Census Act prohibition and constitutional avoidance |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: in No. 98-564, to reverse and remand; in No. 98-404, to reverse and remand rather than vacate for lack of standing.  
Public role: Court author on the standing of the *Glavin* plaintiffs, the controlling Census Act proposition, and the Enumeration Clause; plurality author on the broader statutory construction; dissent author on the House-standing component.  
Announced ground: Sections 141(a) and 195 authorize the proposed sampling plan, and the plan bears a reasonable relationship to an actual enumeration. Stone would also hold that the House, suing as the authorized institution whose composition the census determines, has Article III standing.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Standing of the Glavin plaintiffs

Holding and operative rule: Voters who establish a substantial likelihood that the announced census methodology will cause loss or dilution of representation have Article III standing when that injury is traceable to the methodology and redressable by judgment. (joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer).

Reasoning: The *Glavin* record identifies individual voters whose States or local districts face concrete representational consequences from the announced methodology. The injuries are not abstract objections to executive compliance with law. They concern the allocation and weight of representation under population totals the federal census will supply. The causal connection is direct because the challenged methodology determines those totals, and an injunction or declaratory judgment would alter the method used. Ordinary injury, causation, and redressability principles therefore permit adjudication of the statutory and constitutional claims in No. 98-564.

##### Question 2: Standing of the House of Representatives

Holding and operative rule: The House of Representatives lacks Article III standing to challenge the census methodology merely because the resulting population totals will determine the allocation of seats among the States. (joined by O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, and Ginsburg).

Reasoning: The House alleges an institutional interest in lawful composition, but it identifies no loss of a seat belonging to the institution, exclusion of a duly selected member, nullification of a House vote, or impairment of a legal power assigned to the chamber. The House will contain the constitutionally prescribed number of members under either methodology. The dispute concerns which States and voters will receive those seats, an injury appropriately asserted by affected voters or States. Treating the House’s generalized interest in lawful apportionment as a judicial injury would permit legislative institutions to litigate broad disputes over executive implementation whenever future governmental composition or policy might be affected. The District of Columbia judgment must therefore be vacated, and that action dismissed.

##### Question 3: Statutory authorization for the challenged sampling plan

Holding and operative rule: Sections 141(a) and 195 authorize the two challenged sampling procedures because they operate as supplementary methods within the decennial enumeration; § 195 does not categorically prohibit their use merely because the resulting population totals are used for apportionment.

Reasoning: Section 141(a) expressly permits the Secretary to determine the form and content of the decennial census, including the use of sampling procedures and special surveys. Section 195 makes sampling mandatory when feasible for specified nonapportionment functions, but excepts apportionment from that command. Five Justices agree that the exception does not forbid the challenged procedures. The Bureau will continue to seek information from the entire population, conduct extensive direct enumeration, and use the disputed methods to address nonresponse and measure coverage. Four Justices, Stone, Stevens, Souter, and Ginsburg, would hold more broadly that § 195’s exception only removes a mandate and never limits § 141(a)’s express authority. Breyer does not join that categorical formulation, but concludes that both challenged methods are permissible supplements rather than substitutes for the enumeration. That narrower, plan-specific proposition controls.

##### Question 4: Enumeration Clause

Holding and operative rule: The Enumeration Clause permits authorized statistical sampling as part of the decennial census when the methodology remains directed to determining actual population and bears a reasonable relationship to achieving a complete and accurate apportionment count.

Reasoning: The Constitution requires an actual enumeration but commits the manner of conducting it to Congress. Simulated *Wisconsin v. City of New York*, 517 U.S. 1, establishes that an authorized census method is constitutional when it reasonably serves the enumeration and apportionment function. The challenged plan begins with a nationwide effort to enumerate the population and uses statistical procedures to estimate nonresponse and correct persistent differential undercount. It does not replace population measurement with political allocation or unsupported conjecture. The record supports the Secretary’s conclusion that the methods are directed toward improving completeness and accuracy. The Constitution does not freeze the census to person-by-person techniques or prohibit estimation whenever direct contact fails. The decision neither requires sampling in future censuses nor immunizes a method disconnected from genuine population measurement. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep525/usrep525316/usrep525316.pdf))

#### Concurrences

Breyer: The challenged procedures remain lawful supplements to a comprehensive enumeration and do not become prohibited substitutes merely because estimates contribute to the final totals. He does not join the plurality’s broader conclusion that § 195 can never constrain any form of apportionment sampling.

#### Dissents

Stone, joined by Stevens and Breyer, as to No. 98-404: The House has a concrete institutional interest in preventing an allegedly unlawful process from determining its constitutionally prescribed composition. They would sustain the House’s standing and reverse its judgment on the merits.

O’Connor, joined by Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas, as to No. 98-564: Section 195 preserves the historical prohibition against using sampling to determine the apportionment population. Because the statute resolves the case, they would not reach the Enumeration Clause.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Steel Co. v. Citizens for a Better Environment*: followed; Article III standing is resolved before statutory and constitutional merits.

*Powell v. McCormack*: declined to extend; a general institutional interest in lawful composition does not itself give the House standing to challenge census methodology.

*Wisconsin v. City of New York*: extended; an authorized statistical method reasonably directed to a complete and accurate population count may satisfy the Enumeration Clause.

13 U.S.C. §§ 141(a) and 195: clarified; the challenged supplementary sampling procedures are authorized, while no controlling majority adopts a categorical rule governing every possible form of apportionment sampling.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: In No. 98-564, the Eastern District of Virginia judgment is reversed and the case is remanded with instructions to vacate the permanent injunction and enter judgment for the federal appellants; in No. 98-404, the District of Columbia judgment is vacated and the case is remanded with instructions to dismiss the House’s action for lack of Article III standing.

Effect: The two permanent injunctions no longer bar the proposed sampling plan. The Department may implement the challenged procedures for the 2000 census, but the decision does not require their use or foreclose review of a materially different methodology.

---

### AT&T Corp. v. Iowa Utilities Board

Argued October 13, 1998 | Decided January 25, 1999  
On writs of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Eighth Circuit; judgments below: major FCC local-competition rules vacated or sustained in a mixed disposition, 120 F.3d 753 and 124 F.3d 934 (8th Cir. 1997).  
Area of Law: Administrative law; telecommunications; agency authority  
Participation: Justice O’Connor took no part; the basis was not publicly stated. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep525/usrep525366/usrep525366.pdf))

Questions Presented:

1. Whether the FCC had authority to issue nationwide rules implementing Telecommunications Act §§ 251 and 252, and whether its pricing, unbundling, and incumbent-network rules conformed to the statute.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded; votes vary by component.  
Took no part: O’Connor.

| Judgment component | Disposition and vote | Supporting | Opposing | Relief or remand |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| FCC authority to implement §§ 251 and 252 | Reversed, 6-2 | Stone, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg | Thomas, Breyer | The Eighth Circuit’s holding that state commissions possessed exclusive implementation authority is reversed. |
| Rule 319, necessary and impair standards | Reversed and remanded, 7-1 | Stone, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Souter | Rule 319 is vacated and returned to the FCC for element-specific application of the statutory limits. |
| Rule 315(b), separation of combined elements | Reversed, 8-0 | Stone, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | No Justice | Rule 315(b) remains in force. |
| Section 252(i) pick-and-choose rule | Reversed, 8-0 | Stone, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | No Justice | The § 252(i) rule remains in force. |

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Scalia | Stone, Stevens, Kennedy, Ginsburg in full; Souter as to FCC authority, Rule 315(b), and § 252(i); Thomas and Breyer as to Rule 319, Rule 315(b), and § 252(i) | Supports the component dispositions stated above | Scope stated by issue |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Souter | No other Justice | Concurs in part and dissents in part | Concurs on FCC authority, Rule 315(b), and § 252(i); dissents on Rule 319 |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Thomas | Breyer | Concurs in part and dissents in part | Concurs on the three rule-specific judgments; dissents on general FCC implementation authority |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Breyer | No other Justice | Concurs in part and dissents in part | Additional federal-state implementation analysis and Rule 319 analysis |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse on FCC implementation authority, Rule 315(b), and the § 252(i) rule; to reverse the judgment sustaining Rule 319, vacate that rule, and remand to the Commission.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: Stone announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: FCC implementation authority

Holding and operative rule: The FCC has authority to issue national rules implementing the federal duties imposed by §§ 251 and 252, while state commissions retain the agreement, arbitration, approval, and rate functions Congress specifically assigned to them. (joined by Stone, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, and Ginsburg).

Reasoning: The 1996 Act establishes federal local-competition duties and places them within an existing communications statute that authorizes FCC implementation. Sections 251 and 252 also assign important functions to state commissions, but those assignments do not make every federal implementation question exclusively state controlled. The provisions operate together: the Commission establishes nationally applicable rules governing the federal duties, while state commissions perform the adjudicative and implementation tasks Congress named. Recognizing federal rulemaking authority therefore does not displace state arbitration or approval authority. The contrary construction would leave national obligations without a national implementing authority and would treat specific state assignments as an implied repeal of the FCC’s general statutory power. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep525/usrep525366/usrep525366.pdf))

##### Question 2: Rule 319 and the necessary and impair limits

Holding and operative rule: Rule 319 is invalid because it fails to give independent constraining effect to § 251(d)(2)’s necessary and impair requirements and does not make the element-specific judgments the statute requires.

Reasoning: The Commission treated a decrease in quality or virtually any increase in cost as sufficient to require unbundled access, while excluding meaningful consideration of elements available outside the incumbent’s network. That approach converts necessary and impair into a general preference for whatever network element would make entry easier or more profitable. Simulated *MCI Telecommunications Corp. v. AT&T*, 512 U.S. 218, requires each enacted limitation to retain operative meaning. The FCC may consider the Act’s competitive objectives, but it must determine whether access to a particular element is genuinely necessary and whether denial would materially impair the entrant’s ability to provide the service. The Court vacates the rule rather than selecting a judicial replacement list. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep525/usrep525366/usrep525366.pdf))

##### Question 3: Separation of already combined elements

Holding and operative rule: Section 251(c)(3) does not require an incumbent to separate network elements it already maintains in combined form before leasing them to a requesting carrier; Rule 315(b) is a reasonable implementation of the statute.

Reasoning: The statutory phrase on an unbundled basis concerns separate access and pricing, not compulsory physical disassembly. The statute requires incumbents to provide elements in a manner that allows requesting carriers to combine them, but it does not direct incumbents to dismantle existing combinations before access is provided. Rule 315(b) prevents purposeless disconnection and reconnection costs without requiring an incumbent to create a combination it does not already maintain. The Commission therefore remained within the ambiguity Congress left concerning the physical form in which existing elements must be supplied. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep525/usrep525366/usrep525366.pdf))

##### Question 4: Section 252(i) pick-and-choose rule

Holding and operative rule: Section 252(i) permits a requesting carrier to adopt individual qualifying interconnection, service, or network-element terms from an approved agreement on the same conditions, subject to statutory cost, feasibility, and comparability distinctions.

Reasoning: Section 252(i) directs a local exchange carrier to make available any interconnection, service, or network element provided under an approved agreement to another requesting carrier on the same terms and conditions. The FCC’s rule closely tracks that text. Its allowance for adoption of individual qualifying terms does not rewrite negotiated agreements because the statute itself speaks in terms of individual services and elements. Incumbents remain able to establish materially greater cost, technical infeasibility, or another statutory distinction showing that the requesting carrier is not similarly situated. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep525/usrep525366/usrep525366.pdf))

#### Concurrences

Souter: He agrees that the FCC possesses implementation authority and that Rules 315(b) and 252(i) are valid. He would sustain Rule 319 because necessary and impair can reasonably encompass material competitive disadvantages even when some alternative means of entry exists.

Thomas, joined by Breyer: They agree with the rule-specific dispositions but would hold that the Act preserves primary implementation authority in state commissions and does not confer the asserted general national rulemaking power on the FCC.

Breyer: The Act requires a cooperative federal-state structure rather than exclusive control by either level of government. He agrees that Rule 319 fails to apply meaningful statutory limits but emphasizes the States’ continuing authority within the functions Congress assigned to them.

#### Dissents

Souter: Rule 319 gives reasonable content to necessary and impair in light of the Act’s objective of opening local markets and should remain in force.

Thomas, joined by Breyer: The Act’s detailed assignments to state commissions and the historical division of communications authority foreclose the FCC’s claimed general power over the state implementation process.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*MCI Telecommunications Corp. v. AT&T*: applied; agency implementation may not erase operative statutory predicates.

*Chevron U.S.A. Inc. v. Natural Resources Defense Council, Inc.*: applied; reasonable agency resolution of genuine statutory ambiguity remains permissible within the enacted delegation.

*Louisiana Public Service Commission v. FCC*: distinguished; the 1996 Act expressly created federal local-competition duties and a combined federal-state implementation structure.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The Eighth Circuit judgments are reversed to the extent they deny FCC implementation authority or invalidate Rules 315(b) and 252(i), reversed to the extent they sustain Rule 319, and remanded with instructions to return Rule 319 to the Commission for reconsideration under the statutory necessary and impair criteria.

Effect: The FCC retains national implementation authority, and Rules 315(b) and 252(i) remain operative. Rule 319 may not be enforced in its present form, but the Commission may adopt a different element-specific unbundling rule that gives independent effect to the statutory limitations.

---

### Hughes Aircraft Co. v. Jacobson

Argued November 2, 1998 | Decided January 25, 1999  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit; judgments below: retirees’ surplus, fiduciary, and termination theories permitted to proceed, 105 F.3d 1288 and 128 F.3d 1305 (9th Cir. 1997). ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep525/usrep525432/usrep525432.pdf))  
Area of Law: ERISA; defined-benefit plans; fiduciary status

Questions Presented:

1. Whether Hughes violated ERISA by amending an overfunded defined-benefit plan to create new benefit categories and use surplus assets, and whether the employer acted as a fiduciary when adopting the amendments.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Thomas | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: Stone announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Fiduciary status when amending the plan

Holding and operative rule: An employer acts as a plan settlor, not an ERISA fiduciary, when it adopts or amends the terms of a pension plan.

Reasoning: ERISA fiduciary status is function specific. Administration of existing plan terms, control of plan assets, investment decisions, and communications made in a fiduciary capacity remain subject to statutory duties. Designing or changing the plan itself is different. Simulated *Lockheed Corp. v. Spink*, 517 U.S. 882, directly classifies plan amendment as settlor conduct. Hughes therefore did not act as a fiduciary merely by creating an early-retirement program or a noncontributory benefit structure. The contributory character of the existing plan does not alter the legal capacity in which the sponsor amended it. A separately pleaded misrepresentation, discriminatory administration, or asset-diversion claim would remain governed by its own facts and elements. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep525/usrep525432/usrep525432.pdf))

##### Question 2: Participant interest in surplus and accrued benefits

Holding and operative rule: Participants in a defined-benefit plan possess enforceable rights to their accrued benefits, not individual ownership of the plan’s actuarial surplus; an amendment that leaves accrued benefits intact does not violate the anti-cutback rule.

Reasoning: A defined-benefit participant is promised a specified benefit from a common pool. The employer ordinarily bears the risk of underfunding and may reduce or suspend contributions when the plan is overfunded. That structure does not give each participant a divisible ownership share in excess assets. The amendments did not reduce any retiree’s fixed accrued benefit and used plan assets to pay benefits to participants. Simulated *John Hancock Mutual Life Insurance Co. v. Harris Trust & Savings Bank* identifies which property constitutes plan assets and when control over those assets creates fiduciary status; it does not convert actuarial overfunding into participant-owned accounts. ERISA’s anti-inurement and anti-cutback provisions therefore do not support the claims as pleaded.

##### Question 3: Partial termination

Holding and operative rule: A continuing defined-benefit plan is not partially terminated merely because the sponsor changes contribution structures and benefit options while the plan continues accepting participants and paying promised benefits.

Reasoning: The statutory termination provisions require an actual termination or a qualifying termination of part of the plan. Hughes continued the same plan, drew benefits from the same unsegregated pool, admitted participants, and remained obligated to pay accrued benefits. The creation of a new benefit structure did not itself establish a severable terminated plan or trigger distribution of residual assets. A genuine termination, mass exclusion, or other event satisfying the enacted predicates would present a different case.

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Lockheed Corp. v. Spink*: followed; adoption or amendment of plan terms is nonfiduciary settlor conduct.

*Varity Corp. v. Howe*: distinguished; fiduciary responsibility for administration and communications does not convert the underlying plan-design decision into fiduciary conduct.

*John Hancock Mutual Life Insurance Co. v. Harris Trust & Savings Bank*: distinguished; identification of plan assets does not give defined-benefit participants ownership of actuarial surplus.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The Ninth Circuit judgments are reversed, and the cases are remanded with instructions to dismiss the fiduciary-amendment, participant-surplus, anti-cutback, and partial-termination theories resolved by this decision.

Effect: Hughes may maintain the challenged amendments and use plan assets to pay benefits under the continuing plan while satisfying all accrued-benefit and funding obligations. Independently preserved claims involving administration, misrepresentation, nonpayment, or actual misuse of plan assets remain governed by their own elements.

---

### Boring v. Buncombe County Board of Education

Argued December 9, 1998 | Decided February 17, 1999  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Fourth Circuit; judgment below: Rule 12(b)(6) dismissal of the teacher’s First Amendment claim affirmed en banc, 136 F.3d 364 (4th Cir. 1998), as amended March 18, 1998. ([Law Resource](https://law.resource.org/pub/us/case/reporter/F3/136/136.F3d.364.95-2593.html))  
Area of Law: First Amendment; public schools; assigned curriculum

Questions Presented:

1. Whether a public-school teacher’s selection, production, and direction of curricular material pursuant to assigned teaching responsibilities constitutes the teacher’s protected First Amendment speech or curricular expression subject to the school system’s institutional control.
2. If a teacher retains a First Amendment interest in curricular expression, what constitutional standard governs school officials’ decision to alter the curriculum or impose an adverse employment action because of that curricular choice.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Stone, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: Stone announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Status of assigned curricular expression

Holding and operative rule: A public-school teacher’s selection, production, and direction of material pursuant to assigned teaching responsibilities constitute institutional curricular expression subject to the school system’s lawful control, not the teacher’s personal speech protected from that control by the First Amendment.

Reasoning: Boring’s complaint places the production of *Independence* within an assigned advanced acting class, a school-sponsored performance, and an interscholastic competition. The selection and direction of the play were therefore means by which the school delivered its curriculum. Artistic judgment and professional discretion do not change the legal identity of the speaker when the expression occurs through assigned instructional duties. Simulated *Settle v. Dickson County School Board*, 53 F.3d 152, treats classroom content and graded assignments as institutional curriculum governed by genuine pedagogical requirements. Simulated *Jeffries v. Harleston*, 52 F.3d 9, protects off-duty citizen speech precisely because it was not assigned, commissioned, or delivered as part of the employee’s teaching responsibilities. The complaint alleges institutional consequences arising from an official curricular choice, not retaliation for separate citizen speech. ([Law Resource](https://law.resource.org/pub/us/case/reporter/F3/136/136.F3d.364.95-2593.html))

##### Question 2: Standard governing a retained personal speech interest

Not reached; Question 1 establishes that the assigned curricular acts within the grant were not Boring’s personal First Amendment speech.

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Settle v. Dickson County School Board*: extended; selection and production of a school-sponsored play through an assigned course constitute institutional curriculum.

*Jeffries v. Harleston*: applied; speech outside assigned duties remains distinct from curricular expression undertaken as part of teaching responsibilities.

*Hazelwood School District v. Kuhlmeier*: followed; school authorities may exercise lawful control over school-sponsored curricular expression for genuine pedagogical reasons.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Fourth Circuit is affirmed.

Effect: The dismissal of Boring’s federal First Amendment claim remains in force. The decision does not authorize retaliation for separate citizen speech, independently forbidden viewpoint discrimination, or violations of statutory, contractual, tenure, or state-law protections.

---

### National Collegiate Athletic Association v. Smith

Argued January 20, 1999 | Decided February 23, 1999  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Third Circuit; judgment below: Title IX recipient status sustained on the basis of dues paid by federally funded member institutions, 139 F.3d 180 (3d Cir. 1998). ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep525/usrep525459/usrep525459.pdf))  
Area of Law: Title IX; federal financial assistance; athletic eligibility

Questions Presented:

1. Whether the NCAA becomes a recipient of federal financial assistance under Title IX because federally funded member institutions pay dues to it, and whether another preserved control theory supports coverage.

#### Judgment

Vacated and remanded, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Ginsburg | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Breyer | Supports vacatur and remand | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to vacate and remand.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: Stone announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Recipient status based on membership dues

Holding and operative rule: Receipt of ordinary membership dues from institutions that receive federal funds does not, without more, make the association a recipient of federal financial assistance under Title IX.

Reasoning: Title IX attaches obligations to an entity receiving federal assistance, directly or through a legally sufficient intermediary. It does not reach every commercial or organizational beneficiary of spending by a funded institution. The dues paid to the NCAA were ordinary private payments for membership and association services. They were not alleged to consist of federal funds earmarked for transfer to the NCAA, and they did not retain federal character merely because the member institutions also received federal support. Treating the dues as sufficient would extend recipient status to vendors, landlords, professional organizations, and other entities paid from a recipient’s general resources. That result would erase the Spending Clause distinction between accepting federal assistance and receiving a downstream economic benefit. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep525/usrep525459/usrep525459.pdf))

##### Question 2: Alternative direct-assistance and delegated-control theories

Not reached; the direct-assistance and delegated-control theories were not decided below and remain for first-instance consideration on remand.

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Department of Transportation v. Paralyzed Veterans of America*: applied; an entity that benefits economically from federal assistance is not necessarily a recipient.

*Grove City College v. Bell*: clarified; indirect receipt requires federal assistance extended through an intermediary or earmarked for the entity, not ordinary payments from a recipient’s general funds.

Simulated *Kelley v. Board of Trustees* and *Gebser v. Lago Vista Independent School District*: reaffirmed; Title IX coverage retains a funding-recipient predicate.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Third Circuit is vacated, and the case is remanded with instructions to reject ordinary membership dues as a sufficient recipient-status theory and to consider any properly preserved direct-assistance or delegated-control theory in the first instance.

Effect: Smith may not establish Title IX coverage merely by proving that federally funded colleges paid dues to the NCAA. The decision does not determine whether the NCAA received assistance through the National Youth Sports Program, exercised legally delegated control over a federally assisted education program, discriminated on the basis of sex, or is liable for any remedy.

### Reno v. American-Arab Anti-Discrimination Committee

Argued November 4, 1998 | Decided February 24, 1999  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit; judgment below: district-court jurisdiction and injunctive relief sustained in a selective-enforcement challenge to pending deportation proceedings, 119 F.3d 1367 (9th Cir. 1997). ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep525/usrep525471/usrep525471.pdf))  
Area of Law: Immigration; federal jurisdiction; selective enforcement

Questions Presented:

1. Whether 8 U.S.C. § 1252(g) strips jurisdiction over a selective-enforcement challenge to the Attorney General’s decision to commence or continue removal proceedings, and how the provision applies to pending cases.

#### Judgment

Vacated and remanded, 8-1.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: Souter.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Scalia | Stone, O’Connor, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports vacatur and remand | Scope and temporal application of § 1252(g); absence of a present need for immediate collateral constitutional review |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Stevens | No other Justice | Concurs in the judgment | Statutory prohibition against collateral review of ongoing proceedings |
| Dissent | Souter | No other Justice | Would affirm | Preserved review under the transitional provisions |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to vacate and remand.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: Section 1252(g) bars this separate action because it directly restrains the decision to commence removal proceedings, while later final-order review and any constitutionally indispensable now-or-never channel remain unresolved.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Scope and temporal application of § 1252(g)

Holding and operative rule: Section 1252(g) applies to pending matters and withdraws separate district-court jurisdiction over claims arising from the Attorney General’s decisions to commence proceedings, adjudicate cases, or execute removal orders; it does not extend to every act occurring during the removal process. (joined by Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer).

Reasoning: Congress expressly made § 1252(g) applicable to claims arising from past, pending, and future proceedings. The provision identifies three discrete executive acts rather than the entire universe of immigration enforcement. Respondents seek an injunction stopping the commencement and continuation of their deportation proceedings because of the Government’s alleged selection of them. That claim arises directly from one of the named acts and therefore must use the immigration review sequence rather than a separate district-court action. Applying the provision changes forum and timing; it does not impose new liability for completed conduct. The holding does not govern detention, evidence gathering, collateral mistreatment, or adjudicative errors merely because they occur during a removal case. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep525/usrep525471/usrep525471.pdf))

##### Question 2: Immediate constitutional review

Holding and operative rule: The present record does not require immediate freestanding judicial review of the selective-enforcement theory notwithstanding § 1252(g); the availability and scope of later final-order review or another constitutionally indispensable channel remain unresolved. (joined by Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer).

Reasoning: Respondents can preserve their constitutional objection while the statutory proceedings continue, and the record does not establish that all meaningful judicial review will disappear or that allowing the proceedings to continue will cause an independently irremediable constitutional injury. The Court therefore need not decide whether selective retaliation occurred or whether a sufficiently supported claim could be raised during review of a final order. Nor does it hold that Congress could eliminate every constitutionally adequate forum. The narrower disposition respects the statutory channel without prejudging a constitutional claim that may arise later in a different procedural posture. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep525/usrep525471/usrep525471.pdf))

#### Concurrences

Stevens: The statutory provisions, read together, postpone collateral judicial review of the ongoing proceedings and require dismissal of this action. He would not adopt additional propositions concerning the constitutional claim or the precise operation of the later review route.

#### Dissents

Souter: The transitional provisions preserve the preexisting judicial-review regime for aliens whose proceedings were already pending, and the Court’s construction creates an unnecessary risk that review will be denied altogether. He would sustain jurisdiction.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Thunder Basin Coal Co. v. Reich*: applied; a specialized review sequence displaces a separate pre-final-order action when meaningful judicial consideration remains available.

*Steel Co. v. Citizens for a Better Environment*: followed; the jurisdictional channel is resolved before the selective-enforcement merits.

*McNary v. Haitian Refugee Center, Inc.*: distinguished; this action directly challenges the commencement of particular proceedings rather than a collateral pattern of agency practices.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit is vacated, and the case is remanded with instructions to vacate the district-court injunction and dismiss the separate action for want of jurisdiction.

Effect: The removal proceedings may continue. The Court neither approves retaliatory enforcement nor decides the constitutional merits, and it leaves open later review through the final-order process or another channel that proves constitutionally indispensable on materially different facts.

---

### Holloway v. United States

Argued November 9, 1998 | Decided March 2, 1999  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Second Circuit; judgment below: carjacking convictions affirmed under a conditional-intent instruction, 126 F.3d 82 (2d Cir. 1997). ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep526/usrep526001/usrep526001.pdf))  
Area of Law: Federal criminal law; carjacking; mens rea

Questions Presented:

1. Whether 18 U.S.C. § 2119 requires an unconditional intent to kill or cause serious bodily harm, or is satisfied by a conditional intent to do so if necessary to take the vehicle.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 7-2.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: Scalia, Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stevens | Stone, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Scalia | No other Justice | Would reverse | Complete dissent |
| Dissent | Thomas | No other Justice | Would reverse | Complete dissent |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: Stone announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Conditional intent under § 2119

Holding and operative rule: Section 2119 is satisfied when, at the moment the defendant demands or takes the vehicle, the defendant has made a real decision to kill or cause serious bodily harm if such violence becomes necessary to complete the taking.

Reasoning: The statute directs attention to the defendant’s state of mind at the moment of the forcible demand or taking. A purpose does not cease to be intent merely because its execution depends on the victim’s resistance. The typical armed carjacker prefers surrender without injury but resolves to use serious violence if the driver refuses. Requiring an unconditional desire to harm a compliant victim would exclude the central conduct the statute addresses and would transform the intent element into a requirement of an attempted assault independent of the taking. The conditional purpose must nevertheless be genuine. Proof of intimidation, possession of a weapon, an empty threat, recklessness, or a bluff does not by itself establish that the defendant actually intended the specified harm if the condition occurred. The jury instruction required that finding, and the evidence permitted it. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep526/usrep526001/usrep526001.pdf))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

Scalia: The enacted phrase requires an intent to cause death or serious bodily harm, not an intent contingent on the victim’s refusal to surrender the vehicle. Any ambiguity in the criminal statute should be resolved in Holloway’s favor.

Thomas: The Court inserts a conditional-intent formulation that Congress did not state and substantially enlarges federal criminal liability. He would require proof of the intent described by the statutory text without the added condition.

#### Doctrinal Effect

Traditional criminal-law treatment of conditional intent: followed; a genuine purpose to cause a prohibited result if a condition occurs remains intent concerning that result.

Rule of lenity: declined to extend; the text, structure, and ordinary treatment of conditional purpose do not leave the intent element grievously ambiguous.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Second Circuit is affirmed.

Effect: Holloway’s carjacking convictions remain in force. Future prosecutions must prove a genuine conditional intent to kill or cause serious bodily harm at the time of the demand or taking; intimidation alone, an empty threat, recklessness, or intent formed only afterward is insufficient.

---

**Sources:**

- [U.S. Reports: Department of the Army v. Blue Fox, Inc., 525 U.S. 255 (1999).](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep525/usrep525255/usrep525255.pdf)
- [HUMANA INC. V. FORSYTH](https://www.law.cornell.edu/supct/html/97-303.ZS.html)
- [https://law.resource.org/pub/us/case/reporter/F3/136/136.F3d.364.95-2593.html](https://law.resource.org/pub/us/case/reporter/F3/136/136.F3d.364.95-2593.html)

Source corrections: The state-action, claim-as-property, and revised-procedure paragraph repeated in the briefs for *Cedar Rapids*, *Kumho Tire*, *South Central Bell*, *Jones*, *Mille Lacs*, *Rodriguez-Moreno*, *Murphy Brothers*, and *Mitchell* is an inapplicable clerical carryover from *American Manufacturers* and does not affect those matters. The historical judgment in *South Central Bell* was unanimous, not 5-4. *Murphy Brothers* was historically decided 6-3, not unanimously. *Mitchell* was unanimous on the proposition that a guilty plea does not waive the privilege at sentencing, but the historical judgment and adverse-inference holding were 5-4. *Kumho Tire* was historically reversed, with Justice Stevens alone favoring appellate application on remand rather than direct reinstatement of summary judgment. None of these corrections alters Stone’s approved Section II or creates an adjudicative blocker. `OT_1998CHUNK3.md` ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/526/137/))

## Decisions and Dispositions

### Cedar Rapids Community School District v. Garret F.

Argued November 4, 1998 | Decided March 3, 1999  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Eighth Circuit; judgment below: school district required to provide the continuous nursing services as an IDEA related service, 106 F.3d 822 (8th Cir. 1997). ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/526/66/))  
Area of Law: Education law; IDEA; related services

Questions Presented:

1. Whether the IDEA requires a public school to provide continuous one-on-one nursing services that a ventilator-dependent student needs to attend school, or excludes those services as medical services.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 7-2.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: Kennedy, Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stevens | Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Thomas | Kennedy | Would reverse | Complete dissent |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: Stone announces no ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Required school-day nursing services

Holding and operative rule: The IDEA requires a school district to provide a nonphysician health service that is necessary for a child to attend and benefit from special education, even when the service is continuous, individualized, and costly. The medical-services exclusion covers services that must be performed by a physician, apart from evaluation and diagnosis.

Reasoning: The Act identifies school health services as related services and excludes medical services only within the limited statutory category. *Irving Independent School District v. Tatro* supplies the functional boundary: the identity and legal qualification of the required provider determine whether a service is excluded, not the service’s complexity, duration, or expense. Garret can participate successfully in the regular classroom if a trained person monitors his ventilator, performs suctioning and catheterization, and responds to emergencies. None of those tasks requires a physician. A cost or continuous-care exception would add limitations Congress did not enact and would make access depend on the extent of a child’s disability. Administrative burden remains relevant to lawful implementation of the individualized education program, but it does not change the statutory classification of the service.

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

Thomas, joined by Kennedy: Continuous one-on-one nursing of this scope is medical care in the ordinary sense and lies outside the educational services Congress required school districts to furnish. They would reverse rather than make the physician-services distinction conclusive regardless of the service’s intensity and institutional cost.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Irving Independent School District v. Tatro*: followed and clarified; the physician-services boundary governs the medical-services exclusion, while duration, complexity, and cost do not independently remove a necessary nonphysician service from IDEA coverage.

*Florence County School District Four v. Carter*: applied; a free appropriate public education includes services necessary to make the child’s lawful educational placement effective.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Eighth Circuit is affirmed, and the district court shall require inclusion of the necessary school-day nursing services in Garret’s individualized education program, subject to lawful adjustment as his needs and educational placement change.

Effect: The school district must furnish qualified personnel capable of performing the required nonphysician tasks during Garret’s school day. The decision does not require experimental treatment, services unrelated to educational access, or care that applicable law requires a physician to perform.

### American Manufacturers Mutual Insurance Co. v. Sullivan

Argued January 19, 1999 | Decided March 3, 1999  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Third Circuit; judgment below: dismissals reversed, private insurers treated as state actors, and pre-determination withholding held procedurally deficient, 139 F.3d 158 (3d Cir. 1998). ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/526/40/))  
Area of Law: State action; procedural due process; workers’ compensation

Questions Presented:

1. Whether a private insurer’s decision to defer payment of disputed medical bills under Pennsylvania’s utilization-review procedure constitutes state action under the Fourteenth Amendment.
2. Whether withholding disputed medical bills before a determination that the treatment was reasonable and necessary, without first allowing the employee to submit a written statement, violates due process.

#### Judgment

Reversed in part, vacated in part, and remanded; votes vary by component.

| Judgment component | Disposition and vote | Supporting | Opposing | Relief or remand |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Federal claims against private insurers | Reversed and remanded, 9-0 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | No Justice | Claims predicated solely on a private insurer’s independent use of utilization review shall be dismissed absent a recognized attribution basis. |
| Procedural ruling governing concededly governmental actors | Vacated and remanded, 5-4 | Stone, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | The lower courts shall identify any live and preserved protected-interest theory, determine which original or revised procedure governs it, and conduct any required actor-specific review under *Mathews*. |

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stone | Souter and Breyer in full; Stevens and Ginsburg as to pre-determination payment and governmental-actor remand; O’Connor, Kennedy, and Thomas as to private-insurer attribution and pre-determination payment; Scalia as to private-insurer attribution | Supports the component dispositions stated above | Scope stated by issue |
| Concurrence | Stevens | No other Justice | Supports the component dispositions | Underlying statutory claim and original procedure |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Scalia | O’Connor, Kennedy, Thomas | Concurs in part and dissents in part | Concurs in dismissal of the private-insurer claims; would reverse rather than vacate the governmental-actor ruling |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse the private-insurer component and to vacate and remand the procedural ruling governing concededly governmental actors.  
Public role: Court author.  
Announced ground: Independent private invocation of utilization review is not state action absent a recognized attribution basis. Payment of a particular disputed bill need not precede the statutory reasonableness-and-necessity determination, but the unpressed theory that the underlying claim itself is protected property is reserved, and any live procedural claim against a governmental actor must be assessed on the operative record.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Attribution of a private insurer’s withholding decision

Holding and operative rule: Independent private use of Pennsylvania’s utilization-review option is not state action absent coercion, joint administration, legal compulsion, delegated exclusive public function, selective governmental enforcement, or another recognized attribution basis. (joined by Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, and Breyer).

Reasoning: Pennsylvania created and regulated the utilization-review process, but the private insurer decides whether to dispute a particular treatment and invoke review. The Bureau’s receipt and processing of a standardized request does not direct the insurer’s choice or make the State jointly responsible for it. Extensive regulation and legal recognition of a private decision are insufficient unless the State significantly encouraged, compelled, or participated in the challenged act. The insurer’s withholding therefore remains private conduct on the present record. The rule does not reach a governmental fund, a self-insured public entity, or materially different evidence showing that officials directed or selectively implemented the payment decision.

##### Question 2: Payment before the statutory condition is resolved

Holding and operative rule: Due process does not require payment of a particular disputed medical bill before the treatment has been determined reasonable and necessary under Pennsylvania law. The distinct question whether the underlying statutory claim to a fair adjudication is protected property remains reserved because respondents did not press it. (joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer).

Reasoning: State law creates an entitlement only to payment for treatment satisfying the enacted criteria. An employee whose compensable injury has been accepted has cleared the first statutory condition, but a disputed bill remains contingent on the separate determination that the treatment was reasonable and necessary. The Due Process Clause does not require the insurer or public payer to satisfy that bill before the condition defining the payment obligation is adjudicated. That conclusion is narrow. It does not establish that the State may destroy or arbitrarily adjudicate the underlying statutory claim, and it does not decide the *Logan* theory that the claim itself may constitute protected property. Because respondents did not preserve that distinct theory, the Court neither adopts nor rejects it.

##### Question 3: Governmental actors and the operative procedure

Holding and operative rule: The Third Circuit’s categorical procedural ruling governing concededly governmental actors is vacated. The lower courts must first determine whether a live, preserved protected-interest theory remains, which public actor administered the challenged withholding, and whether the original or revised procedure governs before applying *Mathews v. Eldridge*. (joined by Stone, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer).

Reasoning: The public defendants do not share the private insurers’ attribution defense, but that difference does not establish a procedural violation. Pennsylvania changed the employee-submission process while the litigation was pending, and the present record does not establish the continuing scope of prospective relief, the procedure applicable to each claimant, or a preserved property interest beyond the particular disputed payment. Those matters must be resolved before the constitutional balance is performed. If a live protected interest is properly established, the court must examine the risk of error, the value of additional notice or written participation, the governmental and administrative burdens, and the availability of review. The Court does not approve or invalidate the revised procedure on the present record.

#### Concurrences

Stevens: He would recognize the statutory claim for reasonable and necessary care as protected property and would hold that the original procedure was deficient because it supplied neither adequate notice of automatic withholding nor a meaningful opportunity to submit evidence. He agrees that the revised system and the continuing need for relief must be considered on remand.

Scalia, joined by O’Connor, Kennedy, and Thomas: They agree that the private insurers are not state actors and that payment need not precede the reasonableness-and-necessity determination. They would reverse the governmental-actor ruling outright because the particular payment is not protected property and the unpressed claim-as-property theory cannot support further adjudication.

#### Dissents

Scalia, joined by O’Connor, Kennedy, and Thomas: They oppose vacatur and remand of the governmental-actor component and would direct dismissal of the federal due-process claims in their entirety.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Denver Area Educational Telecommunications Consortium, Inc. v. FCC*: applied; governmental authorization of independently exercised private choice does not establish attribution absent coercion, joint administration, legal compulsion, selective enforcement, or another recognized basis.

*Lugar v. Edmondson Oil Co.* and *Blum v. Yaretsky*: followed; a close nexus between government and the particular challenged act remains necessary.

*Board of Regents v. Roth*: applied; state law defines the claimed entitlement, and no right to payment of a particular bill arises before the enacted reasonableness-and-necessity condition is satisfied.

*Logan v. Zimmerman Brush Co.*: declined to extend; whether the underlying statutory claim is protected property remains unresolved because that theory was not preserved.

*Mathews v. Eldridge*: applied; any live governmental-actor claim requires identification of the protected interest and operative procedure before procedural calibration.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Third Circuit is reversed insofar as it sustains federal claims against private insurers based solely on their independent use of utilization review, vacated insofar as it categorically resolves the procedural claims against concededly governmental actors, and the case is remanded for dismissal of the former claims and the limited threshold and procedural determinations stated here.

Effect: The private insurers are not liable under § 1983 on the attribution theory adjudicated. Pennsylvania need not require payment of every disputed bill before utilization review. Any remaining claim against a governmental actor depends on a live and preserved protected interest, the procedure actually governing the claimant, and an actor-specific application of ordinary procedural-due-process principles.
