### Jackson v. Benson

Argued January 11, 1999 | Decided March 16, 1999  
On writ of certiorari to the Supreme Court of Wisconsin; judgment below: federal Establishment Clause challenge rejected, summary judgment directed for the State, and injunction against the amended Milwaukee Parental Choice Program dissolved, 218 Wis. 2d 835, 578 N.W.2d 602 (Wis. 1998). ([vLex](https://case-law.vlex.com/vid/jackson-v-benson-no-887241195))  
Area of Law: Establishment Clause; school choice; public funding

Questions Presented:

1. Whether a religiously neutral school-choice program violates the Establishment Clause when public funds may be used at religious schools only because eligible parents independently select those schools.
2. What distinguishes constitutionally permissible aid resulting from genuinely independent private choice from governmental financing attributable to the State itself.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 5-4.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Souter | Stone, Stevens, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | O’Connor | Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | Would affirm | Complete dissent |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: Stone announces no ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Public payment of religious-school tuition

Holding and operative rule: A State violates the Establishment Clause when it uses public money or a public monetary equivalent to pay religious-school tuition or finance integrated devotional, theological, or inseparably sectarian instruction, even though neutral eligibility and an intervening parental selection determine the provider.

Reasoning: Wisconsin created the benefit, confined it to approved educational providers, fixed its value by reference to the selected school, and delivered a payment instrument for restrictive endorsement to that provider. The parent could not withdraw the amount for unrestricted private use or redirect it outside the statutory education program. The State therefore satisfied an institutional charge with public value. Parental designation determines which eligible school receives the payment, but it does not change the ownership or designated use of the funds. Simulated *Rosenberger v. Rector and Visitors of the University of Virginia* distinguishes equal civic access from public payment for devotional production. The same distinction applies when the State funds an integrated religious-school curriculum rather than a devotional publication. Neutrality among families and providers does not itself convert public financing into private expenditure.

##### Question 2: Private choice, neutral forums, and attribution

Holding and operative rule: Independent private choice severs governmental attribution when the government supplies a genuinely neutral forum or severable civic service without financing the religious message. It does not sever attribution when restricted public money, a voucher, credit, reimbursement, tax mechanism, or redeemable instrument satisfies a religious provider’s charge for worship or instruction.

Reasoning: The constitutional distinction turns on what government supplies and how the benefit operates. A speaker admitted to an open forum receives access governed by common secular rules, retains authorship, and receives no public payment for the religious message. A tuition program instead transfers state-created value to discharge an educational charge. The form of the transfer cannot control: direct checks, parent-endorsed instruments, reimbursements, redeemable credits, and equivalent mechanisms remain public financing when the beneficiary cannot convert the value into ordinary private funds and the provider receives it for the designated religious service. *American Manufacturers Mutual Insurance Co. v. Sullivan*, decided March 3, 1999, is different. There, government merely authorized an independently exercised private withholding decision; here, the State itself creates, restricts, and disburses the monetary benefit.

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

O’Connor, joined by Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas: The program defines eligibility without reference to religion and leaves the selection of a school to the family, among religious and nonreligious alternatives. They would attribute the resulting aid to the parent’s independent choice and hold that exclusion of religious schools would itself deny equal treatment to eligible families.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Rosenberger v. Rector and Visitors of the University of Virginia*: extended; routing a restricted public payment through a private chooser does not convert public financing of devotional or inseparably sectarian instruction into ordinary private expenditure.

*Capitol Square Review and Advisory Board v. Pinette*: distinguished; neutral forum access without public financing remains private religious expression.

*Good News/Good Sports Club v. School District of Ladue*: distinguished; equal access to publicly controlled facilities under common secular rules differs from payment of a provider’s religious-school tuition charge.

*Board of Education of Kiryas Joel Village School District v. Grumet*: applied; public benefits involving religion must remain governed by neutral civil administration and may not allocate public value to religious exercise beyond genuinely severable civic services.

*American Manufacturers Mutual Insurance Co. v. Sullivan*: distinguished; private invocation of a regulatory option differs from the State’s own creation and disbursement of a restricted monetary benefit.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The federal Establishment Clause component of the judgment of the Supreme Court of Wisconsin is reversed, and the case is remanded with instructions to enter relief preventing public payment for religious-school tuition and integrated devotional or inseparably sectarian instruction, while applying Wisconsin severability law to the remaining program.

Effect: Wisconsin may preserve a severable choice program for nonreligious schools and genuinely severable, publicly controlled secular services supplied to religious and nonreligious participants on equal civil terms. It may not satisfy religious-school tuition through checks, vouchers, reimbursements, credits, tax mechanisms, redeemable instruments, or another third-party route. The state-law judgments outside the grant remain undisturbed.

---

### Kumho Tire Co. v. Carmichael

Argued December 7, 1998 | Decided March 23, 1999  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Eleventh Circuit; judgment below: exclusion of the tire-failure expert and summary judgment for defendants reversed, 131 F.3d 1433 (11th Cir. 1997). ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/526/137/))  
Area of Law: Evidence; Rule 702; expert testimony

Questions Presented:

1. Whether the trial judge’s Rule 702 gatekeeping obligation applies to technical and experience-based expert testimony, and how flexibly the *Daubert* considerations may be used.

#### Judgment

Reversed, 7-2.  
Supporting: O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: Stone, Stevens.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Breyer | Stone and Stevens as to the Rule 702 gatekeeping, flexibility, and review propositions; O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, and Ginsburg in full | Supports reversal | Scope stated by issue |
| Concurrence | Scalia | O’Connor, Thomas | Supports reversal | Complete concurrence |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Stevens | Stone | Concurs in part and dissents in part | Joins the governing Rule 702 propositions; would remand for appellate application |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand rather than reinstate summary judgment directly.  
Public role: joins the Court’s opinion as to Rule 702’s scope, flexibility, and standard of review, and joins Stevens’s concurrence in part and dissent in part in full.  
Announced ground: Rule 702 applies to scientific, technical, and other specialized testimony, and *Daubert* supplies flexible rather than mandatory considerations. Stone would require the Eleventh Circuit to apply those principles and abuse-of-discretion review in the first instance.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Gatekeeping beyond scientific testimony

Holding and operative rule: Rule 702 requires trial judges to assess the reliability and fit of scientific, technical, and other specialized expert testimony. The considerations identified in *Daubert* are flexible, nonexclusive tools whose relevance depends on the particular expertise, methodology, issue, and record. (joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer).

Reasoning: Rule 702 applies one reliability requirement to scientific, technical, and other specialized knowledge. The testimonial latitude granted to experts rests on the premise that their opinions derive reliably from the knowledge and experience of the discipline, regardless of professional label. A trial court may therefore consider testing, peer review, error rates, general acceptance, standards, experience, or another indicator that usefully measures the method at issue. No consideration is mandatory in every field, and the court may not exclude reliable testimony merely because a laboratory test or published validation is unavailable. Under simulated *General Electric Co. v. Joiner*, appellate courts independently review the meaning of Rule 702 but review the trial court’s selection and application of reliability considerations for abuse of discretion.

##### Question 2: Carlson’s tire-failure methodology

Holding and operative rule: The district court did not abuse its discretion in excluding Carlson’s opinion because the record did not reliably connect his visual and tactile inspection method to the conclusion that a manufacturing or design defect, rather than abuse or deterioration, caused this tire’s failure.

Reasoning: Carlson’s experience qualified him to examine the tire, but qualification does not establish that the asserted inferential method is reliable. The tire was old, extensively worn, inadequately repaired, and displayed several conditions Carlson associated with misuse. He did not identify objective standards explaining how his method distinguished defect from overdeflection, puncture-related heat, wear, or repair damage, and he did not show that other tire experts ordinarily made the fine distinctions on which his conclusion depended. The district court reconsidered its initial ruling, recognized that the *Daubert* considerations were illustrative, and examined the method under additional possible reliability criteria. Its conclusion rested on the missing analytical bridge rather than a categorical demand for laboratory science. Seven Justices therefore reinstate the exclusion ruling and the resulting summary judgment.

#### Concurrences

Scalia, joined by O’Connor and Thomas: Flexibility permits the trial judge to select useful reliability measures; it does not authorize abandonment of Rule 702’s reliability requirement. Experience-based testimony must still explain how the experience supports the method, how the method supports the conclusion, and why it was reliably applied.

Stevens, joined by Stone: They agree that Rule 702 governs all expert testimony and that the *Daubert* considerations are nonexclusive. They would remand so the Eleventh Circuit could review the district court’s case-specific ruling under the complete abuse-of-discretion framework before this Court reinstated summary judgment.

#### Dissents

Stevens, joined by Stone: They dissent only from the Court’s direct case-specific disposition and would reverse the Eleventh Circuit’s categorical legal rule while remanding for appellate application.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Daubert v. Merrell Dow Pharmaceuticals, Inc.*: extended and clarified; its gatekeeping obligation applies to all expert knowledge within Rule 702, while its listed considerations remain flexible and nonexclusive.

*General Electric Co. v. Joiner*: followed and reaffirmed; abuse-of-discretion review governs both the ultimate admissibility ruling and the trial judge’s reasonable selection of reliability considerations, and an unsupported analytical gap permits exclusion.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Eleventh Circuit is reversed, and the district court’s exclusion of Carlson’s testimony and entry of summary judgment for the defendants are reinstated.

Effect: Carlson’s opinion is inadmissible, and the Carmichaels’ defect claims cannot proceed on the present record. Trial courts retain broad latitude to assess different forms of expertise but must require a reliable connection between the expert’s method, its application, and the conclusion offered.

---

### South Central Bell Telephone Co. v. Alabama

Argued January 19, 1999 | Decided March 23, 1999  
On writ of certiorari to the Supreme Court of Alabama; judgment below: franchise-tax challenge rejected under prior state litigation and the assessments left in place, 711 So. 2d 1005 (Ala. 1998). ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/526/160/))  
Area of Law: Dormant Commerce Clause; nonparty preclusion; discriminatory taxation

Questions Presented:

1. Whether Alabama’s franchise-tax scheme unconstitutionally discriminated against foreign corporations and whether state procedural grounds barred federal review.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Breyer | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence | O’Connor | No other Justice | Supports reversal and remand | Complete concurrence |
| Concurrence | Thomas | No other Justice | Supports reversal and remand | Complete concurrence |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: Stone announces no ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Supreme Court review and nonparty preclusion

Holding and operative rule: The Eleventh Amendment does not bar Supreme Court review of federal questions decided in a State’s own courts. Taxpayers are not bound by prior litigation involving different taxpayers and tax years merely because they shared an interest in the issue, knew of the earlier case, used overlapping counsel, or requested an abeyance.

Reasoning: *McKesson Corp. v. Division of Alcoholic Beverages and Tobacco* confirms this Court’s appellate authority over federal questions in state-court judgments, including cases in which the State was a defendant below. Alabama supplies no basis for overruling that rule. On preclusion, simulated *Richards v. Jefferson County* controls directly. The prior litigation was not a class action, the present taxpayers did not control it, and no fiduciary, privity, statutory-representation, or other legally accountable relationship required the earlier plaintiffs to protect their interests. Awareness of the first action and common legal interests do not create representation. The request to hold this case in abeyance reserved the parties’ ability to reactivate it and did not constitute consent to be bound.

##### Question 2: Discrimination against foreign corporations

Holding and operative rule: Alabama’s franchise-tax structure discriminates against foreign corporations because it taxes their actual capital employed in the State while permitting domestic corporations to reduce their tax base by assigning nominal par value. The domestic shares tax is not a compensatory burden because it reaches a different event and is neither substantially equivalent nor roughly comparable.

Reasoning: The practical structure gives domestic corporations control over a franchise-tax base unavailable to competing foreign corporations. That origin-based disadvantage is facially discriminatory and receives the demanding review established in simulated *Oregon Waste Systems, Inc. v. Department of Environmental Quality*. Alabama identifies the domestic shares tax as an offset, but that tax is imposed on ownership of shares in a domestic corporation, while the foreign franchise tax is imposed on the corporation’s decision to employ capital and conduct business in Alabama. The burdens therefore are not mutually exclusive proxies for substantially equivalent events, do not serve the same purpose in the required sense, and are not roughly approximate in amount. Under simulated *West Lynn Creamery, Inc. v. Healy*, the Court examines the linked operation of the scheme rather than its labels.

##### Question 3: Constitutionally adequate tax remedy

Holding and operative rule: Alabama must provide a constitutionally adequate remedy for the discriminatory exaction, but the State may select among lawful refund, retrospective equalization, corrective assessment, or another mechanism that fully removes the unconstitutional disadvantage.

Reasoning: Once a State collects a tax under an unconstitutional discriminatory structure, it may not retain the benefit while denying affected taxpayers an adequate corrective route. Federal law requires a real remedy but does not compel this Court to design Alabama’s tax administration in the first instance. The state courts must determine which mechanisms remain available under state law and apply one consistently with *McKesson*. Any retrospective assessment must satisfy due process and other governing limits, and any refund must cure rather than preserve the differential burden. Prospective amendment alone cannot dispose of properly preserved claims concerning completed discriminatory collections.

#### Concurrences

O’Connor: Alabama did not properly preserve its request that the Court abandon dormant Commerce Clause doctrine, and nothing in the present briefing supplies a persuasive reason to reconsider that established body of law.

Thomas: He agrees that Alabama’s broad challenge to dormant Commerce Clause doctrine was not timely presented and therefore joins the Court’s application of existing precedent without addressing that challenge.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*McKesson Corp. v. Division of Alcoholic Beverages and Tobacco*: followed; the Eleventh Amendment does not restrict this Court’s appellate review of federal questions decided by state courts, and discriminatory taxation requires an adequate remedy.

*Richards v. Jefferson County*: followed; shared taxpayer interests, awareness, overlapping counsel, and a routine abeyance request do not bind nonparties absent legally accountable representation or another recognized relationship.

*Oregon Waste Systems, Inc. v. Department of Environmental Quality*: applied; a facially discriminatory tax survives as compensatory only upon proof of a substantially equivalent intrastate burden serving the same purpose.

*West Lynn Creamery, Inc. v. Healy*: applied; practical operation and linked economic burdens control over formal labels.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Supreme Court of Alabama is reversed, and the case is remanded with instructions to reject the preclusion defense, invalidate the discriminatory franchise-tax structure as applied to the petitioners, and provide a constitutionally adequate remedy under lawful state procedures.

Effect: The challenged assessments cannot remain effective merely because different taxpayers lost earlier litigation. Alabama may choose the remedial mechanism, but it must eliminate the discriminatory burden and address the petitioners’ preserved claims for completed tax years consistently with federal law.

### Jones v. United States

Argued October 5, 1998 | Decided March 24, 1999  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit; judgment below: conviction and twenty-five-year sentence affirmed after the judge found serious bodily injury by a preponderance of the evidence, 116 F.3d 1487 (9th Cir. 1997). ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/526/227/))  
Area of Law: Criminal law; jury trial; federal carjacking

Questions Presented:

1. Whether the serious-bodily-injury and death provisions of 18 U.S.C. § 2119 are sentencing factors for the judge or offense elements that must be charged and proved to a jury.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 5-4.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, Scalia, Souter, Ginsburg. Opposing: O’Connor, Kennedy, Thomas, Breyer.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Souter | Stone, Stevens, Scalia, Ginsburg | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Breyer | O’Connor, Kennedy, Thomas | Would affirm | Complete dissent |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: Stone announces no ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Injury and death as aggravated-offense elements

Holding and operative rule: The governing version of § 2119 creates aggravated offenses. Serious bodily injury and death increase the maximum punishment authorized by the base carjacking conviction and therefore must be alleged in the indictment and admitted as elements or proved to a jury beyond a reasonable doubt.

Reasoning: Section 2119 establishes a base carjacking offense and sharply higher punishment when the offense results in serious bodily injury or death. Victim injury traditionally distinguishes greater offenses, and the statutory structure links those facts to increases in the maximum authorized punishment rather than merely to selection of a sentence within one range. Simulated *Almendarez-Torres v. United States*, decided March 24, 1998, supplies a direct rule: every fact increasing the conviction-authorized maximum is an element, including a fact Congress may have expected a judge to find at sentencing. Jones’s indictment did not allege serious bodily injury, the jury returned only a general verdict on the base offense, and the judge supplied the omitted finding afterward. Simulated *United States v. Watts* cannot sustain the sentence because its rule is confined to findings made within the maximum already authorized by the conviction.

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

Breyer, joined by O’Connor, Kennedy, and Thomas: The statute’s grammar and placement of the enhanced penalties indicate sentencing factors rather than separate offenses. They adhere to their dissent in simulated *Almendarez-Torres* and would permit judicial determination of the injury fact under the applicable sentencing standard.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Almendarez-Torres v. United States*, decided March 24, 1998: followed; a fact increasing the statutory maximum is an aggravated-offense element that must be charged and admitted or proved to a jury.

*United States v. Gaudin*: applied; the jury determines every charged offense element beyond a reasonable doubt.

*United States v. Watts; United States v. Putra*: distinguished; judicial factfinding remains available only within the punishment authorized by the conviction.

*Holloway v. United States*, decided March 2, 1999: distinguished; its conditional-intent holding governs the mens rea of the carjacking offense and does not classify the separate injury and death provisions.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit is reversed, Jones’s twenty-five-year sentence is vacated, and the case is remanded for resentencing within the range authorized by the indictment and verdict establishing the base offense.

Effect: Jones’s base carjacking conviction remains intact, but the judicial serious-bodily-injury finding cannot support the higher statutory maximum. Any prosecution of an aggravated offense remains subject to ordinary indictment, proof, finality, and double-jeopardy rules.

### Minnesota v. Mille Lacs Band of Chippewa Indians

Argued December 2, 1998 | Decided March 24, 1999  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Eighth Circuit; judgment below: continuing 1837 Treaty hunting, fishing, and gathering rights recognized subject to appropriate conservation regulation, 124 F.3d 904 (8th Cir. 1997). ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/526/172/))  
Area of Law: Federal Indian law; treaty rights; statehood

Questions Presented:

1. Whether the Mille Lacs Band’s 1837 Treaty hunting, fishing, and gathering rights were terminated by an 1850 Executive Order, the 1855 Treaty, Minnesota statehood, or later events.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 6-3.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Stone, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Scalia | Kennedy, Thomas | Would reverse | Complete dissent |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: Stone announces no ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Continuing force of the 1837 Treaty rights

Holding and operative rule: The Mille Lacs Band’s 1837 Treaty hunting, fishing, and gathering rights survived because the 1850 Executive Order did not lawfully revoke them, the 1855 Treaty did not clearly relinquish them, and Minnesota’s admission to the Union did not itself extinguish federal treaty rights.

Reasoning: Treaty terms are read as the tribes would reasonably have understood them, and a reserved right is not terminated by implication. Although the 1837 Treaty made exercise of the privilege subject to presidential pleasure, the 1850 order relied on removal authority that did not lawfully authorize cancellation of the separate usufructuary reservation in the ceded territory. The 1855 Treaty conveyed additional lands and established new arrangements but contained no clear language surrendering the 1837 rights. Silence, changed circumstances, and later nonuse cannot supply the missing relinquishment. Minnesota entered the Union subject to the Supremacy Clause, and equal footing does not repeal an existing federal treaty. The rights remain subject to necessary, nondiscriminatory conservation regulation under the standards governing treaty-reserved resources, but the State may not regulate them as though no federal reservation existed.

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

Scalia, joined by Kennedy and Thomas: The 1837 Treaty expressly conditioned the privileges on presidential pleasure, the 1850 order exercised that authority, and the 1855 negotiations and statehood confirm termination. They would read the historical instruments together and reverse the judgment recognizing continuing rights.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*United States v. Dion*: followed; abrogation of treaty-reserved rights requires clear federal action and may not be inferred from general policy or later circumstances.

*Menominee Tribe v. United States*: followed; a later treaty does not extinguish reserved hunting and fishing rights without clear relinquishment.

*Washington v. Washington State Commercial Passenger Fishing Vessel Assn.*: applied; continuing treaty rights remain enforceable subject to necessary and nondiscriminatory conservation measures.

*Ward v. Race Horse*: overruled in part; statehood alone does not terminate federal treaty rights absent clear federal abrogation.

*Hagen v. Utah*: distinguished; clear statutory language supporting reservation diminishment does not authorize implied abrogation of a separate treaty-reserved use right.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Eighth Circuit is affirmed, and the declaratory and injunctive framework recognizing the Band’s treaty-reserved rights remains in force.

Effect: The Mille Lacs Band may continue exercising the 1837 hunting, fishing, and gathering rights within the treaty territory. Minnesota may enforce measures shown to be necessary for conservation and applied without discrimination against the treaty right. Private land access, conduct outside the ceded territory, and unrelated criminal regulation remain unresolved.

### United States v. Rodriguez-Moreno

Argued December 7, 1998 | Decided March 30, 1999  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Third Circuit; judgment below: § 924(c) conviction vacated for improper venue because the firearm use occurred outside New Jersey, 121 F.3d 841 (3d Cir. 1997). ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/526/275/))  
Area of Law: Criminal procedure; venue; firearm offenses

Questions Presented:

1. Whether venue for a § 924(c) prosecution lies in a district where a continuing kidnapping occurred even though the defendant used the firearm in another district.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 7-2.  
Supporting: Stone, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: Stevens, Scalia.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Thomas | Stone, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Scalia | Stevens | Would affirm | Complete dissent |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: Stone announces no ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Venue tied to a continuing predicate offense

Holding and operative rule: When a § 924(c) charge is predicated on a continuing kidnapping, venue lies in every district where the kidnapping continued, even if the firearm was physically used in another district, because the firearm offense’s essential conduct includes its use or carriage during and in relation to that continuing crime.

Reasoning: Venue is determined from the nature of the offense Congress defined and the location of its essential conduct. Section 924(c) does not punish firearm possession in isolation. It punishes use or carriage during and in relation to a predicate crime, and the relation to that crime is indispensable to the federal offense. Kidnapping under § 1201 continues while the victim remains unlawfully seized, confined, and transported. The victim remained captive while the defendants moved through New Jersey, so an essential part of the conduct to which the firearm offense was tied occurred there. This is not venue based merely on consequences or background circumstances. The § 924(c) offense could not exist on the charged theory without the continuing kidnapping itself. The rule does not place venue wherever effects are felt or where a completed predicate has no continuing connection to later firearm conduct.

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

Scalia, joined by Stevens: The essential conduct prohibited by § 924(c) is using or carrying the firearm, and venue should lie only where that conduct occurred. They would treat the predicate offense as a circumstance establishing federal liability rather than an independent situs for the firearm count.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*United States v. Cabrales*: distinguished and clarified; venue may not rest on effects alone, but it may lie where an essential continuing conduct element of the charged offense occurs.

18 U.S.C. § 3237(a): applied; a continuing kidnapping occurs in each district through which the unlawful confinement and transportation proceed.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Third Circuit is reversed, the § 924(c) conviction is reinstated, and the case is remanded for further proceedings on any unresolved claim consistent with that reinstatement.

Effect: Rodriguez-Moreno may be prosecuted for the firearm offense in New Jersey because the charged kidnapping continued there. The decision does not permit venue based solely on remote effects, the victim’s later location, or a predicate offense completed before the firearm conduct began.

### Murphy Brothers, Inc. v. Michetti Pipe Stringing, Inc.

Argued March 1, 1999 | Decided April 5, 1999  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Eleventh Circuit; judgment below: pre-service receipt of the complaint held sufficient to start the removal period and remand to state court ordered, 125 F.3d 1396 (11th Cir. 1997). ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/526/344/))  
Area of Law: Civil procedure; removal; service of process

Questions Presented:

1. Whether the 30-day removal period begins when a defendant receives a courtesy copy of the complaint before formal service or only when formal service or waiver makes the defendant subject to the court’s authority.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 7-2.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: Scalia, Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Ginsburg | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Scalia | Thomas | Would affirm | Complete dissent |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: Stone announces no ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Trigger for the removal period

Holding and operative rule: The § 1446(b) removal period begins when formal service, waiver, or a legally equivalent authority-asserting process subjects the defendant to the court’s power and supplies the complaint, not when the defendant informally receives a courtesy copy before service.

Reasoning: Service of process is the event that formally makes a named defendant a party and imposes the obligation to appear, defend, or risk default. Section 1446(b)’s reference to receipt through service or otherwise must be read within that procedural setting. The additional language accommodates state systems in which the summons is served before the complaint, the complaint is made available through filing, or process takes a legally equivalent form. It does not make a faxed courtesy copy the one procedural event capable of extinguishing a federal removal right before any court has acquired authority over the defendant. Murphy received the complaint informally, was formally served later, and removed within 30 days of that service. Its notice was timely. The holding does not extend the period after valid service or excuse deliberate evasion of lawful process.

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

Scalia, joined by Thomas: Section 1446(b) starts the period upon receipt of the filed complaint through service or otherwise, and the phrase’s ordinary meaning includes informal delivery. They would affirm because Murphy possessed the complaint more than 30 days before removal.

#### Doctrinal Effect

28 U.S.C. § 1446(b): clarified; informal pre-service possession of a complaint does not start the removal period.

Federal Rule of Civil Procedure 4: followed; formal service or waiver remains the ordinary authority-asserting event that obliges a named defendant to participate in the litigation.

*Caterpillar Inc. v. Lewis*: distinguished; cure of a removal defect before trial does not determine when the statutory removal clock begins.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Eleventh Circuit is reversed, and the case is remanded with instructions that the district court treat Murphy’s notice of removal as timely and decide subject-matter jurisdiction and any other preserved remand ground.

Effect: The faxed courtesy copy did not forfeit Murphy’s opportunity to remove. The decision establishes timeliness only and does not itself establish federal subject-matter jurisdiction or foreclose another independently valid basis for remand.

### Mitchell v. United States

Argued December 9, 1998 | Decided April 5, 1999  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Third Circuit; judgment below: sentence affirmed after the guilty plea was treated as waiving the privilege and silence was used to support the drug-quantity finding, 122 F.3d 185 (3d Cir. 1997). ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/526/314/))  
Area of Law: Fifth Amendment; guilty pleas; sentencing

Questions Presented:

1. Whether a guilty plea waives the privilege against compelled self-incrimination at sentencing and whether a court may draw an adverse inference from the defendant’s silence in finding drug quantity.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 6-3.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: O’Connor, Scalia, Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Kennedy | Stone, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Scalia | O’Connor, Thomas | Would affirm | Adverse-inference holding only |
| Dissent | Thomas | No other Justice | Would affirm | Additional objection to extending the no-adverse-inference rule |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand with instructions to vacate the sentence.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: Stone announces no ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Effect of the guilty plea on the privilege

Holding and operative rule: A guilty plea does not waive the Fifth Amendment privilege at sentencing with respect to disputed facts bearing on punishment, unless the defendant has separately waived the privilege through voluntary testimony or another legally sufficient act. (joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer).

Reasoning: A plea admits the elements of the offense and surrenders the right to trial; it does not make the defendant a compelled witness at the sentencing stage. The limited statements required by Rule 11 serve to establish that the plea is knowing, voluntary, and factually supported. They do not place every disputed detail of the offense into testimonial controversy or authorize compulsory examination about punishment. Until sentence is imposed, answers concerning drug quantity and related conduct may increase penal exposure. *Estelle v. Smith* therefore remains applicable outside capital cases: the privilege continues while testimony can produce additional criminal punishment. Mitchell admitted the conspiracy but expressly disputed the quantity attributable to her.

##### Question 2: Adverse inference from sentencing silence

Holding and operative rule: A sentencing court may not draw an adverse factual inference from the defendant’s silence when determining the circumstances and details of the offense that affect punishment.

Reasoning: Sentencing is part of the criminal case, and the Government remains responsible for proving the facts on which it seeks greater punishment under the governing standard. Converting silence into evidence would burden the continuing privilege and make the defendant an instrument for filling gaps in the prosecution’s proof. The district court expressly treated Mitchell’s failure to contradict cooperating witnesses as a reason to credit their higher quantity account. That use of silence was impermissible. The court may evaluate the admissible testimony, documentary evidence, and voluntary statements, but it must decide quantity without adding evidentiary weight to Mitchell’s invocation of the privilege. The Court does not decide whether silence may bear on lack of remorse or whether a defendant seeking an affirmative acceptance-of-responsibility reduction may be required to satisfy a lawful burden associated with that benefit.

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

Scalia, joined by O’Connor and Thomas: They agree that the guilty plea did not waive Mitchell’s right to invoke the privilege at sentencing but would permit the sentencer to draw reasonable factual inferences from her refusal to testify. In their view, an inference is not compulsion within the Fifth Amendment’s text.

Thomas: He separately questions the constitutional foundation of the no-adverse-inference rule and would not extend *Griffin* from the trial verdict to judicial factfinding at sentencing.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Estelle v. Smith*: followed and extended; the privilege continues through noncapital sentencing while testimony may increase punishment.

*Griffin v. California*: extended; the prohibition against adverse factual inferences from a defendant’s silence applies when a sentencing court determines facts concerning the offense.

*Ohio Adult Parole Authority v. Woodard*: distinguished; an optional executive-clemency interview after conviction and sentence is not part of the criminal adjudication and does not govern sentencing factfinding.

Federal Rule of Criminal Procedure 11: clarified; a guilty plea and plea colloquy waive trial rights but do not themselves waive the privilege concerning disputed sentencing facts.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Third Circuit is reversed, Mitchell’s sentence is vacated, and the case is remanded for resentencing based on admissible affirmative evidence without treating her silence as evidence against her.

Effect: Mitchell’s guilty plea and admitted offenses remain intact. The district court must redetermine drug quantity without an adverse inference from silence. Voluntary statements, immunity-backed testimony, and independently applicable requirements for an affirmative sentencing reduction remain governed by their own rules.

Chronology and source corrections: *Houghton* shares the April 5 release date with *Murphy Brothers* and *Mitchell* and, under the Engine’s rule for uncoordinated same-date decisions, uses the same pre-April 5 entering-law baseline. The official reports also establish that Justice Breyer filed a concurrence in *Houghton*; *UNUM* was affirmed in part, reversed in part, and remanded; Justice Thomas concurred in the judgment in *203 North LaSalle* while Justice Stevens dissented; *El Paso* was vacated and remanded; and *Saenz* reviewed the Ninth Circuit’s judgment at 134 F.3d 1400 affirming a preliminary injunction, not a permanent judgment entered by a three-judge district court. These corrections do not alter Stone’s approved Section II or create an adjudicative blocker. `OT_1998CHUNK4.md` ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep526/usrep526295/usrep526295.pdf))

## Decisions and Dispositions

### Wyoming v. Houghton

Argued January 12, 1999 | Decided April 5, 1999  
On writ of certiorari to the Supreme Court of Wyoming; judgment below: conviction reversed and methamphetamine found in Houghton’s purse suppressed, 956 P.2d 363 (Wyo. 1998). ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep526/usrep526295/usrep526295.pdf))  
Area of Law: Fourth Amendment; automobile searches; passenger belongings

Questions Presented:

1. Whether probable cause to search a vehicle for contraband automatically authorizes police to search a passenger’s closed purse found in the vehicle.

#### Judgment

Reversed, 5-4.  
Supporting: O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Breyer. Opposing: Stone, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Scalia | O’Connor, Kennedy, Thomas, Breyer | Supports reversal | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence | Breyer | No other Justice | Supports reversal | Automobile-only and container-versus-person boundaries |
| Dissent | Stevens | Stone, Souter, Ginsburg | Would affirm | Complete dissent |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.  
Public role: joins Stevens’s dissent in full.  
Announced ground: Probable cause arising from the driver’s possession and admissions does not automatically extend to an objectively identifiable personal effect of a nonsuspect passenger without facts connecting that passenger or container to the suspected contraband.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Personal privacy in the passenger’s purse

Holding and operative rule: A passenger may invoke the Fourth Amendment with respect to a closed purse established as her own. Lack of a privacy interest in the surrounding vehicle does not eliminate the passenger’s independent privacy interest in the container. (joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer).

Reasoning: Simulated *United States v. Wellons* makes privacy in closed personal luggage independent of the owner’s authority over the vehicle in which it is found. Houghton therefore may challenge the opening of her purse even though she neither owned nor controlled the car. Her identification of the purse after its opening does not prevent her from establishing that the searched effect was hers. The personal privacy interest answers who may invoke the Fourth Amendment, but it does not itself determine whether the automobile exception supplied lawful authority to conduct the search. Ownership and search authority remain distinct inquiries.

##### Question 2: Scope of probable cause to search the vehicle

Holding and operative rule: When officers have probable cause to search a vehicle for a particular object, the automobile exception permits inspection of every container found in the vehicle that is capable of concealing that object, including a container owned by a passenger. The rule does not authorize a search of the passenger’s person.

Reasoning: Simulated *Pennsylvania v. Labron; Pennsylvania v. Kilgore* permits a warrantless search of a readily mobile vehicle and containers within the scope of vehicle probable cause. The permissible scope turns on the object sought and the places within the vehicle where that object could be concealed, not on the ownership of each container. Requiring officers to determine ownership before opening a container would make the lawful scope of the search depend on uncertain statements made during a roadside encounter and would allow contraband to be shifted into a passenger’s property. The purse was inside the car and could contain the drugs for which probable cause existed. The distinction between property and the body remains controlling: *Di Re* and *Ybarra* protect persons against searches based solely on their proximity to suspected activity, but do not remove capable containers from a lawful vehicle search. ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep526/usrep526295/usrep526295.pdf))

#### Concurrences

Breyer: The rule is confined to automobiles and containers found within them. It does not extend to a passenger’s person, and the difference between a container search and a bodily search remains constitutionally significant.

#### Dissents

Stevens, joined by Stone, Souter, and Ginsburg: Probable cause focused on the driver should not permit inspection of an obviously passenger-owned purse without individualized cause connecting the purse or passenger to the contraband. The automobile exception’s reduced protection for the driver’s vehicle does not erase a passenger’s substantial privacy in a closed personal effect.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*United States v. Wellons*: followed and clarified; independent privacy in a closed personal container does not determine whether a recognized warrant exception reaches that container.

*Pennsylvania v. Labron; Pennsylvania v. Kilgore*: clarified and extended; vehicle probable cause reaches passenger-owned containers capable of concealing the object of the search.

*United States v. Ross* and *California v. Acevedo*: followed; the object of probable cause and the places capable of concealing it define the permissible scope of an automobile search.

*United States v. Di Re* and *Ybarra v. Illinois*: distinguished; their heightened protection for searches of persons does not govern containers found in a vehicle.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Supreme Court of Wyoming is reversed, and the Wyoming courts shall conduct further proceedings without suppressing the purse evidence on the passenger-ownership ground rejected here.

Effect: The methamphetamine found in Houghton’s purse is not excluded merely because the purse belonged to a nonsuspect passenger. Any independently preserved challenge to the existence of vehicle probable cause, the stop, a search of Houghton’s person, or another distinct police act remains governed by its own facts and law.

### In re Sealed Case

Argued January 13, 1999 | Decided April 13, 1999  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the District of Columbia Circuit; judgment below: refusal to recognize the asserted Secret Service protective-function privilege and order compelling federal grand-jury testimony affirmed, 148 F.3d 1073 (D.C. Cir. 1998). ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/148/1073/510615/))  
Area of Law: Evidence; Rule 501; federal grand juries

Questions Presented:

1. Whether Federal Rule of Evidence 501 and federal common law recognize a Secret Service protective-function privilege against compelled testimony concerning information obtained while protecting the President.
2. If such a privilege exists, whether it is absolute or qualified, and what showing of criminal investigative need is sufficient to overcome it.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 7-2.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas. Opposing: Ginsburg, Breyer.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Souter | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Breyer | Ginsburg | Would reverse and remand | Qualified protective-function privilege |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: Stone announces no ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Recognition of a protective-function privilege

Holding and operative rule: Federal Rule of Evidence 501 does not recognize, on this record, a Secret Service protective-function privilege against compelled testimony before a federal grand jury concerning information obtained while protecting the President.

Reasoning: Rule 501 begins with the obligation to provide relevant evidence and recognizes a new privilege only when confidentiality serves a sufficiently established public good, the proposed protection is likely to advance that good, and the rule has workable boundaries. Presidential security is an interest of exceptional importance, but the asserted relationship is operational rather than a traditionally protected confidential-communications relationship. The proposed privilege would cover ordinary observations as well as communications and would suppress later-significant evidence unless an agent contemporaneously recognized a felony. The record contains serious predictions that compelled testimony could affect presidential cooperation, but no adjudicative finding that testimony has reduced protective access or materially impaired security. Sealing, protective orders, tailored questioning, executive privilege, classified-information rules, and other source-specific protections remain available. Rule 501 does not convert institutional importance alone into categorical immunity from the grand jury. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/148/1073/510615/))

##### Question 2: Absolute or qualified scope

Not reached; the Court recognizes no protective-function privilege on the record presented and therefore establishes no privilege-specific overcoming standard.

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

Breyer, joined by Ginsburg: The unique constitutional and physical importance of presidential security, together with the evidence concerning the need for close protective proximity, warrants recognition of a qualified privilege for information acquired in immediate protective proximity to the President. They would reverse and remand for a determination whether the grand jury’s particular need for the testimony overcomes that protection. ([Legal Information Institute](https://www.law.cornell.edu/supct/html/98-93.ZD.html))

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Jaffee v. Redmond*: applied and limited to its predicate; Rule 501 protects an established confidential relationship when categorical confidentiality demonstrably serves the relationship, but does not automatically protect operational observations.

*Swidler & Berlin v. United States*: distinguished; refusal to balance away an established attorney-client privilege does not justify creation of a new privilege lacking the required predicate.

*United States v. Nixon* and *Trammel v. United States*: followed; evidentiary privileges are narrowly recognized against the prevailing obligation to supply probative evidence.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the District of Columbia Circuit is affirmed, and the order compelling the Secret Service testimony remains enforceable subject to ordinary evidentiary objections and lawful protective measures imposed by the supervising court.

Effect: Secret Service personnel may not refuse federal grand-jury testimony solely by invoking a protective-function privilege. The decision does not resolve classified information, state secrets, executive privilege, operational security details protected by another source, or a materially different proceeding and record.

### UNUM Life Insurance Co. of America v. Ward

Argued February 24, 1999 | Decided April 20, 1999  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit; judgment below: denial of ERISA benefits reversed under California’s notice-prejudice and employer-agency rules, 135 F.3d 1276 (9th Cir. 1998). ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep526/usrep526358/usrep526358.pdf))  
Area of Law: ERISA; insurance regulation; preemption

Questions Presented:

1. Whether California’s notice-prejudice rule is saved from ERISA preemption as a law regulating insurance and may govern a claim under an insured employee-benefit plan.

#### Judgment

Affirmed in part, reversed in part, and remanded, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

| Judgment component | Disposition and vote | Supporting | Opposing | Relief or remand |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Notice-prejudice rule | Affirmed, 9-0 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | No Justice | California’s notice-prejudice rule shall govern within Ward’s ERISA benefits action. |
| Categorical employer-as-agent rule | Reversed, 9-0 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | No Justice | The categorical agency rule may not be used, and actual prejudice remains for determination on remand. |

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Ginsburg | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Breyer | Supports affirmance in part, reversal in part, and remand | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm the notice-prejudice component, reverse the employer-as-agent component, and remand.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: Stone announces no ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: California’s notice-prejudice rule

Holding and operative rule: California’s notice-prejudice rule regulates insurance and is saved from ERISA preemption. It may supply the rule of decision in an action under ERISA § 502(a)(1)(B), requiring the insurer to establish actual prejudice before enforcing a late-notice forfeiture.

Reasoning: The rule is directed to insurance and alters the enforceability of a central policy term by requiring an insurer to demonstrate that delayed proof of claim caused actual harm. It therefore regulates the insurer-insured relationship rather than affecting insurance only incidentally. Ward invokes the rule within ERISA’s exclusive civil-enforcement action to determine whether benefits are due under the insured plan; he does not assert a separate state cause of action or obtain a remedy outside ERISA. The rule also does not conflict with ERISA’s claims provisions, which establish minimum procedural protections but do not require insurers to enforce every shorter contractual deadline without regard to prejudice. California may regulate an insured plan through a law that genuinely regulates insurance, while self-insured plans remain governed by the statutory deemer clause.

##### Question 2: Employer as the insurer’s categorical agent

Holding and operative rule: California may not categorically deem the employer-plan administrator the insurer’s agent for receipt of every claim notice. That rule relates directly to ERISA plan administration and is preempted.

Reasoning: The agency rule does not merely define the insurer’s substantive promise to the insured. It assigns the employer a mandatory administrative role, changes where a participant may submit notice, and imposes legal consequences on the plan administrator regardless of the parties’ undertaking. That command affects the structure and operation of an ERISA plan in the manner identified by simulated *New York State Conference of Blue Cross & Blue Shield Plans v. Travelers Insurance Co.* and *De Buono v. NYSA-ILA Medical and Clinical Services Fund*. The rule therefore falls within § 514(a) and is not preserved on the theory advanced here. Fact-specific actual authority, apparent authority, or voluntarily assumed agency remains open if established under otherwise applicable law. ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep526/usrep526358/usrep526358.pdf))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*New York State Conference of Blue Cross & Blue Shield Plans v. Travelers Insurance Co.*: applied; a state command assigning claim-administration responsibilities relates directly to an ERISA plan.

*De Buono v. NYSA-ILA Medical and Clinical Services Fund*: applied; generally applicable state law remains outside preemption unless its operation regulates plan benefits, administration, enforcement, or their practical equivalent.

*Metropolitan Life Insurance Co. v. Massachusetts* and *Pilot Life Insurance Co. v. Dedeaux*: clarified; a rule governing enforcement of an insurance-policy deadline may be saved when it regulates insurance and operates within ERISA’s exclusive remedial action.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit is affirmed as to the notice-prejudice rule, reversed as to the categorical employer-as-agent rule, and the case is remanded for determination whether UNUM suffered actual prejudice from Ward’s delayed notice.

Effect: UNUM may not deny the claim solely because notice was late unless it proves actual prejudice. Ward receives no independent state remedy, and he may not rely on a categorical rule automatically treating his employer as UNUM’s notice agent.

### United States v. Sun-Diamond Growers of California

Argued March 2, 1999 | Decided April 27, 1999  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the District of Columbia Circuit; judgment below: gratuity conviction reversed because the jury was not required to connect the gifts to a particular official act, 138 F.3d 961 (D.C. Cir. 1998). ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep526/usrep526398/usrep526398.pdf))  
Area of Law: Federal criminal law; public corruption; illegal gratuities

Questions Presented:

1. Whether 18 U.S.C. § 201(c)(1)(A) criminalizes giving a public official something of value solely because of the official’s position, without a link to a specific official act.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Scalia | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: Stone announces no ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Specific official act required

Holding and operative rule: Section 201(c)(1)(A) requires proof that the thing of value was given for or because of a specific official act. The Government need not prove the corrupt agreement required for bribery, but generalized goodwill toward an officeholder or a gift given solely because of official status is insufficient.

Reasoning: The statute does not prohibit every gift to a federal official. It prohibits giving value for or because of an official act, a defined term referring to a concrete question, matter, decision, or action within the official’s duties. Reading the relational language to mean merely because of public office would erase the words Congress chose, collapse the distinction between unlawful gratuities and general ethical prohibitions, and expose routine ceremonial or status-related gifts to criminal punishment. The gratuity provision differs from bribery because it requires no corrupt exchange or agreement and may reach a reward given after an act is completed. It nevertheless requires the jury to identify the official act to which the gift relates. The instruction below allowed conviction without that finding and therefore could not sustain the verdict. ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep526/usrep526398/usrep526398.pdf))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

18 U.S.C. § 201(c)(1)(A): clarified; an illegal gratuity requires a link to a specific official act but not a corrupt quid pro quo.

18 U.S.C. § 201(a)(3): applied; the statutory definition of official act supplies the concrete governmental matter to which the gift must relate.

Rule of lenity: declined to extend; the statute’s text and structure resolve the status-based theory without reliance on lenity.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the District of Columbia Circuit is affirmed, and the gratuity conviction remains vacated, with any further proceedings governed by an instruction requiring a connection between each charged gift and a specific official act.

Effect: Sun-Diamond may be retried if otherwise lawful on an indictment and evidence satisfying the specific-official-act requirement. The decision does not authorize gifts forbidden by another criminal statute, ethics rule, campaign-finance law, or administrative prohibition.

### Russell v. Burris

Argued February 22, 1999 | Decided April 28, 1999  
On writs of certiorari in companion Nos. 98-397 and 98-399 to the United States Court of Appeals for the Eighth Circuit; judgment below: Arkansas’s $100 and $300 candidate-contribution limits invalidated, with additional campaign-finance provisions resolved outside the grants, 146 F.3d 563 (8th Cir. 1998). ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/146/563/513916/))  
Area of Law: First Amendment; campaign finance; contribution limits

Questions Presented:

1. Whether candidate-contribution limits of $100 and $300 per election are so low that they materially impair protected political association and a candidate’s ability to amass sufficient resources for effective advocacy, notwithstanding the State’s anticorruption interest.
2. What showing of actual or apparent corruption and constitutional fit must the government make when defending contribution limits dramatically below the limits previously sustained by the Court.

#### Judgment

Affirmed in part, reversed in part, and remanded; votes vary by component.

| Judgment component | Disposition and vote | Supporting | Opposing | Relief or remand |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| $300 limits for specified statewide offices | Affirmed, 7-2 | Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Breyer | Stevens, Ginsburg | The injunction against the statewide limits remains in force. |
| $100 limits for judicial offices | Affirmed, 7-2 | Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Breyer | Stevens, Ginsburg | The injunction against the judicial limits remains in force. |
| $100 limits for remaining nonstatewide nonjudicial offices | Reversed, 6-3 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | The injunction shall be dissolved as to these offices. |

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Stone, Souter, Breyer in full; Stevens and Ginsburg as to the governing standard and the remaining nonstatewide nonjudicial limits; Kennedy as to the statewide and judicial limits | Supports the component dispositions stated above | Scope stated by issue |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Stevens | Ginsburg | Concurs in part and dissents in part | Concurs in the governing standard and the nonstatewide nonjudicial judgment; dissents from invalidation of the statewide and judicial limits |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Scalia | Kennedy, Thomas | Concurs in part and dissents in part | Concurs in invalidation of the statewide and judicial limits; dissents from sustaining the remaining $100 limits |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm invalidation of the $300 statewide and $100 judicial limits, reverse invalidation of the remaining $100 nonstatewide nonjudicial limits, and remand.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: Stone announces no ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1A: Remaining $100 nonstatewide nonjudicial limits

Holding and operative rule: The $100 per-election limits for the remaining nonstatewide nonjudicial offices are constitutional on this record because they preserve meaningful contributor participation and do not materially prevent candidates from assembling resources adequate for those campaigns.

Reasoning: A nominal comparison with the $1,000 ceiling sustained in *Buckley v. Valeo* does not itself resolve constitutionality. Campaign scale, constituency, customary receipts, fundraising patterns, and related restrictions determine the actual burden. The bench-trial record showed that ordinary legislative and comparable local campaigns continued to obtain sufficient resources under the $100 ceiling and that contributions materially above that amount could constitute a substantial portion of the receipts in those races. Arkansas therefore could conclude that the limit reduced the risk of actual or apparent candidate indebtedness without eliminating meaningful association or disabling effective advocacy. The Court adopts no universal minimum contribution amount and does not hold that every $100 ceiling is valid.

##### Question 1B: Statewide and judicial limits

Holding and operative rule: The $300 limits for specified statewide offices and the $100 limits for judicial offices are unconstitutional on the present record because their practical operation materially impairs effective advocacy and is not closely connected to the influence risk Arkansas identified. (joined by Stone, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, and Breyer).

Reasoning: The statewide campaigns in the record approached approximately $900,000, yet Arkansas did not establish why a contribution modest in relation to that scale created the asserted quid-pro-quo risk or why a $300 ceiling was needed to address it. Judicial campaigns faced a different defect: the $100 ceiling operated alongside larger constituencies and restrictions limiting direct judicial solicitation, producing a materially greater fundraising burden than the same amount imposed in ordinary small-office races. Closely drawn review requires attention to those operational differences. The Court does not invalidate the provisions because they fall below an inflation-adjusted version of *Buckley*; it invalidates them because the record does not connect their burdens to the State’s justification with the required closeness.

##### Question 2: Anticorruption predicate and constitutional fit

Holding and operative rule: A State defending a candidate-contribution limit need not prove completed bribery, a changed official vote, concealment, or formal legislative findings. It must identify an objectively grounded risk of actual or apparent quid-pro-quo corruption or circumvention and connect the selected ceiling closely to that risk without materially disabling meaningful association or effective campaign advocacy. (joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer).

Reasoning: Contribution limits regulate the size of financial support rather than suppressing the contributor’s underlying political view, but they still burden association and the candidate’s ability to finance communication. The governmental interest is prevention of actual or apparent quid-pro-quo influence and circumvention, not equalization of political voice or generalized suspicion of money in politics. The evidentiary basis may include campaign scale, contribution patterns, office responsibilities, enforcement experience, and reasonable legislative judgment. No one form of proof is indispensable. The fit inquiry remains practical: the amount must address the identified influence risk while leaving contributors a meaningful avenue of support and candidates a workable opportunity to fund advocacy.

#### Concurrences

Stevens, joined by Ginsburg: The State’s anticorruption judgment should receive greater deference, and the record does not establish that the statewide or judicial limits disable effective campaigning. They would affirm the governing standard and the validity of the remaining $100 limits, but reverse the Eighth Circuit as to all of the challenged ceilings.

#### Dissents

Scalia, joined by Kennedy and Thomas: Direct contribution limits suppress political association and should receive stricter constitutional review than the Court applies. They agree that the statewide and judicial limits are invalid but would also affirm the Eighth Circuit’s invalidation of the remaining $100 limits.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Buckley v. Valeo*: clarified; closely drawn review establishes no universal dollar floor or automatic inflation formula and requires examination of the limit’s practical operation and anticorruption fit.

*Colorado Republican Federal Campaign Committee v. Federal Election Commission*: distinguished; regulation of independent party expenditures differs from limits on direct candidate contributions, although both require attention to circumvention and practical burdens.

*Buckley v. American Constitutional Law Foundation, Inc.*, decided January 12, 1999: applied by analogy; a rule’s practical reduction of available political participation matters, but direct contribution limits remain governed by the distinct *Buckley v. Valeo* framework.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Eighth Circuit is affirmed as to the $300 statewide and $100 judicial limits, reversed as to the $100 limits for the remaining nonstatewide nonjudicial offices, and the case is remanded with instructions to modify the injunction accordingly.

Effect: Arkansas may enforce the $100 contribution limits for the remaining nonstatewide nonjudicial offices. It may not enforce the $300 statewide or $100 judicial limits on this record. No judgment is entered concerning PAC provisions, small-donor classifications, independent-expenditure committees, or severability questions outside the grants.
