### INS v. Aguirre-Aguirre

Argued March 3, 1999 | Decided May 3, 1999  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit; judgment below: Board of Immigration Appeals’ application of the serious-nonpolitical-crime bar rejected and further relief ordered, 121 F.3d 521 (9th Cir. 1997). ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep526/usrep526415/usrep526415.pdf))  
Area of Law: Immigration; administrative law; withholding of deportation

Questions Presented:

1. What deference is owed to the Board of Immigration Appeals’ interpretation of the serious-nonpolitical-crime exception to withholding of deportation, and whether the Ninth Circuit applied the correct inquiry.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Kennedy | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: Stone announces no ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Review of the serious-nonpolitical-crime bar

Holding and operative rule: A reviewing court must identify and enforce any fixed statutory boundary, then review the Board’s interpretation of the serious-nonpolitical-crime bar under the governing deference framework. It may not replace a permissible agency construction with mandatory factors the statute does not require, but the Board remains subject to review for statutory error, arbitrary reasoning, and inadequate record support.

Reasoning: The statutory phrase leaves evaluative judgment concerning whether a crime’s violence, objectives, proportionality, and political connection make it serious and nonpolitical. Congress assigned administration of the immigration laws to the Attorney General, whose authority is exercised in adjudication by the Board. The Ninth Circuit therefore was required to examine the interpretation the Board actually adopted and determine whether it preserved the statutory boundary and represented a permissible, reasoned construction. International guidance may assist interpretation, but it does not become a set of binding domestic elements merely because a court finds it persuasive. Simulated *MCI Telecommunications Corp. v. AT&T* and *National Credit Union Administration v. First National Bank & Trust Co.* still require courts to prevent an agency from erasing enacted limits. They do not authorize courts to displace lawful agency judgment with an independently preferred factor test. ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep526/usrep526415/usrep526415.pdf))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Chevron U.S.A. Inc. v. Natural Resources Defense Council, Inc.*: applied; the Board receives deference when the statute leaves evaluative space and the Board adopts a permissible construction.

*MCI Telecommunications Corp. v. AT&T* and *National Credit Union Administration v. First National Bank & Trust Co.*: applied; deference follows identification and enforcement of the statutory boundary, not its elimination.

*INS v. Cardoza-Fonseca*: clarified; courts independently enforce fixed statutory meaning but defer to lawful agency resolution of ambiguity.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit is reversed, and the case is remanded for review of the Board’s actual decision under the governing statutory, deference, reasoned-decisionmaking, and record-support requirements.

Effect: The Ninth Circuit may not impose extra mandatory factors absent a statutory basis. It may address a properly preserved defect in the Board’s construction, explanation, or evidentiary support. The Court makes no final determination whether Aguirre-Aguirre is barred from withholding or eligible for discretionary asylum.

---

### Bank of America National Trust & Savings Ass’n v. 203 North LaSalle Street Partnership

Argued November 2, 1998 | Decided May 3, 1999  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Seventh Circuit; judgment below: Chapter 11 plan confirmed under a new-value theory despite old equity’s exclusive opportunity to obtain the reorganized debtor, 126 F.3d 955 (7th Cir. 1997). ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/526/434/))  
Area of Law: Bankruptcy; absolute priority; new value

Questions Presented:

1. Whether a Chapter 11 plan violates the absolute-priority rule when old equity receives the exclusive opportunity to contribute new capital and obtain the reorganized debtor over the objection of an impaired senior class.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 8-1.  
Supporting: Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: Stevens.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Souter | Stone, O’Connor, Kennedy, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Thomas | Scalia | Concurs in the judgment | Textual rejection of confirmation without broader new-value analysis |
| Dissent | Stevens | No other Justice | Would affirm | Adequate new-value contribution and retained exclusivity |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: Stone announces no ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Old equity’s exclusive new-value opportunity

Holding and operative rule: A plan violates the absolute-priority rule when old equity, over an impaired senior class’s objection, receives an exclusive opportunity to contribute new capital and obtain ownership because of its former position, without market exposure demonstrating that the opportunity carries no residual value. (joined by Stone, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer).

Reasoning: Section 1129(b)(2)(B)(ii) prevents a junior interest holder from receiving or retaining property on account of its prior interest when a dissenting senior class is not paid in full. The former partners received more than the reorganized ownership itself. During exclusivity, they alone could propose the contribution and acquire that ownership, while creditors and outsiders were barred from offering more. Their old equity position was therefore the source of a valuable opportunity. The Court need not decide whether every new-value plan is forbidden. It is enough that an insider-only opportunity, insulated from competition and untested by the market, cannot demonstrate that old equity receives nothing on account of its former status. Termination of exclusivity, competitive bidding, competing plans, or another genuine market process may establish the value of a materially different proposal. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/526/434/))

#### Concurrences

Thomas, joined by Scalia: The plan cannot be confirmed under the enacted text because the dissenting senior class was not paid in full and old equity obtained ownership through the plan. They concur only in the judgment and would not speculate about a general new-value corollary or prescribe market-testing principles beyond the statutory defect presented.

#### Dissents

Stevens: A substantial, necessary, and reasonably equivalent new-capital contribution is not property received on account of the old interest. He would uphold confirmation rather than require market exposure as an additional statutory condition. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/526/434/))

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Case v. Los Angeles Lumber Products Co.*: clarified; the Court neither recognizes nor rejects every new-value plan, but an exclusive insider opportunity without market testing fails the absolute-priority rule.

*Norwest Bank Worthington v. Ahlers*: followed; promises or contributions that do not satisfy the enacted priority structure cannot support junior retention over a dissenting senior class.

11 U.S.C. §§ 1121 and 1129(b)(2)(B)(ii): clarified; plan exclusivity may itself make the opportunity received by old equity property obtained on account of the prior interest.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Seventh Circuit is reversed, the confirmation order is vacated, and the case is remanded for consideration of any plan proposed after termination of exclusivity, competitive bidding, or another lawful process testing the value of old equity’s contribution and opportunity.

Effect: The former partners may not obtain the reorganized partnership through the exclusive arrangement confirmed below. The decision does not categorically prohibit every new-value plan or prescribe one mandatory auction format.

---

### El Paso Natural Gas Co. v. Neztsosie

Argued March 2, 1999 | Decided May 3, 1999  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit; judgment below: tribal-court exhaustion required before federal adjudication of asserted Price-Anderson public-liability claims and injunctive relief modified, 136 F.3d 610 (9th Cir. 1998). ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep526/usrep526473/usrep526473.pdf))  
Area of Law: Federal courts; tribal courts; Price-Anderson Act

Questions Presented:

1. Whether tribal-court exhaustion is required for nuclear-tort claims that Congress made removable to federal court under the Price-Anderson Act, and whether the federal court may enjoin further tribal proceedings.

#### Judgment

Vacated and remanded, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Souter | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports vacatur and remand | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to vacate and remand.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: Stone announces no ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Cross-appeal limitation

Holding and operative rule: An appellee may defend the existing decree on any properly preserved ground, but may not obtain an enlargement of its rights or a reduction of the appellant’s rights without filing a cross-appeal.

Reasoning: Respondents did not cross-appeal the district court’s partial injunctions, yet the Court of Appeals altered those orders for their benefit. The ordinary cross-appeal rule separates alternative grounds for preserving a judgment from requests for greater relief. An appellee may urge any basis supporting the decree actually entered, but a court may not award a more favorable decree when the appellee did not invoke the appellate process required to obtain it. The unauthorized enlargement must therefore be vacated independently of the Price-Anderson and tribal-exhaustion questions.

##### Question 2: Price-Anderson coverage and tribal exhaustion

Holding and operative rule: A federal court must determine Price-Anderson coverage before requiring prudential tribal-court exhaustion. Claims qualifying as public-liability actions proceed through the federal cause, removal right, and consolidation channel Congress prescribed; claims outside the Act remain subject to ordinary jurisdictional and tribal-law principles.

Reasoning: *National Farmers Union Insurance Cos. v. Crow Tribe* and *Iowa Mutual Insurance Co. v. LaPlante* establish prudential respect for tribal courts, not a jurisdictional command immune from later federal legislation. The Price-Anderson Act creates a federal public-liability action, authorizes removal, and provides mechanisms for consolidating nuclear-incident litigation in a federal forum. Requiring covered claims to proceed first through tribal adjudication would create duplicative determinations and delay the federal channel Congress specifically designed. Coverage must therefore be resolved in federal court. The decision does not assume that every pleaded injury is a covered public-liability claim. If a claim falls outside Price-Anderson, the ordinary tribal-jurisdiction and exhaustion rules retain their force. ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep526/usrep526473/usrep526473.pdf))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*National Farmers Union Insurance Cos. v. Crow Tribe* and *Iowa Mutual Insurance Co. v. LaPlante*: declined to extend; prudential tribal exhaustion does not displace the specific federal channel Congress enacted for covered Price-Anderson claims.

*American Railway Express Co. v. Levee*: followed; an appellee may defend the decree but may not enlarge its rights without a cross-appeal.

Price-Anderson Act: clarified; federal courts determine coverage first, and covered public-liability claims proceed through the statutory federal forum.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit is vacated, and the case is remanded with instructions that the district court determine Price-Anderson coverage first, channel covered claims into the prescribed federal proceeding, and refrain from enlarging respondents’ relief absent a cross-appeal.

Effect: Covered nuclear public-liability claims need not complete tribal-court proceedings before federal adjudication. Uncovered claims remain governed by ordinary tribal and federal jurisdictional principles, and any injunction must be confined to the scope necessary to protect the statutory federal channel.

### Redmon v. United States

Argued March 23, 1999 | Decided May 10, 1999  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Seventh Circuit; judgment below: denial of suppression and conditional guilty-plea judgment affirmed en banc, 138 F.3d 1109 (7th Cir. 1998). ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/138/1109/473816/))  
Area of Law: Fourth Amendment; curtilage; garbage searches

Questions Presented:

1. Whether *California v. Greenwood*’s rule governing garbage exposed for collection extends to garbage kept in closed containers immediately beside an attached garage within, or at the boundary of, the home’s curtilage.
2. Whether an intent eventually to discard the garbage, or its accessibility to collectors or other private persons, eliminates Fourth Amendment protection when police must enter or exploit constitutionally protected residential space to inspect it.

#### Judgment

Vacated and remanded, 5-4.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Souter | Stone, Stevens, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports vacatur and remand | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | O’Connor | Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | Would affirm | Collection exposure and shared-driveway access |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to vacate and remand.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: Stone announces no ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Reach of Greenwood at the home’s boundary

Holding and operative rule: *California v. Greenwood* does not categorically authorize warrantless inspection of closed garbage containers located within, or at a disputed boundary of, a home’s curtilage. A court must first determine whether police entered or exploited constitutionally protected residential space to obtain access.

Reasoning: *Greenwood* concerned refuse left for collection outside the curtilage in a location exposed to ordinary public access. Its reasoning does not make the contents of every garbage container unprotected at the instant the owner intends future collection. Redmon placed closed containers immediately beside an attached garage because local rules did not permit ordinary curbside placement. The common-driveway configuration, proximity to the dwelling, use of the area, collection practice, and steps separating it from public access bear on curtilage and exposure under *United States v. Dunn*. The en banc judgment treated collection intent and practical accessibility as sufficient without resolving the protected-space question. Because the record lacks a controlling factual determination whether the containers or police route lay within curtilage or another protected residential area, appellate declaration of that fact in either direction would be improper. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/138/1109/473816/))

##### Question 2: Future discard intent and limited private access

Holding and operative rule: An intent eventually to surrender garbage to an authorized collector and limited practical access by collectors or other private persons do not eliminate Fourth Amendment protection when police obtain the contents by entering or exploiting curtilage or another protected residential area. If no protected space is used and the refuse is genuinely exposed for collection in the manner governed by *Greenwood*, no warrant is required.

Reasoning: A homeowner’s future transfer of garbage to a collector does not necessarily abandon all constitutional protection before the authorized collection occurs. Nor does the possibility that a neighbor, animal, trespasser, or collector could reach an object grant police a general license to enter protected residential space. The inquiry concerns the location and exposure of the containers and the means by which officers actually obtained them. At the same time, the Fourth Amendment does not create continuing privacy in refuse placed outside residential protection and made available to ordinary public access. The lower courts must classify Redmon’s setting under that boundary, then address the warrant’s dependence on the garbage evidence and any independent-source, attenuation, suppression, harmlessness, or conditional-plea consequence.

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

O’Connor, joined by Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas: Redmon deliberately placed the containers for collection on an open shared driveway used by visitors, delivery personnel, neighbors, and collectors, thereby exposing them beyond the privacy protected in *Greenwood*. They would affirm without making formal curtilage classification dispositive when the resident has objectively presented the refuse for removal.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*California v. Greenwood*: clarified and declined categorical extension; its no-privacy rule governs refuse exposed for collection outside protected residential space, not every container intended for future disposal regardless of location.

*United States v. Dunn*: applied; proximity, enclosure, use, and protection from observation or access govern the curtilage inquiry.

Fourth Amendment abandonment doctrine: clarified; future relinquishment intent does not alone authorize police acquisition through protected residential space.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Seventh Circuit is vacated, and the case is remanded for determination whether the containers were within curtilage or another protected residential space, whether officers entered or exploited that space, and what ordinary warrant-fruit and plea consequences follow from that determination.

Effect: If the police used protected residential space, the lower courts must address suppression, causation, independent source, attenuation, and the validity of the conditional plea without presuming the result. If the containers and police access were outside such protection and the garbage was exposed as in *Greenwood*, the judgment may be reinstated.

---

### Saenz v. Roe

Argued January 13, 1999 | Decided May 17, 1999  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit; judgment below: preliminary injunction against California’s first-year welfare reduction affirmed, 134 F.3d 1400 (9th Cir. 1998). ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep526/usrep526489/usrep526489.pdf))  
Area of Law: Fourteenth Amendment; right to travel; state citizenship

Questions Presented:

1. Whether California may limit welfare benefits for residents during their first year to the amount they would have received in their prior State.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 8-1.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stevens | Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Thomas | No other Justice | Would reverse | Privileges or Immunities and durational-benefit classification |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: Stone announces no ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Equal state citizenship after migration

Holding and operative rule: A United States citizen who becomes a bona fide resident of a new State is entitled to the same generally available benefits as other similarly situated citizens of that State. A State may not reduce first-year welfare benefits solely because of recent arrival or the benefit level in the citizen’s former State.

Reasoning: The right to interstate travel includes the right to enter another State, to be treated as a welcome temporary visitor, and, upon establishing bona fide residence, to receive equal treatment as a citizen of the new State. The third component is secured by the Fourteenth Amendment’s Privileges or Immunities Clause, which makes citizenship of the State follow bona fide residence and does not permit inferior first-year citizenship. California’s rule applies to persons who are already current residents and otherwise satisfy the State’s ordinary eligibility requirements. It nevertheless assigns benefits by length of residence and former State, creating multiple subclasses of equally needy California citizens. Fiscal savings and prior tax contributions do not justify withholding generally available state benefits from new citizens. Bona fide residence verification and equal prospective eligibility rules remain permissible. ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep526/usrep526489/usrep526489.pdf))

##### Question 2: Congressional authorization

Holding and operative rule: Congress may structure and condition a federal welfare program, but it may not authorize a State to violate the Fourteenth Amendment’s guarantee of equal state citizenship.

Reasoning: The federal welfare legislation permitted States to use a former State’s benefit level during a resident’s first year, but statutory permission cannot lower the constitutional floor governing state action. Congress’s Article I authority remains subject to the specific restrictions of the Constitution, and the Fourteenth Amendment limits both federal and state efforts to create inferior classes of state citizenship. Because California’s classification violates that guarantee, congressional authorization does not validate it. The Court does not decide whether Congress could adopt a materially different nationwide benefit rule administered on uniform federal terms. ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep526/usrep526489/usrep526489.pdf))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

Thomas: The Privileges or Immunities Clause should not be used to constitutionalize equality among state welfare classifications, and California’s temporary rule should be reviewed as a fiscal residence measure under the received equal-protection and travel precedents. He would reverse the preliminary injunction.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Shapiro v. Thompson*, *Dunn v. Blumstein*, and *Zobel v. Williams*: followed and clarified; a State may not make current residents’ access to generally available benefits depend on recent arrival or prior residence.

*Slaughter-House Cases*: applied at its preserved scope; the Privileges or Immunities Clause protects a citizen’s right to be treated equally as a citizen of the State of bona fide residence.

Congressional authorization doctrine: reaffirmed; Congress may not authorize state conduct that violates the Fourteenth Amendment.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit is affirmed, and the preliminary injunction against California’s first-year lower-benefit rule remains in force.

Effect: California may not reduce otherwise available welfare benefits for bona fide residents solely because they arrived within the preceding year or previously lived in a lower-benefit State. It may verify bona fide residence and enforce equal prospective eligibility requirements applicable to new and established residents alike.

---

**Sources:**

- [U.S. Reports: Wyoming v. Houghton, 526 U.S. 295 (1999).](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep526/usrep526295/usrep526295.pdf)
- [https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/148/1073/510615/](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/148/1073/510615/)
- [https://www.law.cornell.edu/supct/html/98-93.ZD.html](https://www.law.cornell.edu/supct/html/98-93.ZD.html)
- [Bank of America Nat. Trust and Sav. Assn. v. 203 North LaSalle Street Partnership | 526 U.S. 434 (1999) | Justia U.S. Supreme Court Center](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/526/434/)

| Date | Case or matter | Event |

| May 17, 1999 | Clinton v. Goldsmith | Decision |

Chronology correction: The four May 17 decisions are inserted alongside *Saenz v. Roe*, already rendered in Chunk 4. Under the Engine's rule for uncoordinated same-date decisions, all five use the pre-May 17 entering-law baseline, none influences another, and their combined legal effect operates prospectively after that date. The five May 24 decisions likewise use one common pre-May 24 baseline.

Source corrections: The controlled brief incorrectly states that Goldsmith's court-martial sentence included dismissal; the official record identifies six years of confinement and partial forfeiture of pay. The official reports also record *Florida v. White* as reversed and remanded and identify a Souter concurrence joined by Breyer; identify Breyer, not Ginsburg, as the author in *Cleveland*; record a unanimous Fourth Amendment holding but an 8-1 historical qualified-immunity judgment in *Wilson*; and record *Richardson* as vacated and remanded, with Ginsburg joining Kennedy's historical dissent. These corrections concern the public record and historical comparators only. They do not alter Stone's approved Section II or create an adjudicative blocker. `OT_1998CHUNK5.md` ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep526/usrep526529/usrep526529.pdf))

## Decisions and Dispositions

### Clinton v. Goldsmith

Argued March 22, 1999 | Decided May 17, 1999  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Armed Forces; judgment below: injunction entered under the All Writs Act against the President and military officials to prevent Goldsmith's administrative removal from the Air Force rolls, 48 M.J. 84 (C.A.A.F. 1998). ([tile.loc.gov](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep526/usrep526529/usrep526529.pdf?utm_source=chatgpt.com))  
Area of Law: Military justice; jurisdiction; All Writs Act

Questions Presented:

1. Whether the Court of Appeals for the Armed Forces may use the All Writs Act to enjoin the President and military officials from administratively dropping a servicemember from the rolls when that action is not part of the court-martial judgment under review.

#### Judgment

Reversed, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Souter | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports reversal | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: Stone announces no ground beyond the Court's opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: All Writs authority of the military appellate court

Holding and operative rule: The All Writs Act permits a military appellate court to protect only jurisdiction independently conferred over court-martial findings and sentences. It does not authorize an injunction against a separate administrative personnel action outside that statutory review.

Reasoning: The Court of Appeals for the Armed Forces possesses the review authority Congress assigned over specified court-martial findings and sentences. The Air Force's proposed action to drop Goldsmith from the rolls was an executive personnel action authorized by a separate statute, was not imposed by the court-martial, and did not revise the final findings or sentence. The All Writs Act can preserve or effectuate jurisdiction that already exists, but cannot turn a court of limited review into a general supervisor of military administration. The constitutional importance of Goldsmith's objections cannot supply the missing jurisdiction. Administrative review through the military correction process and judicial review in a court possessing independent statutory authority remain available, so extraordinary relief was neither jurisdictionally auxiliary nor necessary as a substitute route. ([tile.loc.gov](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep526/usrep526529/usrep526529.pdf?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Judicial Council v. McBryde*: followed; extraordinary writs may run only in aid of supervisory or appellate jurisdiction independently conferred on the issuing court.

*Pennsylvania Bureau of Correction v. United States Marshals Service*: reaffirmed; the All Writs Act is not an independent grant of jurisdiction.

*Noyd v. Bond*: distinguished; military appellate courts may issue extraordinary process within their lawful review authority, not over a separate executive personnel action.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Armed Forces is reversed, and its injunction against dropping Goldsmith from the Air Force rolls is vacated and dissolved.

Effect: The administrative process may continue. Goldsmith remains free to pursue any independently available administrative or Article III route challenging the removal statute or its application, and the Court expresses no view on the merits of those claims.

---

### Hunt v. Cromartie

Argued January 20, 1999 | Decided May 17, 1999  
On appeal from the United States District Court for the Eastern District of North Carolina, convened as a three-judge court; judgment below: summary judgment entered invalidating North Carolina's Twelfth Congressional District and enjoining elections under the plan, 34 F. Supp. 2d 1029 (E.D.N.C. 1998). ([tile.loc.gov](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep526/usrep526541/usrep526541.pdf?utm_source=chatgpt.com))  
Area of Law: Equal protection; elections; racial predominance in districting

Questions Presented:

1. Whether summary judgment was proper on the claim that race predominated in drawing North Carolina's Twelfth Congressional District when the State offered evidence that partisan affiliation, not race as such, explained the district's design.

#### Judgment

Vacated and remanded, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Thomas | Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy | Supports vacatur and remand | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Stevens | Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Concurs in the judgment | Disputed race-and-party inference and need for developed factfinding |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to vacate and remand.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: Stone announces no ground beyond the Court's opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Summary judgment on racial predominance

Holding and operative rule: When supported evidence permits both a racial and a political explanation for challenged districting choices, racial predominance may not be found against the nonmoving State on summary judgment unless no reasonable factfinder could accept the political account. The predominance question must return for factfinding.

Reasoning: Simulated *Miller v. Johnson / Abrams v. Johnson / United States v. Johnson* makes predominance a district-specific inquiry into whether race materially subordinated traditional race-neutral districting principles. The challengers offered the district's shape, demographic concentrations, boundary choices, and use of racial data. The State answered with legislative affidavits and an expert analysis of all boundary segments asserting that electoral performance and partisan goals explained the lines at least as well as race. Because race and party were highly correlated, the same evidence could support competing inferences about motive and the treatment of traditional criteria. Rule 56 required the district court to credit the State's supported account and draw reasonable inferences in its favor. The Court decides only that the record could not be resolved against the State without trial. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep526/usrep526541/usrep526541.pdf))

#### Concurrences

Stevens, joined by Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer: The district's irregular configuration establishes neither a racial nor a political explanation by itself, and registration data cannot be treated as a complete substitute for evidence of actual electoral behavior. They agree that the present record requires ordinary factfinding rather than summary judgment.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Miller v. Johnson / Abrams v. Johnson / United States v. Johnson*: applied; predominance remains a factual inquiry into whether race materially subordinated traditional race-neutral districting principles.

*Abrams v. Johnson*: reaffirmed; racial awareness and correlation between race and political preference do not alone establish racial predominance.

Federal Rule of Civil Procedure 56: clarified; supported racial and political explanations for correlated districting evidence may not be weighed against the nonmovant at summary judgment.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The summary judgment and injunction are vacated, and the case is remanded for trial or other proceedings that determine racial predominance on a developed record.

Effect: North Carolina has not established that District 12 is constitutional, and the challengers have not established that race predominated. No ruling is entered on the scrutiny applicable if predominance is proved or on the district's ultimate validity.

### Florida v. White

Argued March 23, 1999 | Decided May 17, 1999  
On writ of certiorari to the Supreme Court of Florida; judgment below: warrantless seizure of White's automobile for civil forfeiture held unconstitutional and the case remanded after suppression of the inventory-search evidence, 710 So. 2d 949 (Fla. 1998). ([tile.loc.gov](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep526/usrep526559/usrep526559.pdf?utm_source=chatgpt.com))  
Area of Law: Fourth Amendment; automobile seizures; civil forfeiture

Questions Presented:

1. Whether probable cause that an automobile was previously used to facilitate drug transactions permits police to seize it without a warrant from a public workplace parking lot for civil forfeiture.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 6-3.  
Supporting: O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Breyer. Opposing: Stone, Stevens, Ginsburg.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Thomas | O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence | Souter | Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Public-place seizure of the vehicle itself as forfeitable property |
| Dissent | Stevens | Stone, Ginsburg | Would affirm | Planned forfeiture seizure without a warrant or concrete exigency |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.  
Public role: joins Stevens's dissent in full.  
Announced ground: Probable cause supplied a basis to seek a warrant but did not excuse prior judicial authorization for a planned forfeiture seizure conducted months after the suspected transactions, while White was under arrest and no concrete mobility or evidence-loss risk was shown.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Warrantless seizure of a forfeitable automobile

Holding and operative rule: The Fourth Amendment does not require police to obtain a warrant before seizing an automobile from a public place when they have probable cause to believe the vehicle itself is forfeitable contraband under governing law.

Reasoning: The automobile was parked in an area open to public access, remained readily movable, and was itself the property the officers had probable cause to believe was subject to forfeiture because of its use in drug deliveries. Fourth Amendment doctrine has long distinguished police entry into a home or other private place from seizure of a vehicle exposed in public, and it accords police greater latitude over automobiles because mobility can defeat enforcement before a warrant is obtained. The seizure did not depend on probable cause that drugs remained inside the car; it depended on probable cause that the car itself had become forfeitable under state law. The subsequent inventory did not retroactively justify the seizure, but the lawful public-place seizure supplied the predicate for the inventory process. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep526/usrep526559/usrep526559.pdf))

#### Concurrences

Souter, joined by Breyer: The judgment is confined to a vehicle seized from a public place on probable cause that the vehicle itself was forfeitable. It does not authorize entry onto private premises, eliminate ordinary limits on inventory searches, or create a general warrant exception for planned seizures of movable personal property.

#### Dissents

Stevens, joined by Stone and Ginsburg: Automobile mobility should not become a standing license for a planned civil-forfeiture seizure when officers had months to obtain process, arrested the owner, and identified no immediate risk that the parked car would be removed. They would require a warrant and preserve suppression of the direct fruits.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*United States v. James Daniel Good Real Property*: distinguished; its pre-seizure process rule for an immovable home does not govern an automobile seized from a public place.

*G. M. Leasing Corp. v. United States*: followed; officers may seize an automobile from a public place without a warrant when probable cause establishes the Government's lawful claim to the vehicle itself.

*Carroll v. United States*: extended; the mobility rationale applies when the automobile, rather than only its contents, is the object of lawful forfeiture probable cause.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Supreme Court of Florida is reversed, and the case is remanded for further proceedings without suppressing the inventory evidence on the ground that a warrant was required for the public-place forfeiture seizure.

Effect: Florida may rely on the seized evidence subject to any independently preserved challenge to probable cause, the forfeiture statute, the inventory's administration, or another distinct police act. The decision does not govern seizures from a home or other private place.

### Ruhrgas AG v. Marathon Oil Co.

Argued March 22, 1999 | Decided May 17, 1999  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Fifth Circuit; judgment below: personal-jurisdiction dismissal vacated and subject-matter jurisdiction made categorically antecedent in removed cases, 145 F.3d 211 (5th Cir. 1998) (en banc). ([tile.loc.gov](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep526/usrep526574/usrep526574.pdf?utm_source=chatgpt.com))  
Area of Law: Federal courts; jurisdictional sequencing; removal

Questions Presented:

1. Whether a federal court must resolve subject-matter jurisdiction before dismissing for lack of personal jurisdiction in every removed case.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Ginsburg | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: Stone announces no ground beyond the Court's opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Sequence among nonmerits jurisdictional grounds

Holding and operative rule: A federal court may dismiss for lack of personal jurisdiction before resolving subject-matter jurisdiction when the personal-jurisdiction issue is straightforward, the subject-matter issue is difficult, and the court reaches no merits question.

Reasoning: *Steel Co. v. Citizens for a Better Environment* forbids a federal court to assume Article III power in order to decide the merits. It does not establish a rigid hierarchy among independent threshold grounds that each prevents merits adjudication. A personal-jurisdiction dismissal determines only the court's authority to bind the particular defendant and creates no substantive ruling on liability. Here, the removed action presented difficult questions concerning diversity, federal-question jurisdiction, and an international arbitration statute, while the foreign defendants' lack of Texas contacts furnished a discrete threshold ground. Judicial economy, restraint, and sensitivity to the foreign parties all permitted the district court to decide personal jurisdiction first. A different order may be appropriate when subject-matter jurisdiction is simple or the personal-jurisdiction inquiry is burdensome. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep526/usrep526574/usrep526574.pdf))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Steel Co. v. Citizens for a Better Environment*: clarified; jurisdiction must precede the merits, but no mandatory hierarchy governs independent nonmerits jurisdictional dismissals.

*Gulf Oil Corp. v. Gilbert*: applied; threshold sequencing may account for judicial restraint, convenience, and the character of the competing jurisdictional questions.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Fifth Circuit is reversed, and the case is remanded for review of the personal-jurisdiction dismissal without requiring prior resolution of the difficult subject-matter questions.

Effect: The district court's personal-jurisdiction ruling remains subject to ordinary appellate review. Neither the district court nor the court of appeals may use the sequencing rule to decide the merits without first establishing the authority necessary to do so.

### Cleveland v. Policy Management Systems Corp.

Argued February 24, 1999 | Decided May 24, 1999  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Fifth Circuit; judgment below: summary judgment entered against Cleveland under a rebuttable presumption that her SSDI position estopped her ADA qualification claim, 120 F.3d 513 (5th Cir. 1997). ([tile.loc.gov](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep526/usrep526795/usrep526795.pdf?utm_source=chatgpt.com))  
Area of Law: Disability law; ADA; SSDI representations

Questions Presented:

1. Whether an application for Social Security Disability Insurance benefits automatically estops a claimant from proving that she is a qualified individual with a disability under the ADA.

#### Judgment

Vacated and remanded, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Breyer | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg | Supports vacatur and remand | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to vacate and remand.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: Stone announces no ground beyond the Court's opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Effect of SSDI representations on ADA qualification

Holding and operative rule: An SSDI application or award does not automatically estop an ADA plaintiff from proving qualification because the two statutory schemes ask materially different questions. The plaintiff must nevertheless explain any apparent factual inconsistency sufficiently to permit a reasonable juror to find that she could perform the essential job functions with reasonable accommodation.

Reasoning: SSDI asks whether the claimant can engage in substantial gainful work under the Social Security Act and its administrative rules. That process ordinarily does not determine whether a particular employer could provide a reasonable workplace accommodation, while the ADA expressly treats performance with such accommodation as qualification. Timing, changes in condition, administrative presumptions, and job-specific accommodations can therefore make the two positions consistent. A claimant may not, however, ignore a prior sworn assertion that appears to negate an ADA element. At summary judgment she must offer a legally and factually coherent explanation that would allow a reasonable juror to accept both the prior statement, or her good-faith belief in it, and the present qualification claim. Cleveland was not given a proper opportunity to make that showing. ([tile.loc.gov](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep526/usrep526795/usrep526795.pdf?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

The ADA and SSDI: established; representations under the two statutes are assessed under their distinct elements and assumptions rather than through automatic estoppel.

*Celotex Corp. v. Catrett*: applied; the ADA plaintiff must produce an explanation sufficient to support a reasonable finding on the qualification element.

Judicial estoppel: declined to extend into a special presumption that an SSDI application or award ordinarily defeats an ADA claim.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Fifth Circuit is vacated, and the case is remanded so Cleveland may offer an explanation of her prior statements and the lower courts may decide qualification, accommodation, and remaining defenses under the ADA.

Effect: Cleveland's ADA claim is not barred by the SSDI proceedings, but she receives no finding that she was qualified or that Policy Management Systems violated the ADA. Her prior statements remain admissible evidence and may support summary judgment if her explanation is insufficient.

### Wilson v. Layne

Argued March 24, 1999 | Decided May 24, 1999  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Fourth Circuit; judgment below: media presence during execution of the arrest warrant held unconstitutional, but the participating officers granted qualified immunity, 141 F.3d 111 (4th Cir. 1998). ([tile.loc.gov](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep526/usrep526603/usrep526603.pdf?utm_source=chatgpt.com))  
Area of Law: Fourth Amendment; warrant execution; qualified immunity

Questions Presented:

1. Whether officers executing an arrest warrant in a home violate the Fourth Amendment by bringing members of the news media who do not assist execution, and whether the officers are entitled to qualified immunity.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 7-2.  
Supporting: O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: Stone, Stevens.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Stone (Fourth Amendment merits); Stevens (Fourth Amendment merits); Scalia (complete opinion); Kennedy (complete opinion); Souter (complete opinion); Thomas (complete opinion); Ginsburg (complete opinion); Breyer (complete opinion) | Supports affirmance | Fourth Amendment merits for all nine; qualified immunity for seven Justices |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Stevens | Stone | Concurs in part and dissents in part | Concurs in the Fourth Amendment holding; dissents from qualified immunity |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm the Fourth Amendment ruling, reverse the qualified-immunity judgment, and remand.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court as to the Fourth Amendment merits and joins Stevens's concurrence in part and dissent in part in full.  
Announced ground: The media's warrant-unrelated presence enlarged the home intrusion beyond lawful execution, and the obvious mismatch between a law-enforcement warrant and admission of unrelated private observers defeated qualified immunity despite the absence of a prior media-ride-along decision.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Unrelated media presence during home entry

Holding and operative rule: Officers violate the Fourth Amendment when they bring private observers into a home under a warrant although those persons serve no function reasonably related to lawful execution. (joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer).

Reasoning: A warrant authorizes the intrusion reasonably necessary to locate the person or evidence described, secure the premises, protect participants, and perform other execution functions grounded in law. The reporters and photographer did none of those things. They entered to observe and record the encounter for publication, a purpose that neither the arrest warrant nor the officers' execution authority encompassed. Their presence therefore added an independent residential intrusion without advancing the justification that made entry lawful. The holding does not prevent officers from using interpreters, medical personnel, technical specialists, identification witnesses, or other persons whose presence is reasonably connected to execution. ([tile.loc.gov](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep526/usrep526603/usrep526603.pdf?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

##### Question 2: Qualified immunity

Holding and operative rule: The officers are entitled to qualified immunity because, when the 1992 entry occurred, the unlawfulness of a media ride-along during residential warrant execution was not clearly established with the specificity required for personal damages liability.

Reasoning: The Court considers the complete body of materially relevant law, but the general principles limiting residential entries and warrant execution had not yet produced controlling precedent or a robust appellate consensus addressing nonparticipating media observers. Existing law made the constitutional rule the Court now states, yet reasonable officers could have understood then-current federal and local ride-along policies as leaving the precise practice unresolved. Qualified immunity therefore bars damages for this past entry even though the Court rejects the practice on the merits. The conclusion is limited to the state of law in 1992 and does not authorize repetition after this decision. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep526/usrep526603/usrep526603.pdf))

#### Concurrences

Stevens, joined by Stone: They agree that the media entry violated the Fourth Amendment and that the complete body of law, rather than a party's citations alone, governs immunity review.

#### Dissents

Stevens, joined by Stone: The settled limits of a warrant and the home's special protection made it objectively unreasonable to admit unrelated private guests for publicity. A prior case involving the same ride-along label was unnecessary, and they would remand for adjudication of causation, damages, and other preserved defenses.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*United States v. Ramirez*: applied; conduct during warrant execution remains independently subject to objective reasonableness and must be connected to lawful execution needs.

*Elder v. Holloway*: applied; qualified-immunity review considers the complete body of materially relevant law existing when the conduct occurred.

*Anderson v. Creighton*: followed; personal damages liability requires a right clearly established at a sufficiently specific level.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Fourth Circuit is affirmed.

Effect: The participating officers receive qualified immunity from the personal-capacity damages claims arising from the 1992 entry. The constitutional rule now forbids officers from bringing nonassisting media or other unrelated private observers into a home under warrant authority.

### Davis v. Monroe County Board of Education

Argued January 12, 1999 | Decided May 24, 1999  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Eleventh Circuit; judgment below: dismissal of the Title IX damages claim for student-on-student harassment affirmed en banc, 120 F.3d 1390 (11th Cir. 1997). ([tile.loc.gov](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep526/usrep526629/usrep526629.pdf?utm_source=chatgpt.com))  
Area of Law: Title IX; education; peer sexual harassment

Questions Presented:

1. Whether a federally funded school district may be liable in damages under Title IX for deliberate indifference to severe student-on-student sexual harassment in circumstances under the district's substantial control.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 6-3.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Stone, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Kennedy | Scalia, Thomas | Would affirm | Complete dissent |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: Stone announces no ground beyond the Court's opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Recipient liability for peer sexual harassment

Holding and operative rule: A Title IX funding recipient may be liable in damages when it is deliberately indifferent to known student-on-student sexual harassment that is severe, pervasive, and objectively offensive, occurs in a context substantially controlled by the recipient, and deprives the victim of equal access to educational opportunities or benefits.

Reasoning: Liability rests on the recipient's own conduct, not automatic responsibility for a student's act. A school district exercises substantial control over its premises, school-day relationships, disciplinary system, and the officials capable of responding to reported misconduct. When an appropriate institutional official receives reliable notice of serious peer harassment and the district chooses a response that is clearly indifferent to its continuation, the resulting educational exclusion is discrimination under the federally funded program attributable to the recipient. The complaint alleges repeated physical and verbal sexual conduct at school, reports by the student and her mother to teachers and the principal, ineffective or nonexistent corrective action, and concrete interference with education. Those allegations plausibly satisfy the recipient-fault, severity, control, causation, and educational-denial requirements. Simulated *Gebser*'s existing rule concerning a willfully blind, deliberately indifferent reporting failure remains available on facts that establish it, but need not be separately resolved at this pleading stage because actual notice is alleged. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep526/usrep526629/usrep526629.pdf))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

Kennedy, joined by Scalia and Thomas: Title IX's Spending Clause notice and its prohibition of discrimination under a recipient's program do not clearly authorize damages for one student's harassment of another. They would leave peer misconduct to school discipline and other law unless Congress expressly creates the asserted recipient liability.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Gebser v. Lago Vista Independent School District*: extended; the recipient-fault and causation framework applies to peer harassment occurring under the recipient's substantial control, while the existing actual-knowledge and willfully blind reporting-failure routes remain intact.

*Franklin v. Gwinnett County Public Schools*: applied; damages remain available for discrimination attributable to the funding recipient.

*Meritor Savings Bank v. Vinson*: applied by analogy; severity and educational exclusion are informed by hostile-environment principles without importing Title VII agency rules into schools.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Eleventh Circuit is reversed, and the case is remanded for adjudication of recipient fault, severity, substantial control, educational denial, causation, and damages.

Effect: Davis's complaint may proceed, but no liability or entitlement to damages is established. School officials retain disciplinary discretion, and Title IX does not federalize ordinary teasing, impose strict liability, or require a particular punishment.

---

### City of Monterey v. Del Monte Dunes at Monterey, Ltd.

Argued October 7, 1998 | Decided May 24, 1999  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit; judgment below: jury verdict and damages judgment for the landowner on the § 1983 regulatory-taking claim affirmed, 95 F.3d 1422 (9th Cir. 1996). ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep526/usrep526687/usrep526687.pdf))  
Area of Law: Seventh Amendment; regulatory takings; § 1983

Questions Presented:

1. Whether a § 1983 damages claim alleging that repeated land-use denials effected a regulatory taking carries a Seventh Amendment jury right, and whether the jury instructions and verdict may stand.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 5-4.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas. Opposing: O’Connor, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Kennedy | Stone (complete opinion); Stevens (complete opinion); Scalia (except the inverse-condemnation analogy); Thomas (complete opinion) | Supports affirmance | Jury right, jury allocation, Dolan nonextension, preservation, and judgment; inverse-condemnation analogy joined by Stone, Stevens, and Thomas |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Scalia | No other Justice | Concurs in the judgment | Alternative § 1983 tort basis for the jury right |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Souter | O’Connor, Ginsburg, Breyer | Concurs in part and dissents in part | Concurs in Dolan nonextension; dissents from the jury-right holding and affirmance |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.  
Public role: joins Kennedy's opinion in full.  
Announced ground: In this legal damages action, the judge states the governing takings law and the jury may determine disputed historical application facts and damages. *Dolan* does not govern an ordinary permit denial, and the case-specific verdict survives without endorsement of an unpreserved free-standing regulatory-taking formulation.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Seventh Amendment allocation

Holding and operative rule: A § 1983 action seeking legal damages for an asserted completed regulatory taking carries a Seventh Amendment jury right. The judge states the governing constitutional law and determines pure legal questions; the jury may resolve disputed historical application facts and the amount of legal damages under proper instructions.

Reasoning: Five Justices agree on the operative jury entitlement although they use two related historical routes. Kennedy, joined on this point by Stone, Stevens, and Thomas, treats the uncompensated interference with property alleged here as analogous to legal tort actions tried to juries. Scalia treats a § 1983 damages action itself as a legal tort without making the jury right depend on a separate inverse-condemnation analogy. Both routes establish that Del Monte Dunes sought legal compensation rather than prospective control of zoning policy. Simulated *Feltner v. Columbia Pictures Television, Inc.* confirms that the court supplies the governing legal standard while disputed historical facts and legal damages ordinarily belong to the jury. The jury therefore could decide the case-specific factual application questions submitted here. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep526/usrep526687/usrep526687.pdf))

##### Question 2: Dolan, preservation, and the verdict

Holding and operative rule: *Dolan* does not govern a permit denial that compels no dedication of land or another property interest. (joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer). On the preserved instructions and record, the verdict and damages judgment remain in force.

Reasoning: Simulated *Dolan v. City of Tigard* is confined to adjudicative exactions that condition approval on conveyance of a property interest. Monterey demanded no dedication, and the jury instructions did not impose rough proportionality. The Ninth Circuit's proportionality discussion was therefore unnecessary and supplies no rule for ordinary permit denials. The City also substantially participated in framing the instructions and did not preserve a broad request to reconsider the regulatory-taking formulation on which the case was tried. The record permitted findings that the City repeatedly changed its requirements, rejected a reduced proposal its officials had indicated could be acceptable, and failed to connect the final denial to the reasons advanced. The affirmance is confined to that posture and does not authorize juries to formulate zoning policy or revive an unpreserved general substantive test. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep526/usrep526687/usrep526687.pdf))

#### Concurrences

Scalia: A § 1983 damages action is itself a legal tort for Seventh Amendment purposes, regardless of whether the underlying constitutional right has a close inverse-condemnation analogue. He otherwise joins the principal opinion and agrees that the particular factual issues were properly submitted.

Souter, joined by O’Connor, Ginsburg, and Breyer: They agree that *Dolan* does not extend beyond compelled property exactions and that its rough-proportionality standard did not govern this permit denial.

#### Dissents

Souter, joined by O’Connor, Ginsburg, and Breyer: An inverse-condemnation claim historically did not carry the jury entitlement recognized by the Court, and § 1983 should not change the allocation merely because damages are sought. They would reverse the jury-based judgment while leaving the substantive land-use dispute for lawful judicial determination.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Feltner v. Columbia Pictures Television, Inc.*: extended; in this § 1983 legal damages action, disputed historical application facts and damages may be assigned to the jury while the judge controls the legal standard.

*Dolan v. City of Tigard*: declined to extend; nexus and rough proportionality remain confined to adjudicative conditions compelling dedication of a property interest.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit is affirmed.

Effect: The damages judgment for Del Monte Dunes remains in force. The decision neither directs approval of a development plan nor extends *Dolan* to ordinary permit denials, and it does not establish a generally applicable free-standing test for future regulatory-taking cases.

### California Dental Association v. Federal Trade Commission

Argued January 13, 1999 | Decided May 24, 1999  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit; judgment below: FTC jurisdiction and an abbreviated rule-of-reason determination sustaining the advertising restraints' illegality affirmed, 128 F.3d 720 (9th Cir. 1997). ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep526/usrep526756/usrep526756.pdf))  
Area of Law: Antitrust; professional associations; rule of reason

Questions Presented:

1. Whether restrictions adopted by a professional association on members' price and quality advertising may be condemned through an abbreviated quick look without fuller market analysis.

#### Judgment

Vacated and remanded, 5-4.  
Supporting: Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Souter, Thomas. Opposing: Stevens, Kennedy, Ginsburg, Breyer.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Souter | Stone (complete opinion); Stevens (FTC jurisdiction); O’Connor (complete opinion); Scalia (complete opinion); Kennedy (FTC jurisdiction); Thomas (complete opinion); Ginsburg (FTC jurisdiction); Breyer (FTC jurisdiction) | Supports vacatur and remand | FTC jurisdiction for all nine; depth of competitive analysis for five Justices |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Breyer | Stevens, Kennedy, Ginsburg | Concurs in part and dissents in part | Concurs in FTC jurisdiction; dissents from vacatur of the liability determination |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to sustain FTC jurisdiction, vacate the liability determination, and remand.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: The nonprofit Association materially advances its profit-seeking members' commercial operations and is within the FTC Act, but the mixed competitive possibilities of the advertising rules require a fuller, restraint-sensitive inquiry than the quick look performed below.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: FTC jurisdiction over the Association

Holding and operative rule: A nonprofit association carries on business for its members' profit, and falls within the FTC Act's covered corporate category, when its activities materially advance the commercial operations of profit-seeking members. (joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer).

Reasoning: The Act reaches an association organized to carry on business for its own profit or that of its members. Nonprofit form therefore is not conclusive. The California Dental Association furnished insurance and preferential financing, conducted marketing and public relations, lobbied and litigated for dentists, and enforced professional rules bearing directly on how members competed for patients. Those activities had a proximate and material relation to members' commercial operations. The rule does not place every nonprofit membership organization within FTC jurisdiction; professional education or civic activity without a materially commercial connection may present a different case. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep526/usrep526756/usrep526756.pdf))

##### Question 2: Depth of rule-of-reason analysis

Holding and operative rule: Abbreviated antitrust review is sufficient only when the restraint's anticompetitive tendency is apparent without substantial market study. The Association's price and quality advertising restrictions require a fuller, restraint-sensitive inquiry because the asserted harm and the consumer-information justifications are both economically plausible.

Reasoning: Simulated *State Oil Co. v. Khan* reserves categorical or near-summary condemnation for restraints whose harmful character and lack of redeeming competitive value are established through durable experience. The rules here may suppress truthful price competition and useful quality information, but they also purport to require verifiable discount terms and prevent unverifiable professional-quality claims in a market marked by information asymmetry. Those explanations may prove unsupported or pretextual, but they are not implausible enough to dismiss at the threshold. The required inquiry is calibrated to the restraint's circumstances, details, and logic. It need not become a full market-definition trial in every case, but it must test likely competitive effects and the asserted information-quality benefits rather than presume the answer. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep526/usrep526756/usrep526756.pdf))

#### Concurrences

Breyer, joined by Stevens, Kennedy, and Ginsburg: They agree that the FTC has statutory jurisdiction over the Association and that antitrust analysis remains sensitive to the particular professional market.

#### Dissents

Breyer, joined by Stevens, Kennedy, and Ginsburg: The Commission's findings and the record sufficiently established restrictions on truthful advertising and likely competitive harm, even after accounting for the asserted justifications. They would affirm the liability determination rather than require another competitive inquiry.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*State Oil Co. v. Khan*: clarified; restraints outside established summary categories receive an inquiry proportionate to their plausible competitive harms and benefits.

*National Collegiate Athletic Assn. v. Board of Regents of the University of Oklahoma*: clarified; a quick look is proper when anticompetitive tendency is intuitively obvious, not whenever a restraint affects price or information.

FTC Act §§ 4 and 5: established; nonprofit form does not defeat jurisdiction when an association materially advances its profit-seeking members' commercial operations.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit is vacated, and the case is remanded with instructions to sustain FTC jurisdiction but return the liability determination for a fuller, restraint-sensitive competitive inquiry.

Effect: The Association receives no antitrust immunity, and the Commission's existing liability determination cannot stand on the abbreviated analysis used below. A properly supported finding of unreasonable restraint remains available without requiring the fullest possible market trial.

---

### Richardson v. United States

Argued February 22, 1999 | Decided June 1, 1999  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Seventh Circuit; judgment below: continuing-criminal-enterprise conviction affirmed despite an instruction that the jury need not agree on the same predicate violations, 130 F.3d 765 (7th Cir. 1997). ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep526/usrep526813/usrep526813.pdf))  
Area of Law: Federal criminal law; jury unanimity; continuing criminal enterprise

Questions Presented:

1. Whether jurors in a continuing-criminal-enterprise prosecution must agree unanimously on the specific predicate drug violations that constitute the statutory continuing series.

#### Judgment

Vacated and remanded, 6-3.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, Scalia, Souter, Thomas, Breyer. Opposing: O’Connor, Kennedy, Ginsburg.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Breyer | Stone, Stevens, Scalia, Souter, Thomas | Supports vacatur and remand | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Kennedy | O’Connor, Ginsburg | Would affirm | Complete dissent |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to vacate and remand.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: Stone announces no ground beyond the Court's opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Unanimity on the continuing-series violations

Holding and operative rule: Each violation necessary to constitute the continuing series under 21 U.S.C. § 848 is an offense element. The jury must unanimously identify the same qualifying violations beyond a reasonable doubt, although it need not agree on every evidentiary detail or means of committing an agreed predicate.

Reasoning: Section 848 defines the greater offense by requiring a continuing series of violations, and the statutory word violations refers to independently criminal acts rather than interchangeable evidentiary details. Treating the predicates only as means would permit one juror to rely on one set of crimes and another juror on an entirely different set while both nominally agreed that a series existed. That outcome conflicts with the traditional requirement that a federal jury agree on each offense element and creates acute unfairness given the breadth and duration of a continuing-criminal-enterprise prosecution. The instruction expressly told jurors they need not agree on which violations formed the series, so the conviction cannot remain undisturbed without review under the correct element-specific rule. Whether the error was harmless, and what proceedings are otherwise lawful, remain for the court of appeals. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep526/usrep526813/usrep526813.pdf))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

Kennedy, joined by O’Connor and Ginsburg: The statute makes the continuing series the element and permits the individual violations to function as alternative means of proving that course of conduct. They would preserve the conviction because unanimity on the existence of the series, the required leadership role, and the remaining statutory elements was sufficient.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*United States v. Gaudin*: applied; a federal jury must determine every offense element beyond a reasonable doubt.

*Schad v. Arizona*: clarified; jurors need not agree on evidentiary means, but must agree on the particular statutory violations that Congress made elements of the continuing series.

*Garrett v. United States*: distinguished; recognition of the continuing-criminal-enterprise offense does not classify its required predicate violations as nonunanimous means.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Seventh Circuit is vacated, and the case is remanded to determine harmlessness and, if necessary, to set aside the continuing-criminal-enterprise conviction and conduct any further proceedings consistent with the element-specific unanimity rule.

Effect: Richardson's conviction may not be sustained merely by agreement that some continuing series existed. Any retrial or other disposition remains subject to ordinary double-jeopardy, sufficiency, preservation, and harmless-error principles.

---

**Sources:**

- [https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep526/usrep526529/usrep526529.pdf](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep526/usrep526529/usrep526529.pdf)
