# OCTOBER TERM 1998, CHUNK 6 OF 8

## Docket and Chronology

| Date | Case or matter | Event |
|---|---|---|
| June 3, 1999 | Reno v. Goncalves | Decision |
| June 7, 1999 | O’Sullivan v. Boerckel | Decision |
| June 10, 1999 | Neder v. United States | Decision |
| June 10, 1999 | City of Chicago v. Morales | Decision |
| June 10, 1999 | Lilly v. Virginia | Decision |
| June 10, 1999 | Dickinson v. Zurko | Decision |
| June 14, 1999 | Greater New Orleans Broadcasting Association, Inc. v. United States | Decision |
| June 14, 1999 | Cunningham v. Hamilton County | Decision |
| June 15, 1999 | Schleifer v. City of Charlottesville | Decision |
| June 17, 1999 | National Aeronautics and Space Administration v. Federal Labor Relations Authority | Decision |

Chronology note: The four June 10 decisions use the same pre-June 10 entering-law baseline, and the two June 14 decisions use the same pre-June 14 baseline. *City of Chicago v. Morales* becomes effective before the June 15 adjudication of *Schleifer v. City of Charlottesville*.

Source corrections: The official reports establish that Justice Souter joined the historical majority and filed a concurrence in *O’Sullivan*; that the historical judgment in *Neder* was affirmed in part, reversed in part, and remanded; that the judgment in *Lilly* was unanimous although its reasoning was fractured; that *Dickinson* was reversed and remanded; and that the unanimous judgment in *Greater New Orleans* included separate writings by Chief Justice Rehnquist and Justice Thomas. The correct lower-court citation in *Cunningham* is 144 F.3d 418, and the NASA record shows that a union representative attended the interview but was restricted in participating rather than excluded altogether. These public-record corrections do not alter Stone’s approved positions or create an adjudicative blocker. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/526/838/)) `OT_1998CHUNK6.md`

## Decisions and Dispositions

### Reno v. Goncalves

Argued April 21, 1999 | Decided June 3, 1999  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the First Circuit; judgment below: dismissal of the § 2241 petition reversed, habeas jurisdiction sustained, AEDPA § 440(d) held inapplicable to the pending former § 212(c) application, and the matter remanded to the Board of Immigration Appeals, 144 F.3d 110 (1st Cir. 1998). ([Department of Justice](https://www.justice.gov/osg/brief/reno-v-goncalves-petition))  
Area of Law: Immigration; habeas jurisdiction; statutory temporal reach

Questions Presented:

1. Whether AEDPA and IIRIRA clearly withdrew district-court jurisdiction under 28 U.S.C. § 2241 to hear statutory or constitutional habeas challenges to deportation by covered criminal aliens.
2. If habeas jurisdiction survives, whether AEDPA § 440(d)’s restrictions on former § 212(c) discretionary relief apply to applications or proceedings already pending when AEDPA took effect.
3. What interpretive weight, if any, is owed to the Attorney General’s contrary determination that § 440(d) applies to pending applications.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 6-3.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: O’Connor, Scalia, Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stevens | Stone, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Scalia | O’Connor, Thomas | Would reverse | Complete dissent |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: Stone announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Continued availability of § 2241 habeas review

Holding and operative rule: AEDPA and IIRIRA did not clearly withdraw district-court jurisdiction under 28 U.S.C. § 2241 over the pure statutory and constitutional challenges presented by a covered criminal alien in Goncalves’s transitional posture.

Reasoning: Section 2241 supplied a longstanding judicial channel for testing the legal authority to detain and deport. Congress altered immigration review extensively in 1996, but the provisions applicable to Goncalves do not expressly eliminate that historic writ or identify an equally clear route for his legal challenge. Withdrawal of habeas jurisdiction is not inferred from restrictions directed to ordinary judicial review, discretionary decisions, or particular executive acts. *Thunder Basin Coal Co. v. Reich*, 510 U.S. 200, permits statutory channeling when the enacted structure fairly discloses that purpose and preserves meaningful review. Here, the Government’s construction would leave the precise statutory and constitutional claims without a clearly available judicial forum. *Reno v. American-Arab Anti-Discrimination Committee*, decided February 24, 1999, concerned a separate action directly restraining commencement or continuation of removal proceedings. It did not decide the survival of § 2241 review of a final legal eligibility determination.

##### Question 2: Temporal reach of AEDPA § 440(d)

Holding and operative rule: AEDPA § 440(d) does not govern a former § 212(c) application already pending when AEDPA took effect because Congress supplied no clear direction applying the new categorical ineligibility rule to those pending applications.

Reasoning: Under *Landgraf v. USI Film Products*, 511 U.S. 244, and *Rivers v. Roadway Express, Inc.*, 511 U.S. 298, the Court first looks for a clear temporal command. Section 440(d) contains none, while neighboring provisions of the same legislation contain express effective-date instructions. That contrast is reinforced by *Lindh v. Murphy*, 521 U.S. 320. Goncalves had invoked the former discretionary process, obtained an immigration-judge ruling on its merits, and pursued an administrative appeal before AEDPA’s enactment. Applying § 440(d) would not merely alter the tribunal or procedure. It would extinguish the legal eligibility for discretionary consideration that governed the proceeding when it began. The Board must therefore adjudicate the pending request under the former eligibility rule. The judgment neither grants relief under § 212(c) nor governs applications first filed after AEDPA.

##### Question 3: Interpretive weight owed the Attorney General

Holding and operative rule: The Attorney General’s conclusion in *Matter of Soriano* receives no controlling weight because ordinary tools of statutory interpretation resolve § 440(d)’s temporal operation, and the agency’s contrary reasoning does not persuasively account for the statutory structure.

Reasoning: The Attorney General possesses substantial authority and expertise in administering the immigration laws, but neither district-court habeas jurisdiction nor Congress’s temporal command is committed to unreviewable agency determination. Under the deference framework then in force, an agency receives controlling weight only within statutory ambiguity and only for a permissible construction. *Soriano* treated statutory silence as sufficient authorization to terminate eligibility in pending proceedings and gave inadequate weight to Congress’s express temporal distinctions elsewhere in the enactment. Because text, structure, and settled nonretroactivity principles establish the governing rule, deference cannot make silence perform work the legislation does not support. The decision does not diminish agency authority over genuinely unresolved administrative details or the ultimate discretionary judgment under former § 212(c).

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

Scalia, joined by O’Connor and Thomas: The 1996 legislation channels covered criminal-alien challenges away from district-court habeas and makes the new limitation applicable to administrative decisions entered after AEDPA’s effective date. They would reverse and require dismissal or sustain the Board’s application of § 440(d).

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Felker v. Turpin*: followed; repeal of historic habeas jurisdiction requires clear statutory direction.

*Thunder Basin Coal Co. v. Reich*: applied and clarified; implied channeling requires a fairly discernible statutory structure that preserves meaningful review of the particular claim.

*Reno v. American-Arab Anti-Discrimination Committee*, decided February 24, 1999: distinguished; its jurisdictional rule for a separate action against commencement or continuation of proceedings does not resolve final-order habeas review of pure legal eligibility questions.

*Landgraf v. USI Film Products*, *Rivers v. Roadway Express, Inc.*, and *Lindh v. Murphy*: applied; statutory silence and contrasting express temporal provisions foreclose application of § 440(d) to pending former § 212(c) applications.

*INS v. Aguirre-Aguirre*, decided May 3, 1999: distinguished; deference concerning an evaluative immigration standard does not make the Attorney General authoritative on habeas jurisdiction or resolved statutory temporal meaning.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the First Circuit is affirmed, and the Board of Immigration Appeals shall consider Goncalves’s former § 212(c) application under the law governing applications pending on April 24, 1996.

Effect: Goncalves is entitled to a discretionary determination, not to favorable relief. The equal-protection claim excluded from the grant remains unadjudicated.

---

### O’Sullivan v. Boerckel

Argued March 30, 1999 | Decided June 7, 1999  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Seventh Circuit; judgment below: claims presented to the Illinois Appellate Court but omitted from a petition for leave to appeal to the Illinois Supreme Court treated as exhausted, 135 F.3d 1194 (7th Cir. 1998). ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/526/838/))  
Area of Law: Habeas corpus; exhaustion; procedural default

Questions Presented:

1. Whether a state prisoner must present federal claims in a petition for discretionary review to the State’s highest court to satisfy the exhaustion requirement.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 6-3.  
Supporting: Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas. Opposing: Stevens, Ginsburg, Breyer.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Stone, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence | Souter | No other Justice | Supports reversal and remand | State authority to define an unnecessary discretionary step |
| Dissent | Stevens | Ginsburg, Breyer | Would affirm | Complete dissent |
| Dissent | Breyer | Stevens, Ginsburg | Would affirm | Practical operation of state discretionary review |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: Stone announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: One complete round of state review

Holding and operative rule: A state prisoner ordinarily must present each federal claim through one complete round of the State’s established appellate process, including an available discretionary petition to the State’s highest court when that petition is an ordinary part of the review system. A clear state rule declaring that step unnecessary controls.

Reasoning: Sections 2254(b) and (c) require a prisoner to give the state courts a fair opportunity to resolve each federal claim before federal intervention. That opportunity is measured by the review process the State actually maintains, not by whether every level grants review as of right. Illinois Rule 315 supplied a regular petition for leave to appeal through which the State’s highest court could consider federal questions, and Illinois had not announced that prisoners could omit that step. Boerckel presented some claims in his petition but withheld others, demonstrating that the route was both known and available. Low grant rates do not convert an ordinary discretionary stage into a nonexistent remedy. If state law now prevents renewed presentation, the omitted claims are procedurally defaulted rather than merely awaiting exhaustion, subject to cause, prejudice, actual innocence, waiver, and any later clear state rule making the petition unnecessary.

#### Concurrences

Souter: A State remains free to remove its discretionary court from the exhaustion sequence by clearly declaring that prisoners need not seek that review. Illinois had made no such declaration when Boerckel litigated his claims.

#### Dissents

Stevens, joined by Ginsburg and Breyer: Presentation to the state trial and intermediate appellate courts afforded Illinois a sufficient opportunity to resolve the federal claims. Requiring petitions to a court that accepts only a small fraction of them burdens both prisoners and the state judiciary without materially advancing comity.

Breyer, joined by Stevens and Ginsburg: Exhaustion should reflect the practical function of state review rather than formal availability alone. He would treat a highly selective court of last resort as unnecessary absent a state indication that such petitions are expected.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Picard v. Connor*: applied; each federal claim must be fairly presented through the ordinary state review machinery.

*Castille v. Peoples*: clarified; discretionary review is part of exhaustion when it is an ordinary available appellate step rather than an extraordinary collateral remedy.

*Lonchar v. Thomas*: applied; exhaustion and procedural default remain source-governed rules rather than generalized finality doctrines.

28 U.S.C. §§ 2254(b) and (c): clarified; one complete round ordinarily includes an available discretionary petition to the State’s highest court, subject to a clear state rule making that step unnecessary.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Seventh Circuit is reversed, and the case is remanded with instructions to treat the omitted claims as unexhausted and, if Illinois review is now closed, procedurally defaulted subject to the recognized exceptions and gateways.

Effect: Boerckel may obtain federal merits review only if an applicable state rule excuses the omitted petition, the State waives the default, or he satisfies cause and prejudice, actual innocence, or another recognized ground.

### Neder v. United States

Argued February 23, 1999 | Decided June 10, 1999  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Eleventh Circuit; judgment below: fraud and tax convictions affirmed after materiality was withheld from the jury and determined by the trial judge, 136 F.3d 1459 (11th Cir. 1998). ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep527/usrep527001/usrep527001.pdf))  
Area of Law: Criminal procedure; offense elements; harmless error

Questions Presented:

1. Whether a trial court’s failure to submit an offense element to the jury is structural error or may be harmless beyond a reasonable doubt when the element was uncontested and supported by overwhelming evidence.

#### Judgment

Affirmed in part, reversed in part, and remanded; votes vary by component.

| Judgment component | Disposition and vote | Supporting | Opposing | Relief or remand |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Tax-fraud counts | Affirmed, 5-4 | Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Thomas, Breyer | Stone, Scalia, Souter, Ginsburg | The tax convictions remain in force because findings the jury actually returned necessarily established materiality. |
| Mail-, wire-, and bank-fraud counts | Reversed and remanded, 9-0 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | No Justice | The lower courts shall determine whether any valid verdict findings necessarily established materiality; absent such findings, the affected convictions and sentences must be vacated. |

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Stone, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer as to materiality and constitutional error; Stevens, Kennedy, Thomas, Breyer as to the tax-count actual-findings disposition; Kennedy, Thomas, Breyer as to the broader harmless-error analysis | Supports the component dispositions stated above | Scope stated by proposition |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Stevens | No other Justice | Concurs in the judgment | Tax-count actual-findings ground and remand of the remaining counts |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Scalia | Stone, Souter, Ginsburg | Concurs in part and dissents in part | Agrees that materiality is an element; would reverse every affected conviction as structurally defective |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand on every count from which materiality was withheld.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court as to materiality and constitutional error, and joins Scalia’s concurrence in part and dissent in part in full.  
Announced ground: Complete removal of an unadmitted offense element leaves no constitutionally valid jury verdict on the charged offense. An appellate court may not supply the missing verdict by declaring the element uncontested or the evidence overwhelming.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1A: Materiality as an offense element

Holding and operative rule: Materiality is an element of the affected mail-, wire-, bank-, and tax-fraud offenses and must be submitted to the jury and proved beyond a reasonable doubt.

Reasoning: Congress used legal terms carrying a settled materiality component, and none of the governing statutes displaces that established meaning. A false statement or representation is material when it has the natural tendency or capacity to influence the decision or action to which it is addressed. That determination forms part of the conduct Congress made criminal rather than a preliminary legal issue assigned exclusively to the judge. Under simulated *United States v. Gaudin*, every enacted offense element belongs to the jury. The trial court therefore committed constitutional error by deciding materiality itself. The Court’s agreement on that point does not determine whether every resulting conviction must automatically be set aside.

##### Question 1B: Consequence of withholding the element

Holding and operative rule: Complete withdrawal of an unadmitted element from the jury is constitutional error. The error may be harmless when facts the jury actually and validly found necessarily embrace the omitted element (joined by Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Thomas, and Breyer); an appellate conclusion that the element was uncontested and supported by overwhelming evidence is not, standing alone, sufficient to preserve the conviction (joined by Stone, Stevens, Scalia, Souter, and Ginsburg).

Reasoning: The Constitution requires a jury determination of guilt, but the effect of an instructional omission depends on what the jury actually decided. On the tax counts, the verdict required the jury to find that Neder knowingly and falsely reported specified amounts of total income. Those affirmative findings necessarily established that the misstatements could influence the tax determination, so Stevens supplies the fifth vote to affirm those counts without accepting a general appellate power to hypothesize a verdict from overwhelming evidence. The mail-, wire-, and bank-fraud verdicts contain no equivalent count-specific findings identified by the Eleventh Circuit. All nine Justices therefore reverse and remand those counts for application of the controlling actual-findings rule. Stone, Scalia, Souter, and Ginsburg would go further and classify complete removal of any unadmitted element as structural error requiring a new trial in every affected count.

#### Concurrences

Necessary fifth vote: Stevens agrees that the omission of an element cannot be cured solely by an appellate assessment that the evidence was uncontested and overwhelming. He nevertheless concludes that the tax verdicts themselves necessarily established materiality because the jury affirmatively found knowing false reporting of total income. He joins affirmance of those counts on that confined ground and agrees that the remaining counts must be reconsidered.

Scalia, joined by Stone, Souter, and Ginsburg, agrees that materiality is an offense element and that the non-tax counts cannot stand under the Eleventh Circuit’s reasoning. Their separate writing treats complete removal of an unadmitted element as structural error.

#### Dissents

Scalia, joined by Stone, Souter, and Ginsburg: They oppose affirmance of the tax counts because the jury was never instructed to decide materiality as an element of those offenses. They would vacate every affected conviction and permit retrial under complete instructions.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*United States v. Gaudin*: followed; materiality, when incorporated into the enacted offense, must be determined by the jury beyond a reasonable doubt.

*Sullivan v. Louisiana*: clarified; an element omission is not cured merely by appellate confidence in the evidence, although a valid verdict may necessarily encompass the omitted proposition.

*Chapman v. California*: clarified; harmlessness must rest on the constitutional adjudicative record rather than a judicially hypothesized guilty verdict.

*Carella v. California* and *California v. Roy*: applied; actual jury findings may establish that an instructional error did not remove a genuinely disputed factual determination from the verdict.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Eleventh Circuit is affirmed as to the tax-fraud counts, reversed as to the mail-, wire-, and bank-fraud counts, and the case is remanded for count-specific application of the actual-findings rule and for vacation and lawful retrial of any conviction not supported by such findings.

Effect: Neder’s tax convictions remain in force. The remaining fraud convictions may survive only if a valid verdict necessarily established materiality; otherwise their convictions and sentences must be set aside.

---

### City of Chicago v. Morales

Argued December 9, 1998 | Decided June 10, 1999  
On writ of certiorari to the Supreme Court of Illinois; judgment below: the gang-loitering ordinance invalidated on its face and the resulting convictions reversed, 177 Ill. 2d 440, 687 N.E.2d 53 (1997). ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep527/usrep527041/usrep527041.pdf))  
Area of Law: Due process; vagueness; public-order regulation

Questions Presented:

1. Whether Chicago’s ordinance criminalizing failure to obey a dispersal order when a police officer reasonably believes a gang member and others are loitering with “no apparent purpose” is unconstitutionally vague.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 7-2.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: Scalia, Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Stone, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Scalia | No other Justice | Would reverse | Complete dissent |
| Dissent | Thomas | Scalia | Would reverse | Complete dissent |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: Stone announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Standardless dispersal trigger

Holding and operative rule: A criminal ordinance is facially vague when every prosecution depends on an officer’s unexplained conclusion that persons are remaining in public with “no apparent purpose,” and the law supplies no objective criterion governing that antecedent judgment. A later refusal to obey the resulting dispersal order does not cure the standardless trigger.

Reasoning: The ordinance requires no obstruction, threat, trespass, intimidation, unlawful purpose, criminal conduct, or minimum duration of presence. Its decisive predicate is an officer’s inability to discern an apparent purpose, a condition that describes the officer’s state of understanding rather than an objective act by the persons ordered to leave. Chicago argues that criminal liability arises only after refusal to disperse, but the order itself becomes legally compulsory only because of the uncontrolled antecedent judgment. A person cannot determine whether ordinary standing, conversation, or association will produce an enforceable order, and patrol officers receive no meaningful standard distinguishing permitted presence from conduct subject to dispersal. The Illinois Supreme Court rejected the City’s proposed narrowing constructions, and this Court may not create a replacement ordinance. Chicago remains free to regulate objectively defined obstruction, trespass, intimidation, threats, emergency conditions, or criminal activity.

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

Scalia: The ordinance criminalizes only a knowing refusal to obey a police order and gives adequate notice of that duty. He would treat the officer’s preliminary assessment as ordinary enforcement discretion rather than an element requiring further statutory definition.

Thomas, joined by Scalia: Chicago historically possesses broad police power to regulate public assemblies associated with gang activity and protect affected neighborhoods. He would sustain the dispersal mechanism as a reasonable public-order measure.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Papachristou v. City of Jacksonville*: applied; a public-order law may not make innocent presence criminal through an undefined official judgment.

*Kolender v. Lawson*: followed; penal law must constrain street-level discretion at the point where official action becomes compulsory.

*Grayned v. City of Rockford*: applied; fair notice and meaningful enforcement standards are required, particularly when ordinary public presence and association are affected.

*Coates v. Cincinnati*: followed; a subjective official assessment cannot supply the decisive boundary of a criminal prohibition.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Supreme Court of Illinois is affirmed, and the invalidation of the ordinance and reversals or dismissals resulting from that judgment remain in force.

Effect: Chicago may not enforce the ordinance in its present form. It may enact an objectively bounded law governing obstruction, trespass, intimidation, threats, criminal conduct, or genuine emergency dispersal.

### Lilly v. Virginia

Argued March 29, 1999 | Decided June 10, 1999  
On writ of certiorari to the Supreme Court of Virginia; judgment below: capital conviction and sentence affirmed after admission of a nontestifying accomplice’s custodial confession, 255 Va. 558, 499 S.E.2d 522 (1998). ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep527/usrep527116/usrep527116.pdf))  
Area of Law: Confrontation Clause; hearsay; accomplice confessions

Questions Presented:

1. Whether admitting a nontestifying accomplice’s custodial confession that shifts blame to the accused violates the Confrontation Clause.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stevens | Stone, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer in full; Scalia as to preservation, absence of a firmly rooted exception, constitutional violation, and remand | Supports reversal and remand | Scope stated by proposition |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Scalia | Thomas | Concurs in the judgment | Formal custodial confession and categorical confrontation ground |
| Concurrence in the judgment | O’Connor | Kennedy | Concurs in the judgment | Blame-shifting portions, absence of a firmly rooted exception, and remand under the received reliability framework |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: Stone announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Blame-shifting custodial confession

Holding and operative rule: A nontestifying accomplice’s custodial confession whose portions used against the accused minimize the declarant’s responsibility and assign the charged killing to the accused is not admissible merely as a statement against penal interest. Its admission without prior cross-examination or particularized guarantees of trustworthiness independent of corroborating trial evidence violates the Confrontation Clause. (joined by Stone, Stevens, Scalia, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer).

Reasoning: The prosecution used Mark Lilly’s accusation for its truth, yet Lilly had no opportunity to test the circumstances of the police interrogation, the declarant’s intoxication, the leading questions, his incentive to obtain favorable treatment, or his motive to shift primary responsibility. A broad category of statements against penal interest is not firmly rooted at the scope asserted here. The self-inculpatory portions of a confession must be separated from collateral accusations that exculpate the speaker and inculpate another person. Under the received *Ohio v. Roberts* framework, reliability must arise from the circumstances surrounding the statement itself; corroborating prosecution evidence cannot make an uncross-examined accusation self-validating. Scalia and Thomas would rest the judgment on the more categorical ground that a formal custodial confession introduced against an accused is a paradigmatic confrontation violation. O’Connor and Kennedy would reserve genuinely self-inculpatory statements and permit a narrower reliability inquiry.

#### Concurrences

Scalia, joined by Thomas: A formal custodial confession prepared for prosecutorial use is within the core class of accusations the Confrontation Clause requires to be tested by cross-examination. Judicial confidence in reliability cannot replace confrontation.

O’Connor, joined by Kennedy: The case does not establish a blanket prohibition against every accomplice statement that also incriminates a defendant. The blame-shifting passages here were not genuinely against the declarant’s interest and require reconsideration under the received trustworthiness framework.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Lee v. Illinois*: followed; custodial accomplice confessions that shift or spread blame are presumptively unreliable.

*Ohio v. Roberts*: clarified; the statement-against-interest category is not firmly rooted for blame-shifting accomplice accusations, and residual trustworthiness must arise from the statement’s own circumstances.

*Williamson v. United States*: applied; genuinely self-inculpatory portions are separated from collateral accusations serving the declarant’s interest.

*Idaho v. Wright*: applied; corroborating evidence introduced elsewhere at trial cannot establish the particularized guarantees needed to admit an uncross-examined accusation.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Supreme Court of Virginia is reversed, and the case is remanded for determination whether admission of the confession was harmless beyond a reasonable doubt and, if not, for vacation of the conviction and sentence and any lawful retrial.

Effect: Virginia may not sustain Lilly’s conviction by treating the blame-shifting confession as admissible under a firmly rooted statement-against-interest exception. Genuinely self-inculpatory, nonaccusatory remarks and statements admitted through another established constitutional route remain governed by their own rules.

---

### Dickinson v. Zurko

Argued March 24, 1999 | Decided June 10, 1999  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Federal Circuit; judgment below: Patent and Trademark Office findings reviewed under a clearly-erroneous standard and the Board’s patentability determination set aside, 142 F.3d 1447 (Fed. Cir. 1998) (en banc). ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep527/usrep527150/usrep527150.pdf))  
Area of Law: Administrative law; patent review; agency factfinding

Questions Presented:

1. Whether the Administrative Procedure Act’s substantial-evidence standard or a more searching clearly-erroneous standard governs Federal Circuit review of Patent and Trademark Office factual findings.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 7-2.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Souter, Thomas, Breyer. Opposing: Kennedy, Ginsburg.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Breyer | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Souter, Thomas | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Kennedy | Ginsburg | Would affirm | Complete dissent |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: Stone announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: APA review of PTO factual findings

Holding and operative rule: Direct Federal Circuit review of Patent and Trademark Office factual findings is governed by the applicable court-agency standard in APA § 706, not by the clearly-erroneous standard governing appellate review of district-court findings. The Federal Circuit must identify and apply the proper § 706 category on the whole record.

Reasoning: The PTO is an administrative agency, and §§ 559 and 706 establish the ordinary judicial-review regime for agency action. The Federal Circuit’s historical use of clearly-erroneous terminology does not establish the kind of unmistakable special statutory arrangement required to displace the APA. The difference is substantive: clearly-erroneous review permits a reviewing court to set aside a finding upon a firm conviction of error, while the APA standards preserve greater responsibility in the agency charged with resolving factual disputes. Simulated *Allentown Mack Sales & Service, Inc. v. NLRB* confirms that whole-record review remains meaningful and requires the agency to apply its announced legal standard, but it does not convert agency review into court-to-court review. The Court leaves the Federal Circuit to determine whether § 706(2)(E) or § 706(2)(A) governs the particular PTO proceeding and expresses no view on ultimate patentability.

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

Kennedy, joined by Ginsburg: The longstanding patent-review practice employed a sufficiently settled and specialized standard to survive the APA. They would preserve the Federal Circuit’s more searching review of technical PTO factfinding.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Allentown Mack Sales & Service, Inc. v. NLRB*: followed; agency factual findings receive whole-record review under the governing APA standard.

Administrative Procedure Act §§ 559 and 706: clarified; direct appellate review of PTO factfinding follows the court-agency model rather than the clearly-erroneous standard used for district-court findings.

Pre-APA patent-review practice: limited; historical terminology does not displace the APA absent a sufficiently clear statutory preservation.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Federal Circuit is reversed, and the case is remanded with instructions to identify and apply the proper APA § 706 standard to the PTO’s factual findings on the whole record.

Effect: The PTO receives no automatic affirmance, and the applicants receive no determination of patentability. The Federal Circuit must conduct meaningful review under the applicable APA category.

### Greater New Orleans Broadcasting Association, Inc. v. United States

Argued April 27, 1999 | Decided June 14, 1999  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Fifth Circuit; judgment below: application of 18 U.S.C. § 1304 to advertisements for lawful private-casino gambling upheld, 149 F.3d 334 (5th Cir. 1998). ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/527/173/))  
Area of Law: First Amendment; commercial speech; gambling advertising

Questions Presented:

1. Whether applying 18 U.S.C. § 1304 to advertisements for lawful private casino gambling violates the First Amendment in light of the statute’s exemptions and the Government’s inconsistent gambling policies.

#### Judgment

Reversed, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stevens | Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports reversal | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Thomas | No other Justice | Concurs in the judgment | Rejects reduced protection for truthful commercial speech |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: Stone announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Truthful advertisements for lawful casino gambling

Holding and operative rule: Section 1304 may not be enforced against the truthful, nonmisleading advertisements for lawful private-casino gambling at issue because the federal scheme’s extensive comparable exceptions prevent the prohibition from directly and materially advancing its asserted interests with a reasonable fit.

Reasoning: The Government identifies interests in reducing gambling demand and respecting the policies of States that restrict gambling. Those interests are substantial, but the actual statutory structure permits or tolerates extensive promotion of state lotteries, tribal gaming, charitable gambling, and other gambling activities capable of stimulating the same general demand. The restriction therefore suppresses truthful information about lawful private casinos while leaving materially comparable incentives untouched. Simulated *Rubin v. Coors Brewing Co.* treats such internal contradictions as evidence that an information ban does not perform the asserted work, and direct regulation of casino locations, operating rules, taxation, wagering conditions, and access remains available. The judgment is confined to truthful advertisements for lawful private casinos in jurisdictions that permit the underlying activity. It does not protect deceptive advertising, promotion of illegal transactions, targeting of minors, or a materially narrower restriction supported by evidence.

#### Concurrences

Thomas: Truthful speech concerning a lawful transaction should not receive reduced constitutional protection merely because it proposes commercial activity. He agrees that § 1304 cannot constitutionally be applied here but would not rely on the existing commercial-speech hierarchy.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Central Hudson Gas & Electric Corp. v. Public Service Commission*: applied; truthful commercial-speech restrictions must materially advance a substantial interest with a reasonable fit.

*Rubin v. Coors Brewing Co.*: followed; statutory exceptions leaving comparable incentives untouched undermine the claimed effectiveness of an information ban.

*United States v. Edge Broadcasting Co.*: distinguished; the present scheme’s breadth and contradictory exemptions do not closely implement a coherent interstate policy boundary.

*Posadas de Puerto Rico Associates v. Tourism Company of Puerto Rico*: limited; governmental authority to regulate an underlying activity does not automatically authorize suppression of truthful speech concerning that activity.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Fifth Circuit is reversed, and 18 U.S.C. § 1304 may not be enforced against the truthful, nonmisleading advertisements for lawful private-casino gambling adjudicated in this case.

Effect: The broadcasters may carry the advertisements described in the record. Section 1304 is not facially invalidated and remains subject to application in materially different settings consistent with the First Amendment.

### Cunningham v. Hamilton County

Argued April 19, 1999 | Decided June 14, 1999  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Sixth Circuit; judgment below: interlocutory appeal from a Rule 37 sanction imposed personally on counsel dismissed for lack of appellate jurisdiction, 144 F.3d 418 (6th Cir. 1998). ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/527/198/))  
Area of Law: Federal appellate jurisdiction; collateral-order doctrine; discovery sanctions

Questions Presented:

1. Whether an order imposing a Rule 37 discovery sanction on an attorney is immediately appealable under the collateral-order doctrine before final judgment in the underlying action.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Thomas | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence | Kennedy | No other Justice | Supports affirmance | Extraordinary-review boundary |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: Stone announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Immediate appeal of a Rule 37 sanction

Holding and operative rule: An order imposing a Rule 37 sanction on counsel during ongoing litigation is not immediately appealable under the collateral-order doctrine when its basis is intertwined with case management and it can be effectively reviewed after final judgment.

Reasoning: The collateral-order doctrine applies only to a small class of decisions that conclusively resolve an important issue separate from the merits and would be effectively unreviewable after final judgment. A discovery sanction does not satisfy those conditions. Determining its validity ordinarily requires examination of the disputed discovery obligations, counsel’s conduct, the parties’ positions, and the district court’s management of the case. Those matters are not sufficiently separate from the ongoing litigation. Postjudgment review can vacate the sanction, order repayment, correct reputational findings, and provide all ordinary appellate relief. The fact that counsel may later withdraw or be disqualified does not transform an otherwise reviewable litigation-management ruling into an immediately appealable order. Section 1292(b), extraordinary mandamus, and independently final contempt proceedings remain available when their separate requirements are met.

#### Concurrences

Kennedy: Exceptional sanctions implicating a right genuinely destroyed before final judgment may remain reviewable through mandamus or another independently authorized route. The ordinary monetary and reputational concerns presented here do not satisfy those demanding standards.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Digital Equipment Corp. v. Desktop Direct, Inc.*: applied; practical unreviewability and exceptional importance are indispensable to collateral-order appeal.

*Cohen v. Beneficial Industrial Loan Corp.*: clarified; professional status does not create a categorical exception to the final-judgment rule.

*Firestone Tire & Rubber Co. v. Risjord*: followed; litigation-management rulings reviewable after final judgment ordinarily fall outside collateral-order jurisdiction.

*Richardson-Merrell Inc. v. Koller*: followed; an attorney-related interlocutory ruling is not immediately appealable merely because it affects counsel personally.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Sixth Circuit is affirmed, and Cunningham’s interlocutory appeal remains dismissed for lack of jurisdiction.

Effect: The Rule 37 sanction remains subject to review after final judgment in the underlying action. The decision does not foreclose another appellate or extraordinary route whose independent requirements are satisfied.

### Schleifer v. City of Charlottesville

Argued April 28, 1999 | Decided June 15, 1999  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Fourth Circuit; judgment below: final judgment rejecting the facial constitutional challenges to Charlottesville’s juvenile-curfew ordinance affirmed, 159 F.3d 843 (4th Cir. 1998). ([Law Resource](https://law.resource.org/pub/us/case/reporter/F3/159/159.F3d.843.97-1723.html))  
Area of Law: Due process; First Amendment; juvenile curfews

Questions Presented:

1. Whether, and to what extent, minors possess a constitutionally protected liberty to move about and remain in public places at night, and what constitutional review governs a juvenile curfew burdening that liberty.
2. Whether a juvenile curfew impermissibly interferes with a fit parent’s constitutional authority to permit and direct a minor child’s otherwise lawful activities.
3. Whether the ordinance’s exceptions and enforcement standards provide adequate notice, constrain official discretion, and sufficiently preserve protected First Amendment activity.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 6-3.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: O’Connor, Scalia, Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Souter | Stone, Stevens, Kennedy, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | O’Connor | Scalia, Thomas | Would affirm | Complete dissent |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: Stone announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Minors’ liberty to remain in public at night

Not reached; the facial vagueness and First Amendment-preservation defect independently requires reversal and complete prospective relief from the ordinance before the Court.

##### Question 2: Fit-parent authority

Not reached; the Court does not determine the constitutional standard governing a precise juvenile curfew that conflicts with a fit parent’s permission for otherwise lawful activity.

##### Question 3: Undefined First Amendment exception and enforcement discretion

Holding and operative rule: A juvenile curfew is facially invalid when lawful nighttime presence depends on an undefined exception for activity “protected by the First Amendment,” and the ordinance supplies neither objective standards nor a binding narrowing construction sufficient to give minors, parents, and police fair notice and to constrain enforcement discretion.

Reasoning: The exception requires a minor, parent, and patrol officer to determine at the point of encounter whether political conversation, informal association, artistic performance, religious activity, travel connected to expression, or another mixed course of conduct receives constitutional protection. The ordinance supplies no textual criteria for making that legal classification, and record testimony confirmed materially inconsistent understandings of its coverage. The warning system postpones prosecution but does not cure the initial stop, dispersal command, record of violation, parental exposure, chilling effect, or repeat-offense consequences. *City of Chicago v. Morales*, decided June 10, 1999, establishes that a later refusal or warning cannot cure a standardless antecedent judgment that activates penal authority. Charlottesville may protect minors and address nighttime crime through defined age, hour, location, conduct, emergency, work, travel, parental-accompaniment, and specified expressive exceptions. It may not make legality depend on an officer’s real-time resolution of the full constitutional boundary.

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

O’Connor, joined by Scalia and Thomas: The ordinance defines the regulated age group and hours, supplies eight express exceptions, and requires warning before prosecution. They would treat the First Amendment exception as protective rather than standardless and sustain the ordinance against the facial challenge.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*City of Chicago v. Morales*, decided June 10, 1999: extended; a warning or later refusal does not cure an undefined antecedent classification controlling whether police may compel dispersal and initiate criminal exposure.

*Reno v. American Civil Liberties Union*: applied; a criminal rule affecting protected expression requires adequate precision, and an additional mental-state or warning requirement does not resolve uncertainty about what activity is lawful.

*Kolender v. Lawson*: followed; penal rules must constrain enforcement discretion through objective standards.

*Grayned v. City of Rockford*: applied; fair notice and precision are especially important where the law reaches speech, association, and presence in public places.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Fourth Circuit is reversed, and the case is remanded with instructions that the district court enter declaratory and prospective relief against enforcement unless a binding state-law construction legally available on remand supplies objective and sufficiently precise boundaries.

Effect: Charlottesville may not enforce the ordinance in its present form. The City remains free to enact a narrower curfew with clearly defined exceptions and enforcement standards, and the Court enters no holding on the scrutiny governing such a law or on the separate rights of minors and parents under a precise ordinance.

### National Aeronautics and Space Administration v. Federal Labor Relations Authority

Argued March 23, 1999 | Decided June 17, 1999  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Eleventh Circuit; judgment below: the Federal Labor Relations Authority’s order recognizing an employee’s § 7114(a)(2)(B) representation right during an Office of Inspector General examination enforced, 120 F.3d 1208 (11th Cir. 1997). ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/527/229/))  
Area of Law: Federal labor law; Inspector General investigations; employee representation

Questions Presented:

1. Whether an Office of Inspector General investigator is a “representative of the agency” conducting an examination within 5 U.S.C. § 7114(a)(2)(B), thereby triggering an employee’s right to union representation.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 6-3.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: O’Connor, Scalia, Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stevens | Stone, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Thomas | O’Connor, Scalia | Would reverse | Complete dissent |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: Stone announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Inspector General investigator as an agency representative

Holding and operative rule: An investigator employed by an agency’s Office of Inspector General is a “representative of the agency” when examining an agency employee within § 7114(a)(2)(B). The office’s statutory independence from ordinary management does not erase that agency relationship or the employee’s representation right.

Reasoning: The investigator works within NASA’s Office of Inspector General, examines a NASA employee concerning agency-related conduct, and develops information capable of producing discipline within the agency. Independence from ordinary management protects the investigation from interference; it does not convert the Inspector General into an entity external to NASA for every statutory purpose. Section 7114 and the Inspector General Act can operate together. A union representative may assist the employee, clarify questions, and protect the statutory interest without directing the investigation, obstructing questioning, compromising confidential information, or controlling investigative decisions. In the interview before the Court, a representative was present but the investigator restricted participation on the premise that the statutory right did not apply. The FLRA properly rejected that categorical premise. The Court prescribes no universal interview protocol and leaves particular limits on participation to lawful administration consistent with both statutes.

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

Thomas, joined by O’Connor and Scalia: An Inspector General investigator acts independently of agency management and should not be treated as management’s representative for the federal labor statute. They would hold that imposing the representation requirement risks compromising the investigative independence Congress specifically protected.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*NLRB v. J. Weingarten, Inc.*: applied by analogy; an employee-representation right may operate during an investigatory examination reasonably feared to result in discipline without transferring control of the interview to the representative.

5 U.S.C. § 7114(a)(2)(B): established; an agency Office of Inspector General investigator remains a representative of the agency for the statutory examination right.

Inspector General Act: clarified; investigative independence from ordinary management coexists with statutory employee-representation protections and does not create external-entity status.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Eleventh Circuit is affirmed, and NASA shall comply with the Federal Labor Relations Authority’s order recognizing the § 7114(a)(2)(B) representation right.

Effect: Bargaining-unit employees may invoke the statutory representation right during qualifying NASA Inspector General examinations. A representative may not obstruct questioning, direct the investigation, or compromise information protected by law.
