# OCTOBER TERM 1998, CHUNK 7 OF 8

## Docket and Chronology

| Date | Case or matter | Event |
|---|---|---|
| June 17, 1999 | Strickler v. Greene | Decision |
| June 17, 1999 | Grupo Mexicano de Desarrollo, S.A. v. Alliance Bond Fund, Inc. | Decision |
| June 18, 1999 | City of Dallas v. Dallas Fire Fighters Association | Decision |
| June 21, 1999 | Martin v. Hadix | Decision |
| June 21, 1999 | Jefferson County v. Acker | Decision |
| June 22, 1999 | Sutton v. United Air Lines, Inc. | Decision |
| June 22, 1999 | Albertson’s, Inc. v. Kirkingburg | Decision |
| June 22, 1999 | Olmstead v. L.C. | Decision |
| June 22, 1999 | Murphy v. United Parcel Service, Inc. | Decision |
| June 22, 1999 | Kolstad v. American Dental Association | Decision |

Chronology and source corrections: *Strickler* and *Grupo Mexicano* share the June 17 baseline with the previously rendered *NASA v. FLRA* decision; *Martin* and *Jefferson County* share a common pre-June 21 baseline; and the five June 22 decisions use a common pre-June 22 baseline and do not influence one another. The official record shows that the Fourth Circuit in *Strickler* relied on procedural default and an alternative merits rejection rather than accepting cause; *Grupo Mexicano* was historically reversed and remanded; the Sixth Circuit in *Martin* exempted post-enactment work as well as pre-enactment work in pending cases; and the historical removal vote in *Jefferson County* was 5-4 while the Tax Injunction Act holding was unanimous. Kirkingburg received an experimental waiver after his discharge, although the basic federal standard remained operative and Albertson’s declined to rehire him. The historical Stevens writing in *Kolstad* was joined by Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer. These corrections do not alter Stone’s controlled positions or create an adjudicative blocker. `OT_1998CHUNK7.md` ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep527/usrep527263/usrep527263.pdf))

## Decisions and Dispositions

### Strickler v. Greene

Argued March 3, 1999 | Decided June 17, 1999  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Fourth Circuit; judgment below: federal habeas relief denied on procedural-default and alternative merits grounds, 149 F.3d 1170 (4th Cir. 1998). ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep527/usrep527263/usrep527263.pdf))  
Area of Law: Criminal procedure; habeas corpus; suppressed impeachment evidence

Questions Presented:

1. Whether the prosecution’s suppression of police notes and letters materially impeaching a central eyewitness violated *Brady* and whether the defaulted claim may be heard in federal habeas.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 6-3.  
Supporting: Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: Stone, Kennedy, Souter.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stevens | O’Connor, Scalia, Ginsburg, Breyer in full; Stone, Kennedy, Souter as to cause and conviction materiality; Thomas as to the judgment-supporting no-materiality analysis | Supports affirmance | Scope stated by issue |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Souter | Stone in full; Kennedy as to penalty-phase materiality | Concurs in affirmance of the conviction and dissents from affirmance of the death sentence | Complete writing for Stone; penalty-phase portion for Kennedy |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm the denial of relief from the conviction, reverse the denial of relief from the death sentence, and remand.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court as to cause and conviction materiality and joins Souter’s concurrence in part and dissent in part in full.  
Announced ground: Suppression and reasonable reliance on the prosecution’s open-file representation establish cause. The withheld materials do not undermine confidence in guilt, but their cumulative effect on the prosecution’s portrayal of Strickler as the dominant aggressor creates a reasonable probability of a different capital-sentencing recommendation.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1A: Cause for the procedural default

Holding and operative rule: Governmental suppression of favorable evidence, combined with reasonable defense reliance on the prosecution’s representation that its file was open and complete, establishes cause for failure to raise the resulting *Brady* claim earlier. (joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer).

Reasoning: The prosecution withheld police notes and correspondence that were not available through any independent source known to the defense. Trial and postconviction counsel reasonably treated the Commonwealth’s open-file representation as fulfillment of its disclosure obligation, and the Commonwealth reinforced that understanding during the earlier proceedings. The default therefore did not result from an informed strategic decision, lack of diligence after discovery of the factual basis, or an available claim counsel simply chose not to pursue. Thomas agrees that the judgment should be affirmed but does not join this cause determination. ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep527/usrep527263/usrep527263.pdf))

##### Question 1B: Materiality to the conviction

Holding and operative rule: The suppressed impeachment evidence does not create a reasonable probability of a different guilt verdict when assessed cumulatively with the complete record. (joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer).

Reasoning: The notes and letters would have substantially impeached Stoltzfus by exposing uncertainty, suggestive interviewing, failures of recollection, and her dependence on investigators. They therefore were favorable and should have been disclosed. Independent evidence nevertheless connected Strickler to the abduction, robbery, murder, disposal of the body, and possession of the victim’s property. The Court does not ask whether that remaining evidence was merely sufficient. It asks whether the suppressed material, considered cumulatively, undermines confidence in the actual guilt determination. All nine Justices conclude that it does not.

##### Question 1C: Materiality to the death sentence

Holding and operative rule: The suppressed evidence does not create a reasonable probability that the sentencing jury would have declined to recommend death.

Reasoning: Six Justices conclude that the capital recommendation rested on evidence of vileness, future dangerousness, Strickler’s conduct before and after the murder, and other proof independent of Stoltzfus’s account. The prosecution did not rely on her testimony during its penalty-phase closing argument, and the majority concludes that severe impeachment of her account would not have altered the sentencing result. Stone, Kennedy, and Souter disagree because the guilt-phase portrayal of Strickler as the principal and most violent actor necessarily informed the jury’s later discretionary selection of death. That disagreement does not displace the six-Justice holding. ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep527/usrep527263/usrep527263.pdf))

#### Concurrences

Souter, joined by Stone and by Kennedy as to the penalty-phase discussion: Cause exists, and the suppressed evidence does not undermine confidence in the conviction. Souter and Stone would separately assess the evidence’s effect on capital selection and conclude that the death recommendation is not reliable.

#### Dissents

Souter, joined by Stone and by Kennedy as to the penalty-phase discussion: The withheld materials materially weakened the prosecution’s dominant-aggressor narrative, creating a reasonable probability that at least one juror would have rejected death. They would grant sentence-only habeas relief while leaving the conviction intact.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Kyles v. Whitley*: applied; all suppressed favorable evidence is assessed cumulatively against the complete record rather than through a remaining-evidence sufficiency test.

*Wood v. Bartholomew*: applied; suppressed information is considered for its concrete downstream impeachment value.

*Murray v. Carrier*: applied; governmental suppression and reasonable reliance that made the factual basis unavailable establish cause.

*Tuggle v. Netherland*: applied; capital-selection materiality is assessed separately from guilt and death eligibility, although the Court finds no sentencing prejudice on this record.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Fourth Circuit is affirmed.

Effect: Strickler’s capital-murder conviction and death sentence remain in force. The decision does not excuse future nondisclosure, make suppression harmless whenever independent evidence exists, or eliminate separate materiality analysis for guilt and capital selection.

---

### Grupo Mexicano de Desarrollo, S.A. v. Alliance Bond Fund, Inc.

Argued March 31, 1999 | Decided June 17, 1999  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Second Circuit; judgment below: preliminary injunction restraining transfer of unencumbered assets affirmed, 143 F.3d 688 (2d Cir. 1998). ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep527/usrep527308/usrep527308.pdf))  
Area of Law: Federal equity; prejudgment remedies; unsecured creditors

Questions Presented:

1. Whether a federal court exercising equity jurisdiction may freeze a defendant’s assets before judgment in an action by general unsecured creditors seeking only money damages.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 5-4.  
Supporting: Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas. Opposing: Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Scalia | Stone, O’Connor, Kennedy, Thomas in full; Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer as to the continued live controversy | Supports reversal and remand | Complete merits opinion for five Justices; threshold issue for all nine |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Ginsburg | Stevens, Souter, Breyer | Concurs that the controversy remains live and dissents from reversal | Complete writing |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: A general unsecured creditor seeking only money damages may not freeze a debtor’s unencumbered assets before judgment without a lien, equitable interest, statutory authorization, or another recognized source of prejudgment control.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Threshold: Continued controversy over the injunction bond

Holding and operative rule: Entry of final judgment on the contract claim did not moot review of the preliminary injunction because potential liability on the injunction bond depends on whether the asset restraint was lawfully issued. (joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer).

Reasoning: The preliminary injunction was later converted into permanent relief, but petitioners retained a possible claim against the bond for losses caused by a wrongful prejudgment restraint. The authority to freeze unrelated assets before judgment presents a question distinct from whether the bondholders ultimately prevailed on their contract claim. A decision on that authority therefore has a concrete consequence for the parties and is not advisory.

##### Question 1: Prejudgment restraint of assets unrelated to the claim

Holding and operative rule: A federal court may not, at the request of a general unsecured creditor seeking only money damages, freeze a defendant’s unencumbered assets before judgment absent a lien, equitable interest, statutory authorization, or another recognized source of prejudgment control.

Reasoning: Federal equity is flexible within the kinds of relief historically entrusted to it, but flexibility does not create a new form of attachment for an unsecured legal claim. The bondholders asserted no ownership, lien, trust, rescission right, equitable restitution claim, or fraudulent-transfer cause of action in the restrained assets. Their requested injunction served only to preserve collectability of a future legal judgment. Recognizing that power would alter creditor priority, bypass Rule 64 and state attachment law, and permit judicial reordering of debtor assets outside the enacted bankruptcy and fraudulent-transfer systems. The asserted risk of insolvency establishes practical need, but necessity does not supply the missing legal source. ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep527/usrep527308/usrep527308.pdf))

#### Concurrences

Ginsburg, joined by Stevens, Souter, and Breyer: The controversy over the injunction bond remains live. They would recognize equitable authority to preserve assets when dissipation threatens to make a probable money judgment ineffectual and the injunction protects rather than adjudicates ultimate priority.

#### Dissents

Ginsburg, joined by Stevens, Souter, and Breyer: Equity should not be frozen at the precise remedies administered in 1789 when the requested restraint is temporary, supported by probable success and irreparable harm, and needed to prevent deliberate frustration of the court’s judgment. They would affirm the injunction.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*De Beers Consolidated Mines, Ltd. v. United States*: followed; preliminary relief must protect an interest or remedy within the suit rather than unrelated assets serving only as security for a legal judgment.

*Deckert v. Independence Shares Corp.*: distinguished; rescission and recovery of a specific fund differ from an unsecured contract action for money damages.

Federal Rules of Civil Procedure 64 and 65: clarified; Rule 65 does not create a prejudgment attachment remedy, while Rule 64 preserves attachment and comparable remedies authorized by an independent source.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Second Circuit is reversed, and the case is remanded with instructions to dissolve the preliminary asset-restraint order and adjudicate any bond consequence under the governing law.

Effect: The bondholders retain their contract judgment but may not secure it through the unauthorized equitable freeze. Attachment, fraudulent-transfer relief, receivership, bankruptcy administration, protection of specific property in dispute, and another remedy supported by statute, rule, lien, or equitable interest remain available when their own requirements are satisfied.

---

### City of Dallas v. Dallas Fire Fighters Association

Argued May 4, 1999 | Decided June 18, 1999  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Fifth Circuit; judgment below: race-conscious and sex-conscious out-of-rank promotions to driver-engineer, lieutenant, and captain invalidated, with a separate deputy-chief disposition outside the grant, 150 F.3d 438 (5th Cir. 1998). ([FindLaw](https://caselaw.findlaw.com/court/us-5th-circuit/1253621.html))  
Area of Law: Equal protection; public employment; remedial classifications

Questions Presented:

1. What showing of the government’s own past discrimination is constitutionally sufficient to justify race-conscious or sex-conscious remedial promotions in public employment.
2. Once a sufficient remedial predicate exists, whether out-of-rank promotions that bypass higher-scoring qualified candidates are sufficiently fitted to the identified discrimination under the distinct constitutional standards governing race and sex.

#### Judgment

Affirmed in part, reversed in part, and remanded; votes vary by component.

| Judgment component | Disposition and vote | Supporting | Opposing | Relief or remand |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Race-conscious promotions | Reversed and remanded, 6-3 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | Relief resting on the conclusion that the challenged race-conscious promotions were unconstitutional shall be dissolved. |
| Sex-conscious promotions | Affirmed, 5-4 | Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Relief against the challenged sex-conscious promotions remains in force. |

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stone | O’Connor in full; Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer as to the racial remedial predicate and narrow tailoring; Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas as to the insufficient sex remedial predicate | Supports the component dispositions | Scope stated by issue |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Stevens | Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Concurs in reversal as to race and dissents from affirmance as to sex | Complete writing |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Scalia | Kennedy, Thomas | Concurs in affirmance as to sex and dissents from reversal as to race | Complete writing |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse as to the race-conscious promotions, affirm as to the sex-conscious promotions, and remand.  
Public role: Court author.  
Announced ground: Dallas established a strong evidentiary basis connecting its own racial discrimination to continuing effects in the internal promotion structure, and the temporary, banded, rank-specific racial remedy was narrowly tailored. The City established no comparable historical, qualified-pool, or causal predicate for sex-conscious promotions.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1A: Racial remedial predicate

Holding and operative rule: A public employer has a compelling remedial interest when objectively grounded evidence connects its own relevant racial discrimination to continuing effects in the challenged employment structure. Dallas established that predicate for the race-conscious promotions. (joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer).

Reasoning: The record concerns Dallas’s own fire department rather than generalized societal discrimination. The Department did not hire its first Black firefighter until 1969; Black and Latino firefighters comprised less than one percent of the Department in 1972; the Department of Justice found impermissible racial discrimination that year; and the City entered a 1976 consent decree directed to the effects of that discrimination. Minorities later entered the Department in greater numbers but remained sharply underrepresented in the upper ranks, which were populated entirely through an internal promotion pipeline. That combination supplies a source-linked basis for concluding that prior exclusion continued to affect access to the ranks at issue. A final liability judgment concerning each beneficiary is unnecessary. ([FindLaw](https://caselaw.findlaw.com/court/us-5th-circuit/1253621.html))

##### Question 1B: Sex remedial predicate

Holding and operative rule: Numerical absence from upper ranks, without comparable evidence of historical discrimination, the relevant qualified pool, a discriminatory employment practice, or a causal link to the challenged structure, does not establish the important remedial objective needed for sex-conscious promotions. Dallas did not establish that predicate. (joined by Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas).

Reasoning: The record showed that women were scarce or absent in several upper ranks, but it did not identify the proportion of qualified women in the relevant feeder ranks, a prior governmental finding of sex discrimination, a discriminatory promotion practice, or evidence connecting Dallas’s conduct to the disparity. Intermediate scrutiny is less demanding than strict scrutiny, but it still requires a genuine important objective rather than numerical balance for its own sake. The racial and sex classifications therefore cannot share one undifferentiated remedial predicate.

##### Question 2A: Fit of the race-conscious promotions

Holding and operative rule: Dallas’s challenged race-conscious promotions are narrowly tailored on this record because they operate only among qualified candidates within score bands treated as equivalent, are rank specific and temporary, cease when the identified imbalance is eliminated, cover no more than half of annual promotions, follow attempted race-neutral reforms, and impose neither discharge nor an absolute barrier to advancement. (joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer).

Reasoning: The City first changed examination practices, eliminated seniority additions, recruited minority candidates, validated examinations, and provided tutoring. The challenged promotions then selected only qualified candidates within bands intended to identify materially equivalent performance. The measure lasted five years, contained a rank-specific termination condition, and limited affirmative promotions to half of annual vacancies. Nonbeneficiaries remained employed, could compete for later vacancies, and faced no permanent bar. These limits connect the remedy to the identified pipeline effects while reducing the individualized burden. The Court does not approve the plan as a whole or any selection outside the established bands. ([FindLaw](https://caselaw.findlaw.com/court/us-5th-circuit/1253621.html))

##### Question 2B: Fit of the sex-conscious promotions

Not reached; the absence of a constitutionally sufficient sex-remedial predicate independently defeats those promotions.

#### Concurrences

Stevens, joined by Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer: They agree that the racial evidence and the temporary banded remedy satisfy equal protection. They would also reverse the sex component because the Department’s historical exclusion, workforce data, and measured promotion structure together supplied an adequate predicate under intermediate scrutiny.

Scalia, joined by Kennedy and Thomas: They agree that the sex-conscious promotions lack an adequate remedial foundation. They would also affirm invalidation of the race-conscious promotions because the older consent decree and continuing disparities do not justify race-determinative displacement of identified higher-scoring candidates who were not shown to have caused the discrimination.

#### Dissents

Stevens, joined by Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer: They dissent from affirmance of the sex-conscious promotion judgment and would sustain both remedial classifications.

Scalia, joined by Kennedy and Thomas: They dissent from reversal of the race-conscious promotion judgment and would invalidate both classifications.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Adarand Constructors, Inc. v. Peña*: applied and clarified; a source-linked remedial predicate and narrow tailoring may sustain a temporary race-determinative public-employment measure.

*City of Richmond v. J.A. Croson Co.*: applied; a prior liability judgment is not indispensable when objective evidence strongly connects the government’s own discrimination to continuing effects in the relevant structure.

*Wygant v. Jackson Board of Education*: distinguished; temporary promotions among qualified candidates impose a lesser and more diffuse burden than race-based layoffs.

*Kelley v. Board of Trustees of the University of Illinois*: applied; sex-conscious restructuring requires demonstrated inequality causally connected to the governmental system, and numerical absence alone is insufficient.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Fifth Circuit is reversed as to the challenged race-conscious promotions, affirmed as to the challenged sex-conscious promotions, and the case is remanded with instructions to conform the declaratory and injunctive relief to those component holdings.

Effect: Dallas may retain the race-conscious promotions to driver-engineer, lieutenant, and captain adjudicated here. The challenged sex-conscious promotions remain unconstitutional. The affirmative-action plan as a whole, the deputy-chief appointment, promotions outside the established equivalence bands, Title VII claims, and Texas-law claims remain outside the judgment.

---

### Martin v. Hadix

Argued March 30, 1999 | Decided June 21, 1999  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Sixth Circuit; judgment below: the PLRA fee limits held inapplicable to both pre-enactment and post-enactment services in cases pending on the statute’s effective date, 143 F.3d 246 (6th Cir. 1998). ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep527/usrep527343/usrep527343.pdf))  
Area of Law: Statutory interpretation; retroactivity; attorney fees

Questions Presented:

1. Whether the Prison Litigation Reform Act’s attorney-fee limits apply to legal services performed before the Act’s effective date, after that date, or both in long-running prison litigation.

#### Judgment

Affirmed in part, reversed in part, and remanded; votes vary by component.

| Judgment component | Disposition and vote | Supporting | Opposing | Relief or remand |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Services completed before the PLRA’s effective date | Affirmed, 9-0 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | No Justice | Pre-enactment work shall be compensated under the law governing when it was performed. |
| Services performed after the PLRA’s effective date | Reversed and remanded, 7-2 | Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Breyer | Stevens, Ginsburg | Post-enactment work shall be compensated subject to the PLRA limits. |

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Stone, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Breyer in full; Scalia except the reliance-based portion; Stevens and Ginsburg as to pre-enactment services | Supports affirmance in part, reversal in part, and remand | Scope stated by temporal category |
| Concurrence | Scalia | No other Justice | Supports the component dispositions | Text-centered temporal analysis |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Ginsburg | Stevens | Concurs as to pre-enactment services and dissents as to post-enactment services | Complete writing |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm protection of fees for pre-enactment services, reverse the exemption for post-enactment services, and remand.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: Applying a reduced compensation rule to completed pre-enactment legal work attaches a new legal consequence to completed conduct. Applying the cap to services undertaken after enactment regulates future work even though the litigation began earlier.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1A: Services completed before enactment

Holding and operative rule: The PLRA fee cap does not govern legal services completed before its effective date because Congress supplied no clear retroactive command and application would reduce compensation for work already performed.

Reasoning: Counsel completed the relevant services under a legal regime permitting compensation at previously established market rates. Applying the later cap would attach a new economic consequence to completed professional conduct and would alter the legal effect of work already undertaken. Neither the fact that payment remained outstanding nor the continuation of the underlying litigation converts completed work into future conduct. Under the ordinary temporal sequence, the old compensation rules govern those services.

##### Question 1B: Services performed after enactment

Holding and operative rule: The PLRA fee cap governs legal services performed after its effective date in litigation filed before enactment. (joined by Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, and Breyer).

Reasoning: A statute does not operate retroactively merely because it applies in a case that began earlier. Attorneys undertaking monitoring and litigation work after the effective date do so while the new compensation rule is in force. The cap regulates the legal consequences of those future services rather than altering the compensation attached to completed conduct. The Sixth Circuit therefore erred by treating the filing date of the underlying institutional litigation as controlling every later fee request. The district court must separate the services according to when the work was actually performed. ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep527/usrep527343/usrep527343.pdf))

#### Concurrences

Scalia: The enacted effective date and the date on which each service was performed resolve the case. He agrees with the component dispositions without relying on broader expectations or reliance interests.

Ginsburg, joined by Stevens: The new cap does not reach services completed before enactment.

#### Dissents

Ginsburg, joined by Stevens: Counsel undertook continuing court-supervised representation in cases already governed by established fee arrangements and could not freely withdraw from the monitoring responsibility. They would hold the PLRA cap inapplicable to the post-enactment services in these pending cases as well.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Landgraf v. USI Film Products*: applied; the temporal inquiry turns on the conduct to which the new legal consequence attaches.

*Rivers v. Roadway Express, Inc.*: followed; statutory silence does not authorize new consequences for completed pre-enactment conduct.

*Lindh v. Murphy*: applied; express and omitted temporal instructions within the statutory structure inform the reach of the new rule.

*Bradley v. School Board of Richmond*: limited; application of current law does not displace the source-specific retroactivity inquiry governing completed conduct.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Sixth Circuit is affirmed as to services completed before April 26, 1996, reversed as to services performed after that date, and the case is remanded for allocation and calculation of fees by service date.

Effect: Pre-enactment work remains compensable under the prior regime. Post-enactment work is subject to the PLRA cap. The decision does not determine vested fee judgments, contractual entitlements, prevailing-party status, or the operation of a statute containing materially different temporal language.

---

### Jefferson County v. Acker

Argued March 29, 1999 | Decided June 21, 1999  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Eleventh Circuit; judgment below: federal-officer removal sustained and Jefferson County’s occupational tax held unconstitutional as applied to federal judges, 137 F.3d 1314 (11th Cir. 1998) (en banc). ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep527/usrep527423/usrep527423.pdf))  
Area of Law: Federal courts; intergovernmental tax immunity; federal-officer removal

Questions Presented:

1. Whether federal judges properly removed a county tax-collection action under the federal-officer statute and whether Jefferson County’s nondiscriminatory occupational tax may be applied to their judicial salaries.

#### Judgment

Affirmed in part, reversed in part, and remanded; votes vary by component.

| Judgment component | Disposition and vote | Supporting | Opposing | Relief or remand |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Federal-officer removal | Affirmed, 6-3 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Ginsburg, Breyer | Scalia, Souter, Thomas | The collection action remains in federal court. |
| Tax Injunction Act | Affirmed, 9-0 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | No Justice | Section 1341 does not bar adjudication of the defense. |
| Intergovernmental tax immunity | Reversed and remanded, 7-2 | Stone, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg | O’Connor, Breyer | The judges receive no federal immunity from the nondiscriminatory levy. |

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Ginsburg | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Breyer as to removal; Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Breyer as to the Tax Injunction Act; Stone, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas as to the tax merits | Supports the component dispositions | Scope stated by issue |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Scalia | Souter, Thomas | Concurs in the Tax Injunction Act and tax-merits rulings and dissents from the removal ruling | Complete writing |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Breyer | O’Connor | Concurs in the removal and Tax Injunction Act rulings and dissents from the tax-merits ruling | Complete writing |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to sustain removal, hold that the Tax Injunction Act does not bar adjudication, reverse the federal tax exemption, and remand.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: The judges asserted a colorable federal immunity defense connected to compensation received for official service, and the County itself initiated the collection actions. The levy operates as a nondiscriminatory compensation tax rather than a local license regulating performance of Article III duties.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1A: Federal-officer removal

Holding and operative rule: A federal judge may remove a county tax-collection action when the demand is sufficiently connected to compensation received for official service and the judge asserts a colorable federal immunity defense. Ultimate failure of the defense does not defeat removal. (joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Ginsburg, and Breyer).

Reasoning: Section 1442 creates an exception to the ordinary rule that a federal defense does not support removal. The judges are federal officers, the County sued them for nonpayment of a levy imposed on compensation earned through their judicial work, and they asserted that the tax unlawfully burdened performance of federal office. That defense is colorable even though it ultimately fails. The ordinance’s statement that covered persons may not engage in their occupation without payment supplies the required office nexus for threshold purposes. Scalia, Souter, and Thomas would require a closer connection to a specific official act and would remand the collection suits to state court. ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep527/usrep527423/usrep527423.pdf))

##### Question 1B: Tax Injunction Act

Holding and operative rule: The Tax Injunction Act does not bar a federal officer from asserting a federal defense after the taxing authority itself files a collection action.

Reasoning: Section 1341 prevents federal courts from enjoining, suspending, or restraining state tax collection when an adequate state remedy exists. It is directed to taxpayer-initiated anticipatory interference. Jefferson County began these actions to collect its tax. Adjudicating defenses in the collection suits does not restrain the initiation of state taxation; it resolves the liability the County itself placed before a court. All nine Justices agree that the Act does not require dismissal or remand on this ground.

##### Question 1C: Occupational tax on federal judges

Holding and operative rule: A nondiscriminatory levy measured by compensation may be collected from federal judges under 4 U.S.C. § 111 when it neither discriminates because of the federal source of pay nor regulates performance of the federal judicial function. (joined by Stone, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, and Ginsburg).

Reasoning: The practical operation of the ordinance, not its license terminology, controls. Jefferson County issues no professional permission, imposes no conditions on judicial decisionmaking, and uses the levy to raise revenue. Federal, state, and local officeholders working in the County are subject to the tax, and the record identifies no unfavorable treatment based on the federal source of compensation. The Public Salary Tax Act expressly permits such evenhanded taxation. The County may collect the levy, subject to any remaining calculation or ordinary procedural defense. ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep527/usrep527423/usrep527423.pdf))

#### Concurrences

Scalia, joined by Souter and Thomas: The Tax Injunction Act does not bar the defense, and the tax is constitutionally valid. They would hold removal improper because refusal to pay a generally applicable compensation tax is not an act performed under color of federal judicial office.

Breyer, joined by O’Connor: Removal is proper and the Tax Injunction Act presents no bar.

#### Dissents

Scalia, joined by Souter and Thomas: They dissent from the federal-officer removal holding and would require the tax defense to be adjudicated in the County’s chosen state forum.

Breyer, joined by O’Connor: Alabama’s network of occupational-license exemptions places federal judges at a structural disadvantage not shared by comparable state-licensed professionals. They would preserve the judgment exempting the judges from this levy.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Mesa v. California*: applied; federal-officer removal requires a colorable federal defense.

*Willingham v. Morgan*: applied; the federal office and the conduct targeted by the state action must have a sufficient causal connection.

*California v. Grace Brethren Church*: distinguished; taxpayer-initiated anticipatory relief differs from a defense in the taxing authority’s own collection action.

*Graves v. New York ex rel. O’Keefe*: followed; nondiscriminatory taxation of federal compensation does not itself burden the Federal Government unconstitutionally.

*Davis v. Michigan Department of Treasury*: applied; source-based discrimination remains prohibited, but no such discrimination is established here.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Eleventh Circuit is affirmed as to removal and the Tax Injunction Act, reversed as to intergovernmental tax immunity, and the case is remanded for calculation and enforcement of the nondiscriminatory tax subject to remaining procedural and calculation defenses.

Effect: The judges must pay the occupational tax on the same lawful terms governing comparable workers and officeholders. The decision does not authorize a levy that discriminates against federal compensation, conditions permission to hold federal office, or regulates performance of judicial duties.

---

### Sutton v. United Air Lines, Inc.

Argued April 28, 1999 | Decided June 22, 1999  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Tenth Circuit; judgment below: dismissal of the actual-disability and regarded-as claims affirmed, 130 F.3d 893 (10th Cir. 1997). ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep527/usrep527471/usrep527471.pdf))  
Area of Law: Disability law; mitigating measures; regarded-as disability

Questions Presented:

1. Whether severe myopia is assessed with or without corrective lenses in determining ADA disability and whether an employer’s uncorrected-vision standard supports a regarded-as claim.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 6-3.  
Supporting: O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg. Opposing: Stone, Stevens, Breyer.

| Judgment component | Disposition and vote | Supporting | Opposing | Relief or remand |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Actual-disability dismissal | Affirmed, 6-3 | O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg | Stone, Stevens, Breyer | The corrected-vision claim remains dismissed. |
| Regarded-as-working dismissal | Affirmed, 7-2 | Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg | Stevens, Breyer | Exclusion from the global-airline-pilot position does not state the pleaded working-limitation claim. |

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg in full; Stone as to the individualized mitigation rule and regarded-as-working analysis | Supports affirmance | Scope stated by issue |
| Concurrence | Ginsburg | No other Justice | Supports affirmance | Complete concurrence |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Stone | No other Justice | Concurs in the regarded-as-working judgment and dissents from the actual-disability judgment | Complete writing |
| Dissent | Stevens | Breyer | Would reverse | Complete dissent |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to vacate the actual-disability dismissal and remand for amendment and factual development, while affirming dismissal of the working and regarded-as-working theories based only on exclusion from one specialized position.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court as to the individualized mitigation rule and the regarded-as-working analysis and files a concurrence in part and dissent in part.  
Announced ground: Actual disability must be assessed through real individualized functioning, considering both the benefits and material burdens of correction. Effective lenses are relevant but should not bar amendment where the pleadings do not develop burden, unreliability, dependence, or interruption consequences; exclusion from one specialized pilot position does not establish broad occupational limitation.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1A: Measurement of actual disability

Holding and operative rule: Actual disability is assessed through the person’s real, present, individualized functioning with the mitigating measures actually used, including both their benefits and any material side effects, burden, unreliability, dependence, or interruption consequences proved in the case. (joined by Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, and Ginsburg).

Reasoning: The statutory phrase substantially limits is directed to the claimant’s actual operation rather than a hypothetical untreated state. Considering mitigation does not make successful treatment automatically dispositive because the treatment itself may create substantial limitations or may leave material residual effects. The inquiry remains claimant specific. Stevens and Breyer would instead assess the impairment without regard to mitigating measures because they understand the statutory coverage determination to precede examination of the claimant’s ability to function with treatment.

##### Question 1B: Application to the twins’ corrected vision

Holding and operative rule: The complaint fails to state an actual-disability claim because it alleges that corrective lenses provide 20/20 vision and that the twins function identically to persons without similar impairments. (joined by O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, and Ginsburg).

Reasoning: Six Justices conclude that the plaintiffs’ own allegations establish effective correction without a pleaded substantial residual limitation or treatment burden. They therefore see no factual gap requiring discovery or amendment. Stone would permit limited amendment because the complaint was framed before the governing measurement rule was fixed and does not address dependence, interruption consequences, or other material limits of correction. Stevens and Breyer would treat the severe uncorrected impairment as sufficient coverage. The six-Justice application controls. ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep527/usrep527471/usrep527471.pdf))

##### Question 1C: Regarded-as limitation in working

Holding and operative rule: A perceived limitation in working requires exclusion from a class or broad range of jobs, not inability to satisfy one employer’s standard for one specialized position. United’s rejection of the twins as global airline pilots does not state the pleaded regarded-as-working claim. (joined by Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, and Ginsburg).

Reasoning: United’s standard concerns one position requiring a particular combination of duties and visual criteria. The complaint does not allege that United regarded the twins as unable to serve as regional pilots, instructors, copilots, or in another broad group of occupations using their skills. A physical employment criterion is not itself an ADA violation unless the employer treats a real or perceived impairment as substantially limiting a major life activity. The Court assumes without deciding that working is a major life activity. ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep527/usrep527471/usrep527471.pdf))

#### Concurrences

Ginsburg: Congress’s numerical and historical findings confirm that ordinary impairments fully corrected without material limitation do not automatically fall within the Act’s protected class.

Stone: He agrees that actual functioning and the full effects of correction govern and that the single specialized position does not establish a working limitation. He would permit amendment and factual development before finally dismissing the actual-disability theory.

#### Dissents

Stone: The pleadings are too thin to establish that correction imposes no material practical burden, and the plaintiffs should receive a confined opportunity to amend under the newly fixed rule.

Stevens, joined by Breyer: Disability should be determined without subtracting the effects of mitigating measures. They would treat the twins’ severe myopia as an impairment covered by the ADA and reverse the dismissal without deciding qualification or the validity of United’s standard.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Bragdon v. Abbott*: clarified; individualized disability analysis considers the claimant’s actual functioning with mitigation while accounting for proved residual limitations and treatment burdens.

Event-date occupational-breadth principles: established; inability to perform a single specialized job does not constitute a substantial limitation in working or a regarded-as-working disability.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Tenth Circuit is affirmed.

Effect: The twins’ ADA action remains dismissed. The decision does not establish that United’s vision standard is necessary, decide qualification or accommodation, treat every corrected condition as outside the ADA, or foreclose coverage when mitigation leaves material limitations or burdens.

---

### Albertson’s, Inc. v. Kirkingburg

Argued April 28, 1999 | Decided June 22, 1999  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit; judgment below: summary judgment for Albertson’s reversed and Kirkingburg’s disability and qualification claims permitted to proceed, 143 F.3d 1228 (9th Cir. 1998). ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep527/usrep527555/usrep527555.pdf))  
Area of Law: Disability law; monocular vision; federal safety qualifications

Questions Presented:

1. Whether monocular vision constitutes an ADA disability and whether an employer must consider a Department of Transportation waiver program when determining whether a commercial driver is qualified.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Souter | Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg in full; Stevens and Breyer as to the operative federal qualification and waiver-program disposition | Supports reversal and remand | Scope stated by issue |
| Concurrence | Thomas | No other Justice | Supports reversal and remand | Complete concurrence |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand with instructions to enter judgment for Albertson’s on qualification.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: Monocular vision requires individualized proof that accounts for actual adaptation and practical visual effects. The experimental waiver program did not amend the binding federal standard or make a driver presently qualified merely because waiver was possible.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1A: Individualized disability and natural compensation

Holding and operative rule: Monocular vision is not an ADA disability as a matter of law merely from diagnosis. The claimant must prove a substantial limitation through individualized evidence that includes actual adaptation, depth perception, field of vision, and practical visual effects. (joined by Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, and Ginsburg).

Reasoning: Monocular individuals may experience materially different functional effects. Some develop subconscious visual techniques that compensate for lost binocular cues, while others remain substantially limited in seeing. Natural adaptation must be considered because it affects actual functioning, but it is not categorically dispositive against coverage. The Ninth Circuit erred by effectively treating the diagnosis itself as establishing disability. Because qualification independently resolves the judgment, the Court leaves Kirkingburg’s ultimate disability status open. ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep527/usrep527555/usrep527555.pdf))

##### Question 1B: Federal vision standard and experimental waiver

Holding and operative rule: An experimental waiver program does not amend the operative federal commercial-driving standard or require a private employer to treat the experimental route as replacing that standard. An employer may insist on the certification the binding rule demands.

Reasoning: The DOT basic rule required specified visual acuity in each eye and binocularly. Kirkingburg did not satisfy that standard when Albertson’s discharged him. The waiver experiment created a separate route for selected drivers and later issued Kirkingburg a waiver, but it did not rewrite the general standard or impose a duty on every employer to participate in the experiment. Albertson’s therefore could rely on the basic federal qualification without independently defending the Government’s rule as its own safety judgment. A materially different case would arise if the governing law made a waiver fully applicable to the employer and the employer ignored it or added an unsupported criterion. ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep527/usrep527555/usrep527555.pdf))

#### Concurrences

Thomas: He agrees that Albertson’s may rely on the operative federal standard and that the experimental program does not replace it. He would reserve broader questions concerning whether the ADA requires an employer to justify a binding governmental safety rule under the Act’s business-necessity or direct-threat provisions.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Bragdon v. Abbott*: applied and clarified; disability remains an individualized inquiry, and natural adaptation is relevant without becoming categorically dispositive.

Federal commercial-driving vision regulation: applied; a binding qualification has independent legal force unless the governing scheme makes an exception or waiver applicable.

Experimental waiver program: clarified; eligibility to seek a waiver does not itself amend the general rule or establish present qualification.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit is reversed, and the case is remanded with instructions to reinstate summary judgment for Albertson’s on the qualification claim.

Effect: Kirkingburg cannot proceed on the theory that the experimental waiver program itself displaced Albertson’s right to use the operative federal qualification. His ultimate disability status remains unresolved, and the decision does not authorize stereotypes, employer-created safety criteria lacking lawful support, or disregard of a waiver made binding and applicable by governing law.

---

### Olmstead v. L.C.

Argued April 21, 1999 | Decided June 22, 1999  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Eleventh Circuit; judgment below: unnecessary institutionalization held actionable under Title II and the case remanded for a restricted cost-based fundamental-alteration inquiry, 138 F.3d 893 (11th Cir. 1998). ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep527/usrep527581/usrep527581.pdf))  
Area of Law: Disability law; public services; unnecessary institutionalization

Questions Presented:

1. Whether unjustified institutional isolation of persons with mental disabilities constitutes discrimination under Title II of the ADA and what limits the duty to provide community-based services.

#### Judgment

Affirmed in part, vacated in part, and remanded, 7-2.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: Scalia, Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Ginsburg | Stone, O’Connor, Souter, Breyer in full; Stevens as to the discrimination rule and governing placement conditions | Supports affirmance in part, vacatur in part, and remand | Scope stated by issue |
| Concurrence in part and concurrence in the judgment | Stevens | No other Justice | Concurs in substantial affirmance and the judgment | Discrimination rule and narrower treatment of the remand |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Kennedy | Breyer as to the statutory caution and institutional context | Concurs in the judgment | Record-development and state-discretion ground |
| Dissent | Thomas | Scalia | Would reverse | Complete dissent |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm in part, vacate in part, and remand.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: Unjustified institutional isolation is discriminatory when state professionals determine community placement appropriate, the person does not oppose it, and placement can be reasonably accommodated without fundamentally altering the State’s service system. A genuine, effectively working placement plan and reasonable waiting list may establish the fundamental-alteration defense.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1A: Unjustified institutional isolation

Holding and operative rule: Unjustified institutional isolation of a qualified person with a disability is discrimination under Title II when state treatment professionals determine that community placement is appropriate, the affected person does not oppose community treatment, and the placement can be reasonably accommodated. (joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer).

Reasoning: Title II prohibits discriminatory administration of public services, and the Attorney General’s integration regulation requires services in the most integrated setting appropriate to the individual’s needs. Unnecessary confinement perpetuates assumptions that persons so isolated are incapable of participating in community life and severely restricts ordinary social, educational, occupational, and cultural activity. L.C. and E.W. remained institutionalized after the State’s own professionals concluded that community care was appropriate, and neither opposed transfer. The rule does not compel community placement contrary to medical judgment or informed personal choice. ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep527/usrep527581/usrep527581.pdf))

##### Question 1B: Fundamental alteration and statewide administration

Holding and operative rule: A State need not make a modification that would fundamentally alter its service system. The inquiry considers available resources, the needs of others receiving disability services, and whether the State maintains a comprehensive, effectively working placement plan with a waiting list that moves at a reasonable pace not controlled by efforts to keep institutions fully populated. (joined by Stone, O’Connor, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer).

Reasoning: Title II does not require a court to dismantle an entire mental-health system or order immediate placement without regard to competing needs. A State may show that the requested relief, viewed in the context of its overall obligations and resources, would fundamentally alter lawful services. General fiscal preference, an inert waiting list, or a conclusory assertion of limited funds is insufficient. Conversely, a concrete plan that allocates resources evenhandedly, expands community options, and advances eligible persons at a reasonable pace may establish lawful administration. The Eleventh Circuit’s remand was too narrowly focused on the incremental cost of serving these two plaintiffs and must be broadened. ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep527/usrep527581/usrep527581.pdf))

#### Concurrences

Stevens: He agrees that unjustified isolation is discrimination and that the State may prove a fundamental alteration. He would simply affirm the Eleventh Circuit’s existing remand rather than reformulate its inquiry, but joins the Court’s judgment because there are not five votes for that disposition.

Kennedy, joined by Breyer as to part of the writing: The statute requires caution before federal courts prescribe statewide resource allocation. He concurs in remand so the lower courts may determine whether the existing summary-judgment record establishes discrimination and give appropriate weight to Georgia’s systemwide costs and responsibilities.

#### Dissents

Thomas, joined by Scalia: Title II prohibits unequal treatment within a public service but does not make the institutional setting itself discriminatory when the State provides the same institutional services to persons with the same needs. They would reverse rather than infer an affirmative community-placement obligation from the general statutory language.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Pennsylvania Department of Corrections v. Yeskey*: extended; Title II’s unqualified coverage of public services includes state mental-health systems and their placement decisions.

*Bragdon v. Abbott*: applied; placement and any asserted justification require individualized rather than stereotyped assessment.

*School Board of Nassau County v. Arline*: applied by analogy; exclusion based on unsupported assumptions about disability is distinguished from individualized program administration.

Title II integration and reasonable-modification regulations: established as governing the conditions and limits of community-placement claims.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Eleventh Circuit is affirmed insofar as it recognizes unnecessary institutionalization as actionable discrimination, vacated insofar as it confines the fundamental-alteration inquiry, and the case is remanded for determination of appropriate placement and relief under the complete systemwide standard.

Effect: Georgia must justify continued institutional placement under the conditions stated by the Court. The plaintiffs receive no automatic entitlement to a particular residence or immediate transfer, and the State may rely on a genuine, effectively working plan that allocates services reasonably among eligible persons.

---

### Murphy v. United Parcel Service, Inc.

Argued April 27, 1999 | Decided June 22, 1999  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Tenth Circuit; judgment below: summary judgment for UPS affirmed on the actual-disability and regarded-as theories, 141 F.3d 1185 (10th Cir. 1998). ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep527/usrep527516/usrep527516.pdf))  
Area of Law: Disability law; medicated hypertension; regarded-as working limitation

Questions Presented:

1. Whether hypertension controlled by medication is assessed in its medicated state for ADA coverage and whether termination under a Department of Transportation blood-pressure standard supports actual-disability or regarded-as liability.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 7-2.  
Supporting: Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg. Opposing: Stevens, Breyer.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Stone, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Stevens | Breyer | Would reverse | Complete dissent |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: A condition that, with the measures actually used, produces no substantial limitation shown on the record is not an actual disability merely because its untreated form would be severe; proved residual limitations and treatment burdens remain relevant. Exclusion from one mechanic position combined with commercial driving does not establish a perceived limitation in a class or broad range of jobs.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1A: Actual disability with medication

Holding and operative rule: The ADA assesses Murphy’s hypertension in its actual medicated operation. A condition that, with the measures actually used, produces no substantial limitation shown on the record is not an actual disability merely because its untreated form would be severe; proved residual limitations, side effects, dependence, and treatment burdens remain relevant.

Reasoning: Murphy’s untreated blood pressure was extremely high, but the evidence before the lower courts established that medication allowed him to function normally in ordinary daily activities. The record identified no material residual limitation, adverse effect, or treatment burden sufficient to create a triable substantial limitation. The seriousness of the untreated condition therefore cannot substitute for the statutory requirement of an actual substantial limitation. The decision creates no categorical rule that hypertension is never disabling and reserves a record showing substantial limitations that persist because of, or despite, treatment. ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep527/usrep527516/usrep527516.pdf))

##### Question 1B: Regarded-as limitation in working

Holding and operative rule: An employer does not regard an employee as substantially limited in working merely by treating him as unable to perform one job configuration requiring a particular license. The perceived restriction must concern a class or broad range of jobs.

Reasoning: UPS believed Murphy could not satisfy the federal certification associated with the commercial-driving component of its mechanic position. The record showed that he remained able to perform mechanic work not requiring commercial driving and that numerous jobs used his mechanical skills without the disputed certification. UPS therefore regarded him, at most, as unable to perform one particular combination of duties. The Court does not decide whether UPS correctly interpreted every DOT option, whether Murphy could obtain temporary certification, or whether another qualification or accommodation claim would succeed. ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep527/usrep527516/usrep527516.pdf))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

Stevens, joined by Breyer: Severe hypertension should be evaluated without subtracting the effects of medication. They would hold Murphy covered by the ADA and remand for consideration of qualification, the certification issue, and remaining defenses.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Bragdon v. Abbott*: clarified; individualized disability analysis concerns actual operation with treatment while preserving consideration of material residual effects and treatment burdens.

Regarded-as working limitation: established; exclusion from one licensed job configuration does not establish perceived inability to perform a class or broad range of jobs.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Tenth Circuit is affirmed.

Effect: UPS retains summary judgment on the actual-disability and regarded-as-working theories adjudicated here. The decision does not determine the correctness of UPS’s DOT interpretation, a separate accommodation theory, or a claim supported by broader perceived occupational exclusion or proved treatment burdens.

---

### Kolstad v. American Dental Association

Argued March 1, 1999 | Decided June 22, 1999  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the District of Columbia Circuit; judgment below: punitive-damages instruction denied for lack of independently egregious discrimination and the judgment affirmed en banc, 139 F.3d 958 (D.C. Cir. 1998). ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep527/usrep527526/usrep527526.pdf))  
Area of Law: Employment discrimination; punitive damages; employer attribution

Questions Presented:

1. What mental state permits punitive damages under Title VII and when an employer is vicariously responsible for a managerial employee’s discriminatory decision.

#### Judgment

Vacated and remanded, 8-1.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Stone, Scalia, Kennedy in full; Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer as to the punitive mental state; Thomas as to managerial attribution and the good-faith limitation | Supports vacatur and remand | Scope stated by issue |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Thomas | No other Justice | Concurs in the employer-attribution analysis and dissents from vacatur | Complete writing |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Stevens | Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Concurs in rejection of an egregiousness requirement and dissents from adoption of the employer good-faith limitation in this case | Complete writing |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to vacate and remand.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: Malice and reckless indifference concern the managerial actor’s awareness of federal rights, not an undefined additional level of outrageousness. An employer avoids vicarious punitive liability only by showing that the act was contrary to meaningful, good-faith compliance efforts reasonably designed and actually implemented to prevent and correct Title VII violations.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1A: Punitive-damages mental state

Holding and operative rule: Title VII punitive damages may be available when a managerial actor intentionally discriminates with malice or reckless indifference to the claimant’s federally protected rights. No separate requirement of independently egregious conduct applies. (joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer).

Reasoning: Section 1981a creates one threshold for intentional discrimination and a higher threshold for punitive damages, but the higher threshold is supplied by the enacted terms malice and reckless indifference. Those terms focus on the actor’s knowledge that the conduct may violate federal law, not on whether a court later labels the conduct especially outrageous. Egregious behavior may be strong evidence of the required state of mind, while an actor unaware of the relevant federal prohibition may lack it despite intentional discrimination. Evidence that the decisionmaker knew Title VII’s command yet selected a man because of sex could permit a punitive finding. ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep527/usrep527526/usrep527526.pdf))

##### Question 1B: Managerial attribution and good-faith compliance

Holding and operative rule: A managerial employee’s qualifying conduct is ordinarily attributable to the employer within the scope of employment, but the employer avoids vicarious punitive liability when it proves that the discriminatory act was contrary to meaningful, good-faith efforts reasonably designed and actually implemented to comply with Title VII. (joined by Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas).

Reasoning: Punitive liability serves deterrence but should not punish an employer that has made a genuine organizational effort to prevent the very violation committed by a managerial employee acting against that system. A written policy alone is insufficient. The evidence may include training, accessible reporting channels, monitoring, enforcement, investigation, and corrective action. The limitation does not protect an employer whose policy is inert, whose managerial conduct implements company policy, or whose compliance structure is a pretext. The record concerning the Association’s policies and their actual implementation is disputed and must be resolved on remand. ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep527/usrep527526/usrep527526.pdf))

#### Concurrences

Thomas: Agency principles place an important limit on employer punitive liability, and a managerial act contrary to genuine compliance efforts may not be attributed for that purpose.

Stevens, joined by Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer: The Court correctly rejects an extra egregiousness requirement. They would remand on the statutory mental-state issue alone and leave the employer-attribution question for a case in which the parties briefed it and the record squarely presents it.

#### Dissents

Thomas: The two-tier remedial structure implies that punitive damages should be reserved for especially egregious intentional discrimination. He would affirm the denial of the instruction.

Stevens, joined by Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer: They dissent from adoption of the employer good-faith limitation in this case, not from the judgment vacating the egregiousness-based ruling.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Smith v. Wade*: applied; reckless indifference focuses on conscious disregard of federally protected rights.

*Faragher v. City of Boca Raton* and *Burlington Industries, Inc. v. Ellerth*: applied by analogy and clarified; agency principles inform attribution, but punitive liability includes a bounded defense for meaningful and actually implemented compliance efforts.

Title VII punitive-damages eligibility: established; intentional discrimination alone is insufficient, independently egregious conduct is unnecessary, and malice or reckless indifference to federal rights supplies the statutory threshold.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the District of Columbia Circuit is vacated, and the case is remanded for determination whether the decisionmaker acted with malice or reckless indifference to Kolstad’s federal rights and whether the conduct is attributable to the Association under the bounded good-faith rule.

Effect: Kolstad receives no punitive award by virtue of this judgment, but she is entitled to have punitive eligibility considered under the correct statutory standard. The Association’s written policy is relevant but not conclusive, and statutory caps, proof of intentional discrimination, ordinary agency limits, and constitutional review remain applicable.
