Chronology and source corrections: All four matters use the same pre-June 23 entering-law baseline. The official reports describe the historical judgment in *Florida Prepaid* as reversed and remanded, not simply reversed; identify a separate Stevens dissent in *College Savings Bank* in addition to Breyer’s four-Justice dissent; and show that *Ortiz* was historically decided 7-2 rather than unanimously, with Souter’s Court opinion joined by seven Justices, a Rehnquist concurrence joined by Scalia and Kennedy, and a Breyer dissent joined by Stevens. These corrections do not alter Stone’s approved Section II or create an adjudicative blocker. `OT_1998CHUNK8.md` ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep527/usrep527627/usrep527627.pdf))

## Decisions and Dispositions

### Florida Prepaid Postsecondary Education Expense Board v. College Savings Bank

Argued April 20, 1999 | Decided June 23, 1999  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Federal Circuit; judgment below:  
denial of Florida Prepaid’s sovereign-immunity defense affirmed and the patent action allowed to proceed, 148 F.3d 1343 (Fed. Cir. 1998).  
Decided with *College Savings Bank v. Florida Prepaid Postsecondary Education Expense Board*.  
Area of Law: Federalism; patent law; state sovereign immunity ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep527/usrep527627/usrep527627.pdf))

Questions Presented:

1. Whether Congress validly subjected nonconsenting States to private patent-infringement damages actions through the Patent and Plant Variety Protection Remedy Clarification Act.

#### Judgment

Vacated and remanded, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stone | Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports vacatur and remand | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to vacate and remand.  
Public role: Court author.  
Announced ground: The Patent Remedy Act validly abrogates state sovereign immunity as applied to intentional or reckless infringement that deprives the patent owner of property without constitutionally adequate state process or relief. The unresolved constitutional predicates must be determined before the federal damages remedy may proceed.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Claim-specific Fourteenth Amendment abrogation

Holding and operative rule: The Patent Remedy Act validly abrogates state sovereign immunity as applied to a claim proving intentional or reckless patent infringement and the absence or inadequacy of constitutionally sufficient state process or relief. Because College Savings alleged willful infringement and the remedial-adequacy issue was not adjudicated, neither categorical enforcement nor categorical dismissal is proper.

Reasoning: A patent is property protected by the Due Process Clause, but every unauthorized use of patented subject matter is not independently a constitutional deprivation. Innocent or negligent infringement does not establish the required constitutional culpability, and even deliberate infringement does not violate due process when the State supplies a constitutionally adequate procedure or remedy. Simulated *City of Boerne v. Flores* requires the Court to examine the particular conduct supporting the statutory claim. When that conduct independently violates the Fourteenth Amendment, the remedy Congress enacted under § 5 attaches to the claim without regard to the statute’s broader applications. College Savings alleged that Florida Prepaid willfully infringed its patent, but the lower courts did not determine culpability under the constitutional standard or the availability and adequacy of Florida remedies. College Savings must therefore receive an opportunity to plead and prove both predicates. Patent validity, statutory infringement, causation, damages, and the adequacy of any particular state remedy remain unresolved. ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep527/usrep527627/usrep527627.pdf))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*City of Boerne v. Flores*: applied and clarified; a federal remedy validly enacted under § 5 attaches claim specifically when the state conduct proved independently violates the Fourteenth Amendment.

*Fitzpatrick v. Bitzer*: followed; valid Fourteenth Amendment enforcement may subject a nonconsenting State to the remedy Congress supplied.

*Parratt v. Taylor*, *Hudson v. Palmer*, and *Zinermon v. Burch*: applied; culpability and the existence of constitutionally adequate state process or relief are material to the alleged property deprivation.

*Seminole Tribe v. Florida*: distinguished; its Article I no-damages boundary does not prevent Congress from supplying monetary relief for independently unconstitutional state conduct under § 5.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Federal Circuit is vacated, and the case is remanded with instructions to return the matter to the district court so College Savings may plead and prove intentional or reckless infringement and the absence or inadequacy of constitutionally sufficient state remedies; the direct-State claim shall be dismissed if either predicate fails.

Effect: Florida Prepaid is not categorically immune from a patent claim establishing both constitutional predicates, but proof of the Patent Act violation alone is insufficient. No determination is made concerning patent validity, infringement, causation, damages, or equitable relief.

---

### College Savings Bank v. Florida Prepaid Postsecondary Education Expense Board

Argued April 20, 1999 | Decided June 23, 1999  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Third Circuit; judgment below:  
dismissal of the Lanham Act action on sovereign-immunity grounds affirmed, 131 F.3d 353 (3d Cir. 1997).  
Decided with *Florida Prepaid Postsecondary Education Expense Board v. College Savings Bank*.  
Area of Law: Federalism; state sovereign immunity; Lanham Act ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep527/usrep527666/usrep527666.pdf))

Questions Presented:

1. Whether Congress validly removed state sovereign immunity for a private Lanham Act false-advertising action and whether a State waives immunity by engaging in interstate commercial activity regulated by federal law.

#### Judgment

Affirmed in part, reversed in part, and remanded; votes vary by component.

| Judgment component | Disposition and vote | Supporting | Opposing | Relief or remand |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Damages and other retrospective monetary relief | Affirmed, 5-4 | Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | The dismissal of damages, restitution, and other retrospective monetary relief remains in force. |
| Prospective Lanham Act claim | Reversed and remanded, 5-4 | Stone, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | The prospective claim shall proceed subject to standing, recurrence, statutory merits, causation, and equitable-remedy requirements. |

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stone | O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas as to retrospective monetary relief and constructive waiver; Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer as to prospective direct-State relief | Supports affirmance in part, reversal in part, and remand | Scope stated by issue |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Scalia | O’Connor, Kennedy, Thomas | Concurs in part and dissents in part | Concurs in the damages and constructive-waiver holdings; dissents from the prospective-relief holding |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Breyer | Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg | Concurs in part and dissents in part | Concurs in the prospective-relief holding; dissents from the damages and constructive-waiver holdings |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm dismissal of damages and other retrospective monetary relief, reverse dismissal of the prospective Lanham Act claim, and remand.  
Public role: Court author.  
Announced ground: The false-advertising claim alleges competitive injury rather than an independent Fourteenth Amendment property deprivation, and Article I does not authorize retrospective monetary liability against the nonconsenting State. Ordinary commercial activity is not consent to suit, but simulated *Seminole Tribe* permits the expressly authorized prospective direct-State claim.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Retrospective Lanham Act damages

Holding and operative rule: The Trademark Remedy Clarification Act does not authorize damages or other retrospective monetary relief against Florida Prepaid on the claim alleged. Competitive loss caused by a rival’s statements about its own product is not an independent Fourteenth Amendment property deprivation, and Article I does not authorize the retrospective treasury remedy. (joined by Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas).

Reasoning: College Savings alleges that Florida Prepaid’s descriptions of its own tuition program diverted customers and impaired College Savings’ commercial position. The alleged injury does not involve Florida’s appropriation, destruction, or exclusionary control of an asset belonging to College Savings. It therefore supplies no Due Process Clause violation to which simulated *City of Boerne* could attach a § 5 damages remedy. Congress unmistakably made States subject to the Lanham Act and supplied the ordinary remedies, but statutory clarity does not expand the constitutional source of remedial authority. Simulated *Seminole Tribe* recognizes Article I authority for an expressly enacted prospective direct-State action while withholding retrospective damages. The practical importance of uniform false-advertising enforcement cannot enlarge that boundary. A materially different claim involving appropriation or destruction of protected property, valid Reconstruction-amendment legislation, genuine consent, or federal public enforcement remains governed by its own source. ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep527/usrep527666/usrep527666.pdf))

##### Question 2: Constructive waiver through commercial activity

Holding and operative rule: A State does not waive sovereign immunity merely by engaging in interstate commercial activity after Congress announces that private suit will follow. Waiver requires an unequivocal and voluntary manifestation of the State’s own consent, and *Parden v. Terminal Railway* is overruled to the extent it recognizes constructive waiver from regulated market participation. (joined by Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas).

Reasoning: Waiver concerns the State’s voluntary legal act, not Congress’s declaration of the consequences it wishes to attach to regulated conduct. Florida enacted no consent, voluntarily invoked no federal forum, accepted no separately valid funding condition, and made no litigation commitment. Its decision to market a tuition program therefore establishes regulatory coverage but does not communicate consent to private monetary suit. Treating continued participation as waiver would allow Congress to reproduce an otherwise unavailable abrogation simply by describing regulated conduct as acceptance of a condition. Express consent, voluntary invocation of federal jurisdiction, and acceptance of a separately valid condition giving clear notice of the suit and remedy remain distinct and sufficient routes.

##### Question 3: Expressly authorized prospective relief

Holding and operative rule: The Trademark Remedy Clarification Act validly authorizes a prospective Lanham Act action directly against a State when the plaintiff establishes an ongoing or imminently threatened statutory violation and satisfies ordinary Article III and equitable-remedy requirements. (joined by Stone, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer).

Reasoning: Simulated *Seminole Tribe* permits Congress, acting through an enumerated commerce power, to authorize a prospective action directly against a State when the statute unmistakably identifies the State as defendant, creates the cause of action, and supplies the prospective remedy. The Trademark Remedy Clarification Act satisfies those source and clarity requirements. This route does not depend on constructive waiver and does not authorize retrospective compensation. The present record does not conclusively establish that Florida Prepaid’s challenged representations continued or were likely to recur when relief would issue. On remand, College Savings must therefore prove injury, recurrence or imminent threat, falsity, materiality, causation, and entitlement to a properly confined injunction or declaration. ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep527/usrep527666/usrep527666.pdf))

#### Concurrences

Scalia, joined by O’Connor, Kennedy, and Thomas: Ordinary commercial activity is not consent to private suit, and the retrospective monetary claims are barred. They would also affirm dismissal of the prospective claim because they adhere to their objection to simulated *Seminole Tribe*’s recognition of Article I abrogation.

Breyer, joined by Stevens, Souter, and Ginsburg: Simulated *Seminole Tribe* requires reinstatement of the expressly authorized prospective claim. They would also preserve *Parden* where Congress clearly identifies the consequence of voluntary market participation and would permit the monetary claim on that waiver ground.

#### Dissents

Scalia, joined by O’Connor, Kennedy, and Thomas: They dissent from reversal and remand of the prospective claim and would affirm dismissal of the action in full.

Breyer, joined by Stevens, Souter, and Ginsburg: They dissent from affirmance of the damages dismissal and from overruling *Parden*.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Seminole Tribe v. Florida*: extended; its rule authorizing an unmistakably enacted prospective direct-State action under an enumerated commerce power applies to the prospective Lanham Act remedy.

*City of Boerne v. Flores*: applied; competitive injury from a rival’s false statements about its own product is not, without appropriation or destruction of protected property, an independent Fourteenth Amendment deprivation.

*Parden v. Terminal Railway*: overruled; ordinary participation in federally regulated commerce does not constitute constructive waiver of sovereign immunity.

*Lujan v. Defenders of Wildlife*: applied; prospective relief requires concrete injury, ongoing or imminent recurrence, causation, and redressability.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Third Circuit is affirmed as to damages and other retrospective monetary relief, reversed as to the prospective Lanham Act claim, and the case is remanded for adjudication of standing, recurrence, the statutory elements, causation, and equitable entitlement.

Effect: College Savings may not recover damages, restitution, or another retrospective monetary award from Florida Prepaid on this claim. It may seek prospective relief against Florida Prepaid only upon proof of an ongoing or imminently threatened violation and every other requirement governing the requested remedy.

---

### Alden v. Maine

Argued March 31, 1999 | Decided June 23, 1999  
On writ of certiorari to the Supreme Judicial Court of Maine; judgment below:  
dismissal of the state probation officers’ Fair Labor Standards Act action on sovereign-immunity grounds affirmed, 715 A.2d 172 (Me. 1998).  
Area of Law: Federalism; state-court sovereign immunity; federal wage law ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep527/usrep527706/usrep527706.pdf))

Questions Presented:

1. Whether Congress may require a nonconsenting State to answer in its own courts to a private damages action under the Fair Labor Standards Act enacted pursuant to the Commerce Clause.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 5-4.  
Supporting: Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas. Opposing: Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Kennedy | Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Thomas | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Souter | Stevens, Ginsburg, Breyer | Would reverse | Complete dissent |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: Stone announces no ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Private Article I damages action in state court

Holding and operative rule: Congress may not use Article I to subject a nonconsenting State to a private damages action in its own courts. Characterizing accrued FLSA liability as earned compensation, restitution, or make-whole relief does not alter the direct State defendant or retrospective treasury remedy.

Reasoning: The FLSA validly regulates Maine as an employer, and the federal substantive duty is supreme within its lawful field. The distinct question is whether Article I authorizes the particular private remedial channel Congress selected. Simulated *Seminole Tribe* recognizes a bounded prospective direct-State action under an enumerated commerce power but does not authorize retrospective monetary recovery. That remedial limit does not disappear when the action is filed in the defendant State’s own courts. *Testa v. Katt* and *Howlett v. Rose* prevent a State from discriminating against federal claims when its courts possess authority over the parties, but they do not remove a generally applicable constitutional immunity belonging to the defendant. Enforcement necessity and the compensatory character of unpaid wages cannot create authority for a treasury judgment. The substantive federal obligation remains enforceable through the United States, unequivocal state waiver, valid Reconstruction-amendment legislation, lawful prospective relief against responsible officers, and remedies Maine itself makes available. ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep527/usrep527706/usrep527706.pdf))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

Souter, joined by Stevens, Ginsburg, and Breyer: The Eleventh Amendment does not govern a State’s own courts, and the Supremacy Clause requires those courts to enforce a valid federal cause of action on the same terms as comparable state claims. They would reverse and permit adjudication of the employees’ FLSA action.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Seminole Tribe v. Florida*: extended; Article I’s no-damages boundary applies to a private direct-State treasury action filed in state court.

*Garcia v. San Antonio Metropolitan Transit Authority*: reaffirmed; Congress may impose the substantive FLSA obligation on state employers even though the private damages remedy is unavailable against a nonconsenting State.

*Testa v. Katt* and *Howlett v. Rose*: clarified; nondiscriminatory state-court enforcement of federal law does not eliminate a defendant’s generally applicable constitutional immunity.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Supreme Judicial Court of Maine is affirmed.

Effect: The probation officers’ private damages action against Maine remains dismissed. The judgment does not excuse Maine from the substantive FLSA duty or bar federal enforcement, unequivocal waiver, valid Reconstruction-amendment abrogation, lawful prospective officer relief, or relief Maine itself provides.

### Ortiz v. Fibreboard Corp.

Argued December 8, 1998 | Decided June 23, 1999  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Fifth Circuit; judgment below:  
certification and approval of the mandatory global asbestos settlement class affirmed, 134 F.3d 668 (5th Cir. 1998).  
Area of Law: Civil procedure; Rule 23; mandatory settlement classes ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep527/usrep527815/usrep527815.pdf))

Questions Presented:

1. Whether the asbestos settlement class satisfied Rule 23(b)(1)(B)’s limited-fund requirements and Rule 23(a)’s adequacy protections for persons with present and future claims.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 7-2.  
Supporting: Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg. Opposing: Stevens, Breyer.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Souter | Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence | Scalia | Kennedy | Supports reversal and remand | Rule-bound treatment of mass-tort settlement and the need for legislative or rulemaking action |
| Dissent | Breyer | Stevens | Would affirm | Complete dissent |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: Stone announces no ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Limited fund and adequate representation

Holding and operative rule: A mandatory settlement class under Rule 23(b)(1)(B) requires a fund independently demonstrated to be fixed at its maximum practical value, insufficient to satisfy the aggregate claims, substantially devoted to the claimant class, and distributed equitably. Rule 23(a) also requires conflict-free representation, including separately represented subclasses when present and future claimants or other materially distinct groups have opposed interests.

Reasoning: Mandatory aggregation eliminates individual control and the right to opt out, so the limited-fund category must remain tied to the traditional conditions that justify binding absent claimants. The asserted limit here was derived primarily from the parties’ negotiated valuation of disputed insurance coverage rather than specific findings establishing the maximum assets practically available to satisfy claims. Fibreboard contributed little of its own net worth and retained substantial value outside the settlement. The agreement also placed currently injured claimants, exposure-only claimants, future disease claimants, and persons affected differently by insurance and payment provisions within one representation structure. Their interests diverged concerning immediate payment, inflation, latency, proof, annual limits, and the value of future claims. Post hoc fairness findings could not replace representatives whose incentives corresponded to each materially distinct group. Settlement may remove trial-management problems, but under simulated *Amchem Products v. Windsor* it does not relax Rule 23’s certification and adequacy requirements. ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep527/usrep527815/usrep527815.pdf))

#### Concurrences

Scalia, joined by Kennedy: The scale and difficulty of asbestos litigation cannot enlarge the authority supplied by the existing Federal Rules. A materially different national solution must come through legislation or lawful rulemaking rather than judicial revision of Rule 23(b)(1)(B).

#### Dissents

Breyer, joined by Stevens: The district court’s extensive findings sufficiently established practical fund limits, equitable treatment, and adequate representation in light of the settlement’s transaction-cost savings and the danger that continued individual litigation would exhaust available assets. They would affirm the certification and settlement approval.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Amchem Products, Inc. v. Windsor*: applied and extended; every Rule 23 requirement continues to govern a mandatory settlement class, and settlement cannot cure conflicting incentives among present and future claimants.

Federal Rule of Civil Procedure 23(b)(1)(B): established; a mandatory limited-fund class requires an independently demonstrated maximum fund, aggregate insufficiency, substantial commitment of the fund, and equitable claimant treatment.

Federal Rule of Civil Procedure 23(a)(4): clarified; materially opposed claimant groups require conflict-free representation and, when necessary, separately represented subclasses.

Rules Enabling Act: applied; Rule 23 may not be enlarged to abridge absent claimants’ substantive rights merely because mandatory aggregation would produce administrative advantages.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Fifth Circuit is reversed, the mandatory class certification and settlement approval are vacated, and the case is remanded for proceedings consistent with Rule 23 and due process.

Effect: The global mandatory settlement may not bind the class in its present form. The parties remain free to pursue individual settlements, narrower opt-out classes, separately represented subclasses, bankruptcy, or a genuine limited-fund proceeding satisfying the governing requirements.
